PASS = no open P0 or P1. WARN = an open P1, including one whose risk the owner accepted.
FAIL = an open P0; an accepted P0 still blocks, because acceptance closes a P1 and nothing
else. UNKNOWN = not assessed - its auditor reported nothing, or a finding in it was never
ruled on - and that is a condition, not a pass.

## Release checklist

_not a gate run - the ten-point checklist is answered in `--mode gate`_

## Blocking - confirmed P0

- **P0** `AI-1` No per-account request, token, concurrency or spend cap on any of the eight customer-reachable AI entry points - Every model call in the product funnels through one function, `structured()`, which has no caller identity check, no counter, no quota and no budget. The only rate limiter in the repository is better-auth's, which only covers better-auth's own `/api/auth/*` paths. Any account that completes the free sign-up can call the model as fast as it can open sockets.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:36`
  - fails when: A stranger completes the free sign-up at kettleworth.vercel.app, PATCHes a minimal profile, and then scripts POST /api/coach/chat with the session cookie at whatever concurrency they can open. Nothing in the request path counts their requests, their tokens or their spend: every call reaches claude-opus-5 and is billed. The same cookie also drives /api/photos/[id]/analyse, /api/nutrition/estimate and /api/onboarding/followup. One account, one afternoon, an invoice the owner only learns about from the provider.
  - fix: Add a cap check inside `structured()` itself, before the `messages.parse` call: read a per-user counter (calls and tokens, per minute and per calendar month) from a shared store and return null when it is exceeded, so every existing deterministic fallback runs; the numbers in env, tiered by `entitlements()`. The branch `ai-policy-and-cron-batching` (commit 0bc0aaf, `packages/api/src/ai/policy.ts` with 12 unit tests) already implements exactly this and should be merged rather than rewritten. If the counter is read from `ai_log`, add an index on `(user_id, created_at)` - the only index today is `ai_log_task_idx` on `(task, created_at)` (`packages/db/src/schema/meta.ts:18`), so a per-user count would scan the table.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:36-49 reads nothing about the caller before messages.parse; t.userId is first touched at the ai_log insert on :62. The only limiter in the tree is better-auth's own rateLimit at packages/api/src/auth.ts:24, which it applies to its own /api/auth/* paths keyed on IP+path, and I confirmed in the installed package that with no secondaryStorage it resolves to the module-level in-memory Map (create-context.mjs:175, rate-limiter/index.mjs:6/245). apps/web/lib/api.ts does session plus zod and nothing else, and there is no middleware.ts anywhere in apps/web.
- **P0** `AI-2` /api/onboarding/followup interpolates three uncapped user strings straight into the prompt, so one request can buy 150,000 input tokens - The body schema caps nothing: `stage`, `answer` and `question` are bare `z.string()`. All three are concatenated into the user part of the prompt and sent to Opus with no truncation, so the per-request cost is set by the attacker, not by the application.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/onboarding/followup/route.ts:6`
  - fails when: An account posts a single followup request whose answer field carries ~600,000 characters of prose - deliberately under the model's context window so the call succeeds and is billed rather than rejected. That is roughly 150,000 input tokens bought with one HTTP request, about fourteen times the unit cost of a coach-chat turn, and the attacker, not the application, chooses the number. Repeated in a loop (there is no limiter on the route) the cost per hour is set by their bandwidth.
  - fix: Put `.max()` on all three fields in the route schema (`stage` is one of a known set and should be `z.enum([...])`; `answer` and `question` at most 2,000 characters, matching `TrainingProfile.notes`' existing 2,000 cap), and add a belt-and-braces character cap plus a token estimate on `t.user` inside `structured()` so no future task can reintroduce the hole.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/onboarding/followup/route.ts:6 is z.object({ stage: z.string(), answer: z.string().optional(), question: z.string().optional() }) with no .max() on any field, and :9-10 take the extraction branch whenever both answer and question are present. packages/api/src/ai/tasks.ts:38 interpolates both verbatim into the prompt. The only truncation in the path is the log write (client.ts:62 slices to 50,000 chars); client.ts:47 sends the untruncated string. Not a duplicate of AI-1: a per-account request cap does not bound the size of one request.
- **P0** `AI-4` The weekly cron fans out one Opus call and one email per profile row, sequentially, in a single invocation, selected by a tautological WHERE - The user selection is `where onboarding_step = onboarding_step`, which matches every profile - unonboarded, dormant and the stray `@kettleworth.test` rows alike - and the loop then awaits an AI call plus an email per user inside one serverless invocation with no batching, no cursor and no resume. It is both the largest single cost line in the product and a job that cannot finish at the stated design point.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:12`
  - fails when: At the design point of 1,000,000 profiles, Monday 06:00 fires one invocation that must make a million sequential model calls and a million email sends. The platform kills it after its duration limit, somewhere in the first few hundred users: those users get a letter, everybody else silently gets nothing, every week, and the handler's own {sent, failed} summary is never returned to anyone. The money that is spent goes disproportionately to dormant accounts, because the tautological filter includes every row that ever signed up and never onboarded.
  - fix: Select only onboarded users with recent activity (session, training session or coach message in the last N days) and process them in cursor-chained batches of a couple of hundred, one invocation or queue message per batch, instead of one loop. Commit 0bc0aaf on `ai-policy-and-cron-batching` already does this (batches of 200 chained by cursor via `after()`, `CRON_ACTIVE_DAYS` flag) and fixes the self-comparison.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:10 is where(eq(profile.onboardingStep, profile.onboardingStep)), which renders onboarding_step = onboarding_step; the column is integer notNull default 0 (packages/db/src/schema/profile.ts), so the predicate is true for every row and the select is the whole profile table. :12 then awaits runWeeklyAdaptation and generateWeeklyLetter per user in series inside one invocation, with no cursor, no batch, no resume and no maxDuration export (the only maxDuration in the app is apps/web/app/api/nutrition/estimate/route.ts:4). generateWeeklyLetter makes one AI_MODEL call (coach.ts:66) and one sendEmail (:70) per user.
- **P0** `LEAN-NEW5` Two routes hand the client a service's internal opts bag, so `force` defeats the only idempotency the paid AI tasks have and `email: true` is reachable although no client sets it - generateWeeklyLetter and getOrBuildMealPlan both guard their AI call with a 'row already exists for this week' check, and both take an internal `opts.force` that skips it. Two route handlers pass the client's body straight into that bag - including `email`, which no client in the repository ever sets - and both also accept an arbitrary `weekStartsOn`. The cache that would bound these two AI tasks to one call per user per week is therefore switched off by the caller, which is the opposite of what a passthrough route should expose.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/coach/letters/generate/route.ts:4`
  - fails when: Two route handlers forward the client's body straight into a service's internal opts bag. apps/web/app/api/coach/letters/generate/route.ts accepts `{ weekStartsOn, email, force }` and passes all three to generateWeeklyLetter; apps/web/app/api/nutrition/mealplan/route.ts does the same with `{ weekStartsOn, force }`. `force` is precisely what defeats the only bound these two paid paths have - `if (existing && !opts.force) return existing` (coach.ts:48) and the equivalent at nutrition.ts:42. A free account (sign-up is open) looping POST /api/coach/letters/generate with `{"force":true,"email":true}` therefore buys one top-tier Claude call plus one outbound email per request, overwriting the same row so nothing accumulates to notice; varying weekStartsOn instead mints an unbounded number of coach_letter and meal_plan rows, one AI call each, every date legal as far as z.string().date() is concerned. route() has no rate limiter - I read the whole helper - and ai/client.ts has no per-user budget, so nothing downstream bounds either call. `email` is reachable although no client in the repository ever sets it, which also puts the product's sending domain behind an attacker-controlled send rate.
  - fix: Stop forwarding the service's internal bag. (1) Remove `email` from the schema at letters/generate/route.ts:4 and set it server-side - only the cron needs it, and no client ever sent it; that is a pure narrowing of the public surface. (2) Make `force` cost something the attacker cannot repeat: keep the flag but have the service treat it as 'at most one regeneration per user per week' (the coach_letter row already carries generatedAt, so `if (existing && opts.force && existing.generatedAt > now() - interval '1 week') return existing`), and do the same against meal_plan. (3) Bound weekStartsOn to the weeks the user actually has data for rather than any valid date. The per-account AI budget in packages/api/src/ai/client.ts is dimension 3's fix and is the real ceiling; what is mine is that these two routes removed the bound the services already had.
  - ruling: I read both route files and both guards they defeat. This is the severity rules' 'no bound on cost or volume where a customer controls the request - an AI endpoint with no per-customer cap ... one customer, one afternoon, an unbounded bill', reachable today by any account. The absent per-account AI cap is the known-broken item the brief already records; what is specific and confirmed here is that these two routes removed the one bound the services themselves had, and exposed the email side-effect as a client-settable flag. The sibling route /api/nutrition/targets shows the right surface: it sets `{ force: true }` server-side and accepts no body.
- **P0** `PERF-1` Weekly letter cron runs the whole fan-out serially in one HTTP invocation, so it dies after a few dozen users and the rest never get a letter or a weekly adaptation - The Monday 06:00 cron loads every profile and then awaits runWeeklyAdaptation + generateWeeklyLetter (one Claude call and one email each) one user at a time inside a single serverless request, with no cursor, no batching, no concurrency and no maxDuration. It cannot finish at today's 147 accounts, let alone the 1M design point.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:12`
  - fails when: The Monday 06:00 cron loads every profile row into one array and then, inside that one HTTP invocation, awaits runWeeklyAdaptation and generateWeeklyLetter for each user in turn - one Claude call and one Resend send each - with no cursor, no batching, no concurrency and no maxDuration. The dominant cost per user is a structured() call on a client configured `timeout: 120_000, maxRetries: 2`. Even at today's handful of accounts the loop cannot finish inside a serverless invocation's limit, and the brief's own load model (250,000 async jobs a day, dominated by this fan-out) is several orders of magnitude beyond it. The function is killed partway: the users at the front of the scan get a letter and an adaptation, everyone behind them silently gets neither - not a slow run but no run - and because the query has no ORDER BY the same prefix is served every week while the same tail never is. The response that would have reported {sent, failed} is never produced, so nothing records the truncation, and nothing retries.
  - fix: Turn the loop into a batched cursor: select N users ordered by user_id above a stored cursor (the `sync_job` table is already the pattern, and ARCHITECTURE.md line 337 specifies batches of 200), do the batch with a bounded Promise pool, persist the cursor, and let the cron re-enter until the cursor is exhausted. The branch `ai-policy-and-cron-batching` already carries this work.
  - ruling: Read the whole handler - fourteen lines, with the loop on line 12 - and confirmed there is no maxDuration export, no batching parameter in vercel.json and no ordering on the select. The failure mode is total rather than degraded for most users, on the load the brief states the system must take, which is the P0 bullet. The branch ai-policy-and-cron-batching is said to carry the batched version; on main this is the single worst finding in the group.
- **P0** `SCALE-1` Weekly letter cron fans out to every user sequentially inside one serverless invocation, with no cursor, batch or lease - The only async job in the product runs as one `for` loop over every user inside a single Vercel function invocation, doing one weekly adaptation, one Claude call and one email per user. It is killed by the platform duration limit after a handful of users and records nothing about where it stopped, so the remainder never receive a letter and no retry resumes.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:12`
  - fails when: Monday 06:00 UTC, Vercel Cron issues one GET. At roughly 5-15 seconds per user - one Claude call, two dozen queries and one email - the invocation completes a handful of users and is killed at the function duration limit mid-letter. The HTTP response carrying {sent, failed} is never returned, so nothing is logged and Vercel does not retry a timed-out cron. Next Monday mondayOf() is a new week, so the cheap idempotency check at coach.ts:48 skips nobody, and because the select has no ORDER BY the run walks roughly the same heap order and serves roughly the same first users again. Those few get a letter every week; everyone else never gets one, and runWeeklyAdaptation - the weekly plan change the product is sold on - silently stops happening for them, every week, with no signal anywhere. At the stated 400,000 onboarded users that is approximately total failure of the product's retention mechanic and of its core promise.
  - fix: Make the route a batch worker: order by a stable key, process a bounded page per invocation (e.g. 50 users), persist the cursor and the per-user outcome in a job row (the `sync_job` shape already exists), return, and let the schedule fire repeatedly until the cursor drains; take an advisory lock so two invocations cannot overlap.
  - ruling: cron/weekly/route.ts:10-13 is exactly as claimed: one unordered select of every profile, then `for (const u of users) { await runWeeklyAdaptation(...); await generateWeeklyLetter(u.userId, undefined, { email: true }); }` in a single invocation. Each iteration is weekStats (seven queries), runWeeklyAdaptation (around fifteen more), a blocking structured() call on AI_MODEL (coach.ts:66) through a client configured with timeout 120_000 and maxRetries 2 (ai/client.ts:14), and a Resend send. No maxDuration export, no LIMIT, no ORDER BY, no cursor, no lease, no retry and no dead letter; the sync_job table exists but nothing consumes it. The failure mode is total rather than degraded, well below the load the brief states, which is what puts it at P0.
- **P0** `SEC-2` Progress-photo upload has no per-account quota and no rate limit, so one free account can write unbounded 8 MB blobs into the primary Postgres - POST /api/photos accepts up to three images of 8 MB each per request from any account that has signed up, stores the bytes as `bytea` in the primary Neon database on Vercel, and enforces no cap on photos per user, bytes per user, or requests per minute. Customer-controlled volume with no bound.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/photos/route.ts:12`
  - fails when: A stranger signs up for free and loops POST /api/photos with a 4 MB JPEG, three files per request, at ten concurrent connections. Nothing refuses the second upload or the millionth. Within a day the account has written tens to hundreds of gigabytes of bytea into the same Postgres instance that serves every read path in the product - storage billed by the gigabyte, autovacuum and WAL pressure on the primary, and a table whose scans degrade the app for every real user. Customer-controlled volume with no bound of any kind.
  - fix: Add a per-account quota in savePhoto (count and total bytes, e.g. reject beyond 60 photos or 200 MB per user) and a per-account upload rate limit at the route; check it in the service, not only at the handler, so analyse/apply paths inherit it.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:17-25 checks exactly two things - the declared content type (:18) and 8 MiB per file (:19) - and then inserts. There is no count query, no SUM(bytes), no per-account ceiling and no limiter anywhere in the path (apps/web/app/api/photos/route.ts:12-26 uses getSession directly, and Math.min(files.length, 3) caps one request, not an account). inDb() at :13 is true in production: PHOTO_STORAGE is set in the Vercel production environment and VERCEL is set with no STORAGE_DIR, so :24 writes the bytes into progress_photo.data in the primary Neon database.

## Conditions - confirmed P1

Each needs an owner and a date, or a written risk acceptance from the owner. A condition with
neither is a blocker.

- **P1** `AI-5` Uncapped profile array fields are re-sent to the model on every later AI call, so one PATCH permanently multiplies the cost of every prompt - `medicalFlags`, `dislikedFoods`, `priorityMuscles` and the `injuries` array have no length bound on the array and no `.max()` on their strings. They are stored on the profile and then serialised into the coach-chat context and the intake and summary prompts on every subsequent call, so a single `PATCH /api/profile` sets the input size of every future AI request for that account.
  - where: `packages/types/src/profile.ts:144`
  - fails when: One PATCH /api/profile with medicalFlags: ["<500,000 characters>"] succeeds. From then on every coach-chat turn, every intake follow-up and every onboarding completion for that account carries ~125,000 extra input tokens, permanently, because those prompts serialise the stored profile. The per-call cost of that account rises by more than an order of magnitude and no counter in the code can see it.
  - fix: Cap the arrays and their elements in the Zod types (for example `z.array(z.string().max(200)).max(20)` for `medicalFlags` and `dislikedFoods`, `.max(20)` on `injuries`, `priorityMuscles` as an enum of the known muscle taxonomy) and cap the intake `transcript` array and its `text`. Separately, bound the assembled prompt inside `structured()` as in AI-2 so no field can reintroduce this.
  - ruling: packages/types/src/profile.ts: priorityMuscles (:126), injuries (:143), medicalFlags (:144) and dislikedFoods (:149) are arrays with no length bound and no .max() on their member strings, next to capped neighbours (vibe max 200 at :122, notes max 2000 at :155). The write path accepts them (apps/web/app/api/profile/route.ts -> upsertProfile at packages/api/src/services/profile.ts:26-32, into jsonb with no length constraint) and the read path re-sends them: coach.ts:95 puts injuries, medicalFlags and priorityMuscles into the chat context and :103 embeds the whole JSON in the prompt, while tasks.ts:20 and :44 stringify the entire profile object. needs_debate is set because the fix scope is disputed: lovedExerciseIds (:119), hatedExerciseIds (:120), knownLifts (:152) and rituals.timezone (:134) are unbounded in the same object and reach the same two prompts, so capping only the four named fields leaves the attack intact.
- **P1** `AI-6` The client controls `force` and `email` on letter generation, so one account can regenerate the paid letter and mail itself without limit - The route forwards client-supplied `force` and `email` flags into `generateWeeklyLetter`, which defeats the only idempotency guard on the most expensive single-user AI task and turns an authenticated endpoint into an unmetered outbound email sender.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/coach/letters/generate/route.ts:4`
  - fails when: A free account loops POST /api/coach/letters/generate with {"force":true,"email":true}. Each iteration is one ~1,500-token Opus completion plus one outbound email; nothing refuses the second or the ten-thousandth. Two harms: an unmetered model bill, and a flood of sends from the product's single sending identity - the same sendEmail that delivers magic links (auth.ts:28-29), so burning that sender's reputation breaks sign-in for everyone. The account's genuine Sunday letter is also overwritten each time by a partial-week regeneration.
  - fix: Drop `force` and `email` from the client contract: the route generates the current week's letter if it does not exist and otherwise returns it, and emailing stays the cron's business. If a manual regenerate is wanted, cap it (one per week per user) in the service and leave `email` server-decided.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/coach/letters/generate/route.ts:4 forwards body.email and body.force into generateWeeklyLetter. packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:48 (if (existing && !opts.force) return existing) is the only idempotency guard on the most expensive single-user task, and :70 sends mail through packages/api/src/email.ts with no counter. weekStartsOn is z.string().date(), so any calendar date is also a fresh letter row and a fresh model call.
- **P1** `AI-7` Every AI task, including trivial extraction, runs on the top model tier; there is no per-task model and no way to configure one - `AI_MODEL` is one global constant used for all ten tasks, so a 400-token intake extraction and a 1,500-token coaching letter both run on `claude-opus-5`. This is the multiplier on every other cost finding and is the stated reason ARCHITECTURE.md's cost gate failed.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:6`
  - fails when: Every AI task, including a 400-token intake extraction, runs on the top model tier. At the design point this is the multiplier that makes ARCHITECTURE.md's cost gate fail - the four LLM lines dominate the bill against a GBP 3 revenue line - and it multiplies every abuse path in AI-1, AI-2 and AI-6 by the same factor, because the attacker's requests also run on the most expensive model available.
  - fix: Add a task-to-tier map read in `structured()` (routine extraction and notes on the cheapest tier, chat and photo reads on the middle tier, keep `AI_MODEL` for anything genuinely premium), with the mapping in config. `packages/api/src/ai/policy.ts` on `ai-policy-and-cron-batching` (commit 0bc0aaf) already implements this behind an `AI_TIERING` flag with tests.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:6 (AI_MODEL = process.env.AI_MODEL ?? "claude-opus-5") and :44 (model: AI_MODEL) are the only model selection in the tree, and StructuredTask (:18-30) carries effort and maxTokens but no model, so a task cannot ask for a cheaper tier even when it declares itself cheap (tasks.ts:19, :38, :70 are all effort "low" with 400-600 max tokens). AI_MODEL is set in the Vercel production environment.
- **P1** `AI-NEW1` A ninth AI entry point is a server-rendered GET page, not a route handler, so it sits outside route() and races itself into duplicate billed calls - The auditor enumerated eight entry points, all POST route handlers. A ninth exists: the /app/nutrition React Server Component calls getOrBuildMealPlan during page render, which makes a meal_plan_note model call. It is a GET, it does not pass through apps/web/lib/api.ts route(), and its only idempotency guard is a read-then-write check against a table with no unique index, so concurrent GETs each buy a model call and each insert a duplicate row.
  - where: `apps/web/app/app/nutrition/page.tsx:22`
  - fails when: On the first visit of a new week an account opens many parallel GETs to /app/nutrition with its session cookie. All of them pass the :39 select before any insert commits, so each one runs mealPlanNote on the top model tier and each one inserts another meal_plan row holding a full WeeklyMealPlan jsonb. The structural half matters as much as the money: route() is the natural place to hang the per-account cap AI-1 needs, and this entry point is not behind it.
  - fix: Put the cap inside structured() (as AI-1's fix already proposes) rather than in route() or a middleware, so RSC render paths inherit it. Separately, make the per-week guard real: add a unique index on meal_plan(user_id, week_starts_on) and change nutrition.ts:52 to onConflictDoUpdate on that target, and move the mealPlanNote call out of the page-render path - render from the existing row and let the client ask for a plan to be built.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/app/nutrition/page.tsx:14 sets dynamic = "force-dynamic" and :22 calls getOrBuildMealPlan on every render. packages/api/src/services/nutrition.ts:39 reads the week's row, :42 returns early only when it exists, and :50 makes a meal_plan_note model call before :52 inserts. meal_plan carries index("meal_plan_user_week_idx") - a plain index, not unique (packages/db/src/schema/nutrition.ts:39) - so concurrent renders neither serialise nor conflict. This is a ninth AI entry point that is a GET page render, so it does not pass through apps/web/lib/api.ts route().
- **P1** `AI-NEW2` /api/nutrition/mealplan takes `force` from the client and `weekStartsOn` as any calendar date, so the per-week guard on the paid meal-plan call is worth nothing - AI-6 found this defect at the letter route and its fix touches only that file. The same defect is at the meal-plan route, where it is worse: the client supplies both `force` and a free-choice `weekStartsOn`, so the idempotency check can be defeated two ways, and because meal_plan has no unique key each distinct date also writes an unbounded number of new rows rather than updating one.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/nutrition/mealplan/route.ts:4`
  - fails when: A free account loops POST /api/nutrition/mealplan with {"force":true\}, or walks weekStartsOn forward one day at a time. Every request rebuilds the plan over the whole recipe table, makes one top-tier model call and inserts another kilobytes-sized jsonb row. A 30-per-minute per-account rate limit would still leave tens of thousands of billed calls and rows a day, so this survives the obvious fix for AI-1.
  - fix: Drop `force` from the client contract here as AI-6 proposes for the letter, and clamp `weekStartsOn` server-side to the Monday of the current or next week (nutrition.ts already has mondayOf at :10) rather than accepting any date. Add the unique index and onConflictDoUpdate from AI-NEW1 so a repeat is an update, not a new row.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/nutrition/mealplan/route.ts:4 passes both body.weekStartsOn (z.string().date(), any calendar date) and body.force into getOrBuildMealPlan, whose only guard is the read-then-return at nutrition.ts:42. Because meal_plan's index is not unique, each distinct date also writes a new row rather than updating one. Separate from AI-6: different file, different handler, and AI-6's fix does not touch it.
- **P1** `AI-NEW4` No absolute ceiling and no runtime kill switch: the only way to stop AI spend is to delete an env var and redeploy - The dimension requires an absolute ceiling as well as per-customer caps - a global daily or monthly spend cap plus a provider-side budget alert - so that a bug in the per-customer logic cannot cost more than a known number. The auditor's AI-1 asks only for per-user counters. Nothing in this repository caps the total bill at any number, nothing alerts on it, and the only off switch requires a Vercel environment change followed by a redeploy.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:10`
  - fails when: Abuse of any AI route starts overnight. Nothing in the application notices - no counter, no metric, no alert - so the first signal is the provider invoice or a provider-side organisation limit, and the amount is whatever the attacker chose. The only way to stop it is to open the hosting dashboard, delete ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and redeploy, which also silently turns off the AI features for every legitimate user because every task simply returns null.
  - fix: Three things, all small. (1) An `AI_ENABLED` env flag checked in aiAvailable() so the feature can be switched off without a code deploy, plus a global daily token or spend ceiling read in structured() with a stated number in config. (2) A provider-side monthly budget cap and alert on the Anthropic organisation, with the number written into ARCHITECTURE.md, because every application-level control has a bug in it. (3) An index on ai_log(created_at) or (user_id, created_at) so 'spend today' and 'spend by user today' are cheap queries, which the per-user cap in AI-1 needs anyway.
  - ruling: There is no global counter, no spend ceiling and no runtime switch anywhere in the tree: packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:10-12 gates the whole AI layer on the presence of ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and nothing else, packages/api/src/ai/policy.ts does not exist on this branch, and ai_log's only index is on (task, created_at), so even the after-the-fact totals are unindexed by user. Distinct from AI-1: a per-account cap and an absolute ceiling are different controls and neither substitutes for the other.
- **P1** `CODE-1` Offline replay deletes a queued set when the server rejects it and counts it as sent, and every server fault is a 400 - flush() treats HTTP 400 and 409 as success: it deletes the queued job and increments `sent`. Because apps/web/lib/api.ts:27 converts every thrown error - including server faults - into a 400, any queued set that the server cannot accept is destroyed silently while the UI has already told the lifter it was saved.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/offline-queue.ts:22`
  - fails when: A lifter logs sets with no signal, so postResilient stores each one in IndexedDB and the UI says it is saved on the device. On reconnect flush() POSTs each job; any job the server answers with 400 or 409 is deleted from the queue and counted in `sent`. Because apps/web/lib/api.ts:27 is the only non-409 error exit in the whole route helper, a Postgres failure, a transient Neon drop and a genuine validation error all arrive as 400 - so a queued set that was never written is destroyed, the pending badge drops to zero, and nothing tells the user. The 409 case is worse than a coin flip: 409 is produced by exactly one service (logSet's needs_confirmation at session.ts:144), on exactly the endpoint that goes into the queue, and the confirmation it is asking for can never be shown because the dialog is gone.
  - fix: Delete the job only on `r.ok`; on 409 keep it and surface it as a set that needs confirmation; on 400 move it to a dead-letter key the UI can show. Separately, make api.ts return 500 for errors that are not validation failures so the queue can tell a bad payload from a broken server.
  - ruling: Read offline-queue.ts:22 - `if (r.ok || r.status === 400 || r.status === 409) { await del(k); sent++; }` - against postResilient at lines 35-37 in the same file, which treats 409 as needs-confirmation and 400 as a throw. Two readings of one contract 14 lines apart, and the destructive one runs on the replay path. There is also no attempt cap and no dead letter, so a 404/500 job is retried on every online event for ever.
- **P1** `CODE-15` logSet rewrites the whole loggedSets array from a stale read, so a concurrent replay and live log lose a set - Logging a set is a read-modify-write of a JSON array with no optimistic lock, no row lock and no conditional update. Two requests for the same exercise instance that overlap both read the pre-existing array and the later writer's array wins, dropping the other set.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:146`
  - fails when: logSet reads the instance at session.ts:139, rebuilds the whole loggedSets array in JavaScript at line 146, and writes it back at line 149 with `where(eq(exerciseInstance.id, instanceId))` - no row lock, no version check, no condition on the value that was read. Two overlapping requests for the same instance both read the pre-existing array and the later writer wins. That overlap is routine, not exotic: the window `online` handler and the service worker's kw-flush both start a flush (and the leaked listener in PERF-12 starts several at once) while the lifter is still tapping sets straight through to the server. One set vanishes and both requests report success, after which progression, personal records and the weekly adaptation are computed from a history that is missing work the lifter did.
  - fix: Merge in SQL with a single statement (`jsonb_set`/`||` on the column, keyed by setNumber) or read and write inside a transaction with `select ... for update` on the instance row.
  - ruling: Read the read-modify-write and confirmed the update is unconditional. The file comment at offline-queue.ts:4 ("Replays are idempotent (setNumber upsert server-side)") is true for a repeat of one set number and says nothing about two different set numbers in flight, which is the case that loses data.
- **P1** `CODE-2` The weekly cron selects every profile row with a tautology, so accounts that never finished intake get an AI letter and an email - The query that is meant to pick out onboarded users compares a column with itself, which is true for every row. Every profile - mid-intake accounts, abandoned sign-ups and the stray @kettleworth.test accounts the brief says exist on production - gets an AI letter generated and emailed every Monday.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:10`
  - fails when: `where(eq(profile.onboardingStep, profile.onboardingStep))` compiles to `onboarding_step = onboarding_step`, and packages/db/src/schema/profile.ts:16 declares that column `.notNull().default(0)`, so the predicate cannot exclude a single row. The cron therefore fans out to every profile ever created: a stranger who signs up, answers two intake questions and leaves gets a top-tier AI letter generated and emailed to them every Monday, as do the stray @kettleworth.test accounts the brief says exist on production. At the 1M design point the handler's first statement is a sequential scan returning 1M user ids into one function's memory, and the predicate is the difference between fanning out to onboarded users and fanning out to everybody.
  - fix: Replace the predicate with `isNotNull(profile.onboardingCompletedAt)` (and skip accounts whose email domain is @kettleworth.test).
  - ruling: The comment two lines above the query says "every onboarded user's letter"; the predicate the rest of the codebase uses for that invariant is `onboardingCompletedAt` (apps/web/app/app/page.tsx:28 and seven sibling pages). I confirmed the column is NOT NULL in the schema, so this is not even a null filter.
- **P1** `CODE-4` Life mode deletes future planned sessions outside a transaction, cascading away logged sets and leaving the plan empty if it fails part-way - restoreUpcoming() deletes every future planned session of the active programme and then re-inserts them one at a time with no transaction. `exercise_instance` cascades on session delete, so any sets already logged into a session that is still `planned` are destroyed, and a failure between the delete and the re-insert leaves the lifter with no upcoming sessions at all.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/life-mode.ts:67`
  - fails when: restoreUpcoming (life-mode.ts:59-78) deletes every future session of the active programme one statement at a time and then re-inserts them one at a time, with no transaction anywhere in the file. packages/db/src/schema/training.ts:73 declares `sessionId ... references(() => trainingSession.id, { onDelete: "cascade" })`, so deleting a session destroys its exercise_instance rows and the loggedSets JSON inside them. logSet never changes session status (session.ts:137-168), so a session that was logged offline - the start() POST failed, the sets replayed straight to /api/instance/[id]/log - is still `planned` and is inside the delete set: the lifter's real work is cascaded away. Independently, if the invocation is killed or Neon drops the connection between the delete loop at line 67 and the insert loop at line 75, the user opens Today to a programme with no upcoming sessions and no way back but regenerating. setLifeMode calls restoreUpcoming on every mode change (line 21), so both paths are one Settings tap away.
  - fix: Wrap restoreUpcoming and each setLifeMode branch in `db().transaction(...)`, and refuse to delete any session whose instances hold a completed logged set (reschedule or leave it instead).
  - ruling: I verified the cascade in the schema and that logSet leaves status alone, and that the only transaction in the service layer is programme.ts:37 - this file has none despite doing a delete-then-rebuild of the user's whole future plan.
- **P1** `CODE-5` Calendar dates are computed in UTC on the server and on the device, so the nutrition day and weigh-ins land on the wrong date outside the UTC band - The app has a time-zone-aware helper (`localTodayIso()`, backed by the kw-tz cookie) and uses it for Today, Programme, Session and the quest routes, but the nutrition day, the weigh-in form and the progress-photo form all compute the date with `new Date().toISOString().slice(0,10)`, which is the UTC date. For any user not on UTC the food log is read and written under the wrong calendar date.
  - where: `apps/web/app/app/nutrition/page.tsx:21`
  - fails when: apps/web/app/app/nutrition/page.tsx:21 computes the day as `new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10)` - the UTC date - while the same repository has `localTodayIso()` behind the kw-tz cookie and uses it on the Today page (page.tsx:31). A user in Los Angeles opening Nutrition at 16:05 local (00:05 UTC) is served tomorrow's empty log, so "calories remaining today" resets to the full target after a day of eating, and the dinner they then log is written to food_log.logged_on = tomorrow, where it will never be shown either. The same UTC-date mistake is made client-side in measurement-form.tsx and body-check.tsx (measuredOn, takenOn) and server-side in activitiesForDay, which buckets the day with `start.setHours(0,0,0,0)` in the process time zone - UTC on Vercel.
  - fix: Pass `await localTodayIso()` into the nutrition page and into the components that build a date, and give the client a single helper that formats the local calendar date (`Intl.DateTimeFormat('en-CA')`) instead of `toISOString()`; use the kw-tz cookie in activitiesForDay and getTodaySession.
  - ruling: I read both the page and apps/web/lib/local-date.ts and confirmed the correct helper exists and is used elsewhere; I also confirmed integrations.ts activitiesForDay uses process-local midnight. The consequence is user data written under a calendar key the user can never read back, for everyone outside the UTC band, which is most of the stated audience.
- **P1** `CODE-NEW1` The weekly cron's bearer check is fail-open: with CRON_SECRET unset, an unauthenticated GET rewrites every user's planned sets and triggers the whole letter and email fan-out - The only guard on /api/cron/weekly is `if (secret && req.headers.get("authorization") !== ...)`. When CRON_SECRET is absent or empty the condition short-circuits and the handler runs for anyone. There is no middleware in the app to catch it, the route does not use the authenticated `route()` helper, and the body of the handler performs cross-tenant writes (runWeeklyAdaptation) and sends email to every account.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:9`
  - fails when: The only guard on /api/cron/weekly is `if (secret && req.headers.get("authorization") !== ...)` (route.ts:9), so the guard exists only while CRON_SECRET exists. With it absent or empty the handler is open: an unauthenticated GET runs runWeeklyAdaptation for every profile row - which updates exercise_instance.plannedSets and, on a swap, clears loggedSets (adaptation.ts:69-70) - and then generateWeeklyLetter with email for every account. There is no middleware.ts in apps/web and this is one of only two handlers that neither use the authenticated route() helper nor verify a signature; the other, the Mux webhook, does check an HMAC.
  - fix: Make the guard fail closed: `if (!secret || req.headers.get("authorization") !== `Bearer ${secret}`) return 401`, and fail the build or boot when CRON_SECRET is missing in production rather than only when it is present. Checking Vercel's own `x-vercel-cron` header is not a substitute, since it is client-settable.
  - ruling: I verified the conditional guard, that .env.example:53 ships CRON_SECRET blank, that no middleware exists, and that runWeeklyAdaptation writes to other users' rows. I could not verify whether CRON_SECRET is actually set in the Vercel project, which is the only thing between this and a P0: proving it unset makes this unauthenticated cross-tenant writes plus an email fan-out and should be re-rated P0 immediately. Rated P1 as a control that disappears under a production configuration nobody asserts. Repeat calls are cheaper than they look - runWeeklyAdaptation early-returns once a week is adapted (adaptation.ts:43) and generateWeeklyLetter returns the existing row unless forced - so the harm of the first unauthenticated call of a week is the full fan-out, and of later calls the re-entry.
- **P1** `CODE-NEW2` listChallenges loads every challenge entrant with no limit and feeds them to inArray, so the community page stops working once a default challenge has more entrants than Postgres will bind - For each open challenge, listChallenges selects every challengeEntry row, then passes the full id list into two `inArray` queries before slicing the board to 20 in application code. There is no LIMIT on the entrant fetch and no pagination, and the function also issues three queries per challenge. Past roughly 65,000 entrants the generated statement exceeds Postgres's bind-parameter ceiling and the query fails outright, taking the whole community page with it.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:205`
  - fails when: listChallenges (community.ts:199-212) loops over every unexpired challenge and, per challenge, selects every challenge_entry row with no LIMIT, then passes the whole id list into two `inArray` queries, and only then sorts in memory and slices to 20. Joining is one tap and ungated (joinChallenge is a bare insert with onConflictDoNothing, and unlike findMatches and listGroups this function has no discoveryEnabled() gate), and the default challenges are global. At the design point a modest join rate on "Show up this week" means tens of thousands of entrant ids transferred and aggregated on every /app/community render to draw 20 rows; past the driver's 65,534 bind-parameter ceiling the statement is rejected outright and the page 500s for every member, entrant or not, because apps/web/app/app/community/page.tsx:16 awaits it inside a Promise.all.
  - fix: Compute the board in SQL: join challenge_entry to the completed-session count, ORDER BY score DESC LIMIT 20, plus a separate single-row lookup for the caller's own rank; keep `participants` as a `count(*)`. Batch the per-challenge queries across all challenges with one grouped query instead of looping.
  - ruling: Read the loop and confirmed there is no cap on the entrant fetch, no gate on the function, and that growthBoard 30 lines later caps its own member fetch at 200 - so the omission is local, not a convention. The parameter ceiling was measured against the project's own driver in the challenge; the unbounded fetch and the per-challenge triple query are visible in the code without it.
- **P1** `LEAN-NEW1` One hand-rolled epoch-week bucket, copied into two services, where core's isoWeek() already exists: newborn life mode drops nothing in its first week and the plateau detector counts 2 real weeks as 4 - The expression `d.slice(0, 7) + <epoch week index>` is written twice as the 'which week is this' key - packages/api/src/services/life-mode.ts:48 and packages/api/src/services/adaptation.ts:23 - while packages/core/src/analytics.ts:52 already exports `isoWeek(dateIso)` and packages/api/src/services/progress.ts:3 already imports it. Both copies are wrong in the same two ways: `Math.floor(t / (7 * 86400000))` buckets from Thursday 1 January 1970, so a Monday-to-Sunday training week always straddles two buckets, and the `YYYY-MM` prefix splits a bucket again at every month end.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/life-mode.ts:48`
  - fails when: The same hand-rolled 'which week is this' key is written twice - life-mode.ts:48 and adaptation.ts:23 - as `date.slice(0,7)` plus `Math.floor(epochMs / (7 * 86400000))`, while core already exports isoWeek and progress.ts already imports it. Epoch weeks begin on a Thursday and the YYYY-MM prefix splits the bucket again at every month end, so one calendar week yields two or three keys. I computed the keys rather than reasoning about them: Mon 7 Dec 2026 and Wed 9 Dec both key to 2026-12:2970 while Fri 11 Dec keys to 2026-12:2971, and Mon 30 Nov, Tue 1 Dec and Wed 2 Dec give 2026-11:2969, 2026-12:2969 and 2026-12:2969. Newborn mode keeps two sessions per bucket, so a Mon/Wed/Fri lifter keeps all three and the mode drops nothing while the session notes and the UI tell a new parent 'two a week is the whole plan'. The same arithmetic inflates weeksTracked in the plateau detector, which core's adaptation rule compares against plateauWeeks = 4, so accessories are rotated out early and the letter tells the user the lift 'has been flat for 4 weeks' after two.
  - fix: Import `isoWeek` from @kettleworth/core (already exported from packages/core/src/index.ts and already used by progress.ts) and use it as the key in both places: `const k = isoWeek(s.scheduledOn)` at life-mode.ts:48 and `new Set(pts.map((p) => isoWeek(p.date))).size` at adaptation.ts:23. Two one-line changes, no new module. Pin it first with a core test asserting that Mon-to-Sun dates share one key and that a Wednesday and the following Friday do not split - the two assertions both copies fail today. This is a behaviour change (newborn mode will start dropping sessions, accessories will stop rotating early), so it is its own commit, never folded into a date-module refactor.
  - ruling: I re-derived both key sets in node against the live expression rather than taking the numbers on trust, and confirmed weeksTracked feeds detectStaleness via adaptation.ts:57. Rated P1: this is the engine's core behaviour - what the product sells - wrong for every user on every Monday run, and packages/api carries no tests, so nothing pins it. Fixing it changes behaviour (newborn mode will start dropping sessions, accessories will stop rotating early), so it belongs in its own commit, not folded into a date-module refactor.
- **P1** `LEAN-NEW4` The Sunday letter's week is an implicit default argument, and the Monday 06:00 cron takes it: every user is emailed a letter about the week that has just started, with zero data in it - `generateWeeklyLetter(userId, weekStartsOn = mondayOf(new Date()), ...)` defaults to the Monday of the current week. The cron that sends the letter runs at 06:00 on Monday and passes `undefined`, so the letter it generates and emails always covers the week that began six hours earlier, not the week that ended. Which week the letter is about is nobody's decision: the service's default argument makes it, the caller cannot see it, and the comment on the cron ('Sunday evening job') and the schedule in vercel.json disagree about the day.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:46`
  - fails when: generateWeeklyLetter's week is an implicit default argument: `weekStartsOn = mondayOf(new Date())` (coach.ts:46). The cron passes undefined (route.ts:12) and vercel.json schedules it `0 6 * * 1` - Monday 06:00 UTC - so mondayOf(now) is today and the letter always covers the week that began six hours ago. weekStats then measures [thisMonday, thisMonday+7): planned = this week's sessions, completed = 0, tonnage = 0, prs = [], while prevTonnage correctly reads the week that ended. Every user is emailed 'A quiet week. Next one starts Monday.' and 'nothing was logged this week, and total volume was down 100% on last week' - and with a key present, one top-tier model call is spent writing that falsehood in better prose. Because coach_letter is unique on (userId, weekStartsOn) and the regenerate button sends only `{ force: true }`, pressing regenerate rebuilds the same empty week, so the user can never get the letter about the week that actually ended.
  - fix: Make the week the caller's decision rather than a default: pass it explicitly from the cron, `await generateWeeklyLetter(u.userId, lastMonday, { email: true })` where lastMonday is the shared `addDays(mondayOf(new Date()), -7)` from the date module LEAN-2 asks for, and drop the default argument from generateWeeklyLetter so no future caller can inherit the wrong week silently. Fix the comment on the cron at route.ts:6 while it is open ('Sunday evening job' describes a cron that runs Monday). Pin it with a test on weekStats asserting that a letter for week W counts only sessions scheduled inside W, and that the cron's chosen week is the one that has ended.
  - ruling: I checked mondayOf's arithmetic at Monday 06:00 UTC (it returns today), the cron expression in apps/web/vercel.json (day-of-week 1 = Monday), and the window in weekStats. The retrospective call exists two files away and is correct - runWeeklyAdaptation uses weekStats(userId, prev.startsOn). Rated P1: the product's headline feature is wrong for every user on every run, the week is nobody's explicit decision, and no test pins it.
- **P1** `OPS-1` CI has never succeeded once in the repository's history: every run aborts at pnpm setup, and main has no protection, so nothing has ever been gated - The workflow declares pnpm twice - `pnpm/action-setup@v4` with `version: 12` and `packageManager: "pnpm@12.4.1"` in the root package.json - which pnpm/action-setup refuses. All 40 workflow runs in the repository's history, from 2026-09-14 to 2026-11-24, failed at that step, before install, typecheck, test, build or e2e. `main` has no branch protection and no rulesets, so nothing blocks a merge or a deploy either.
  - where: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:21`
  - fails when: A commit breaks the 137 core tests in packages/core. The workflow dies 22 seconds in at the pnpm step, exactly as it has for all 40 runs since 14 Sep, so the red X is indistinguishable from the standing one and carries no information. No required status check exists, nothing blocks the merge, and because production deploys on push (OPS-NEW4) the broken commit is live in about three minutes. No typecheck, test or e2e has ever executed against any commit in this repository.
  - fix: Drop `with: { version: 12 }` from the pnpm/action-setup step so it takes the version from `packageManager`, then make the `test` job a required status check on main.
  - ruling: I reproduced it: ci.yml:20-21 pins pnpm 12 in pnpm/action-setup@v4 while package.json:4 already declares packageManager pnpm@12.4.1, and the action refuses both. `gh run list --limit 200` returns 40 runs, all failure; the log of run 45406937251 ends at `##[error]Error: Multiple versions of pnpm specified`. `gh api .../branches/main/protection` is 404 Branch not protected and `.../rulesets` is [].
- **P1** `OPS-2` No configuration schema and no boot-time validation: a missing key surfaces as an HTTP 400 to a user mid-onboarding, and two signature checks silently become no-ops - Nothing validates configuration at startup. There is no `instrumentation.ts`, no `middleware.ts` and no env schema anywhere in the repo; the production build completes with every variable unset. Required keys are read lazily at the point of use and throw into a request, and two security guards are written as `if (secret) { ...verify... }`, so they disable themselves when their key is absent rather than refusing to start.
  - where: `packages/db/src/crypto.ts:6`
  - fails when: An operator sets HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY under a mistyped name on the Vercel project. Nothing fails: the build has no boot-time check, the landing page and /library render, sign-up works. The first user to finish an onboarding stage hits POST /api/profile -> upsertProfile -> encryptJson -> key() throws, and apps/web/lib/api.ts:27 returns HTTP 400 with the body {"error":"HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY is not set"} - the config key's name disclosed to the browser, onboarding dead for every new account, and no alert anywhere. The same absence of validation means a deploy with CRON_SECRET or MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET missing silently turns those two routes into unauthenticated endpoints instead of refusing to start.
  - fix: One Zod schema for the required keys in `packages/config`, imported by a new `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` that throws at boot listing every missing name; change the two `if (secret)` guards to `if (!secret) throw` and add `MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET` to DEPLOY.md step 4.
  - ruling: Verified: no instrumentation.ts and no middleware.ts exist anywhere (find over apps and packages returns nothing), every required key is read lazily at point of use (crypto.ts:5-6, db/src/index.ts:12), auth.ts:16 passes BETTER_AUTH_SECRET with no check at all, and two security checks are written to disable themselves - cron/weekly/route.ts:9 `if (secret && ...)` and webhooks/mux/route.ts:10 `if (secret) { ...verify... }`. MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET appears in .env.example:25 but not in docs/DEPLOY.md's variable list.
- **P1** `OPS-3` No error tracker, no metrics and no alerts: the only error channel is console.error on an ephemeral filesystem, and the env vars that advertise observability are read by nothing - There is no error tracker, no metrics collection, no alert and no release version or request id on any log line. `SENTRY_DSN` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY` are documented in .env.example but no SDK is imported anywhere, so an operator who sets them believes errors are being captured when nothing is. None of the four numbers (request rate, error rate, latency, saturation) is collected, and nothing watches the one resource that saturates first - Postgres storage, which holds 8 MB progress photos as `bytea` plus full AI prompts.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/api.ts:26`
  - fails when: Postgres storage on the Neon plan fills - which the 8 MB bytea photos and the full-prompt ai_log rows reach first. Inserts start failing, so every set log, weigh-in and food entry throws, api.ts:27 turns each into an HTTP 400, and the offline queue then deletes the queued write as permanently rejected (OPS-4). The only record is console.error lines in a Vercel log stream nobody is tailing, with no error rate, no latency metric, no saturation metric and no alert attached. The owner learns about it from a user email, or not at all. An operator who set SENTRY_DSN believes errors are captured; nothing imports the SDK.
  - fix: Import the Sentry Next.js SDK behind the `SENTRY_DSN` that is already documented, attach `VERCEL_GIT_COMMIT_SHA` as the release and a generated request id in `route()`, and add one alert on 5xx rate plus one on Neon storage percentage.
  - ruling: A grep for sentry|posthog|opentelemetry|datadog|pino|winston over apps and packages returns nothing outside .next build artefacts, against .env.example:48-50 which advertises NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY and SENTRY_DSN. api.ts:26's console.error is the whole error path, with no request id and no release. photos.ts:24 writes up to 8 MB per photo into Postgres and ai/client.ts:62 writes up to 70 KB per AI call, and nothing reads or alerts on database size.
- **P1** `OPS-4` Every server-side exception is returned as HTTP 400, so the platform's error rate reads zero and the offline queue deletes the user's set as if it had been accepted - The shared route wrapper catches every exception - database errors, Anthropic failures, missing config - and returns HTTP 400 with the raw `e.message`. Two consequences: the only error-rate signal a serverless host gives for free (5xx) stays flat during a genuine outage, and the IndexedDB replay queue treats a 400 as a permanent client-side rejection and deletes the queued write, counting it as sent.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/api.ts:27`
  - fails when: A lifter logs a set with no signal; postResilient enqueues it in IndexedDB. Outside the gym the connection returns and flush() POSTs to /api/instance/<id>/log at a moment when Neon is mid-failover or the pool is exhausted. The handler throws, api.ts:27 answers 400, offline-queue.ts:22 calls del(k) and increments `sent`. The set was never written to the database, is gone from the device, and the UI reports it as flushed. The same request appears in Vercel's dashboard as a 4xx, i.e. the user's fault, so no error-rate alarm could ever fire on a genuine outage.
  - fix: In `route()`, return 500 for anything that is not a recognised domain error (keep 400 for Zod and 409 for `needs_confirmation`), and in `flush()` only delete the job on `r.ok`, 409, or a 4xx that is not 408/429.
  - ruling: api.ts:22-27 has exactly one branch before the catch-all (code === "needs_confirmation" -> 409) and then returns status 400 with the raw e.message for every other throw - database error, Anthropic failure, missing config alike. offline-queue.ts:22 is `if (r.ok || r.status === 400 || r.status === 409) { await del(k); sent++; }`, so the client treats that 400 as a permanent rejection and deletes the job.
- **P1** `OPS-5` AI token counts are written to ai_log and never read by anything, and the table has no index to support a per-account cost query - Every AI call records input and output tokens, model, user and latency, which is the right data. Nothing ever reads it: there is no admin page, no query, no aggregate, no alert. The only index is on `(task, created_at)`, so the question an operator would actually ask - how many tokens has this account burned today - is a sequential scan over the largest-growing table in the schema. Dimension 3 owns the missing cap; this is the missing signal, and the two together mean an abusive account is invisible until the invoice.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:62`
  - fails when: A free account loops POST /api/coach/chat with 1000-character messages overnight. Every call writes a complete ai_log row, so the evidence of the spend sits in Postgres - and nothing reads it: no dashboard, no budget threshold, no alert. The operator's natural query, `select sum(input_tokens) from ai_log where user_id = $1 and created_at > now() - interval '1 day'`, has no index to use and degrades as the table grows. With no cap on main either, the first signal of an abusive account is the Anthropic invoice.
  - fix: Add an index on `ai_log(user_id, created_at)` as a new additive migration, and a single daily query in the runbook (tokens by user, tokens by task) wired to one alert threshold.
  - ruling: ai/client.ts:62 records model, user, both token counts and latency per call. A grep for aiLog|ai_log across apps and packages returns only the schema, the migration and that one insert - zero readers, no admin page, no aggregate, no threshold. meta.ts:18 declares only index("ai_log_task_idx").on(task, createdAt); user_id carries no index (confirmed in 0000_init.sql:562, the only ai_log index).
- **P1** `OPS-6` No rollback procedure anywhere in the repository, and the migration-compatibility question is unanswered in writing - The release runbook is one line - commit, then `vercel --prod` - and has no counterpart for going back. Vercel's instant rollback is never named, no rollback has been performed or recorded, and the runbook does not state whether the previous version can run against the current schema, which matters because migrations are applied by hand from a laptop in a separate step before the deploy.
  - where: `docs/DEPLOY.md:21`
  - fails when: A Monday release ships a regression in the session player that loses a logged set. There is no written rollback, so under pressure the operator improvises: redeploy an older commit, whose schema expectations nobody has recorded, or hunt for a Vercel rollback control they have never used, while users are mid-session. Because migrations are applied by hand from a laptop in a separate step, the question that decides whether rolling back is even safe - does the previous build run against the current schema - is answered for the first time during the incident.
  - fix: Add a six-line "Rollback" section to DEPLOY.md: the Vercel instant-rollback command (`vercel rollback`), the statement that migrations 0000-0008 are additive so the previous build runs against the current schema, and a date on which a rollback was actually performed once.
  - ruling: docs/DEPLOY.md:20-21 is the entire release section (`git commit` then `vercel --prod`, plus "New migrations: run step 5's migrate command first"). A grep for backup|restore|rollback|retention over docs/ and README.md finds no rollback, incident or restore section; the only hits are ARCHITECTURE.md's exit-plan table. drizzle-kit generates no down migrations and nothing states whether build N-1 can run against schema N.
- **P1** `OPS-7` No backup the project owns and no restore anyone has performed, for health data and progress photographs that cannot be reconstructed - The repository defines no backup at all - no scheduled `pg_dump`, no retention statement, no restore procedure and no record of a restore ever being proven. Everything the product holds that a user could not re-enter (progress photographs as `bytea`, body measurements, encrypted wearable time series, free-text injury and medical notes) lives in exactly one Neon database, and the only export path written down is the lift-and-shift paragraph in ARCHITECTURE.md, which is a migration plan rather than a tested restore.
  - where: `docs/DEPLOY.md:16`
  - fails when: A bad adaptation run corrupts profile.sensitive on Friday, or a Neon branch is deleted, or the operator runs db:push instead of db:migrate with the production URL exported (OPS-13). The recovery attempt is the first one ever made, under incident conditions, with nothing written down about what Neon's history window is and no dump the project controls - on data no user can re-enter: progress photographs, body measurements and medical notes.
  - fix: A scheduled `pg_dump` to object storage outside Neon with a stated retention, plus one documented restore into the local docker-compose Postgres with the date it was last proven written into DEPLOY.md.
  - ruling: DEPLOY.md mentions the database only to create it (line 7) and to migrate and seed it (16-17). There is no backup, retention or restore section in DEPLOY.md, README.md or anywhere under docs/; the only pg_dump references are ARCHITECTURE.md's lift-and-shift table, whose weekend test is recorded as "not started". photos.ts:13 and :24 put the photo bytes inside the same single Neon database, so photographs, measurements, encrypted wearable series and injury free-text share one failure domain with no dump the project owns and no restore anyone has performed.
- **P1** `OPS-NEW1` GDPR hard delete fails outright for any account that created a community group or filed a report: two foreign keys are ON DELETE NO ACTION - `deleteAccount` is one `delete from user` and its comment asserts "everything cascades". Two foreign keys to `user` are not cascades: `group.created_by` and `report.reporter_id` are both ON DELETE NO ACTION. Both rows are created by ordinary authenticated users through ungated routes, so any user who has ever created a group or reported a post can never delete their account. The delete throws a Postgres constraint violation which apps/web/lib/api.ts:27 returns to the browser as HTTP 400 with the raw message.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/account.ts:34`
  - fails when: A member reports an abusive post (one POST /api/community/report) in week one. In week three they ask to be deleted. POST /api/account/delete revokes their wearable providers, irreversibly deletes their progress photographs, inserts an `account.deleted` audit row - and then line 34 raises `update or delete on table "user" violates foreign key constraint "report_reporter_id_user_id_fk"`. api.ts:27 returns that Postgres message to the browser as a 400. The account, the encrypted profile, the measurements and the wearable samples all survive; the photographs are already gone; the audit log now asserts a deletion that did not happen. Every member who has created a group or filed a report is in the same position, and the same throw breaks scripts/purge-test-accounts.ts, which calls deleteAccount.
  - fix: In `deleteAccount`, before the user delete, reassign or null the two referencing columns in the same transaction - delete the user's `report` rows and either transfer `group.created_by` to another admin from `group_member` or null it (the column would need to become nullable, which is an additive migration). Wrap the whole function in one transaction so a failure cannot leave photos deleted and the user row intact, and add a test that deletes an account which owns a group and has filed a report.
  - ruling: I confirmed both foreign keys myself: 0000_init.sql:520 `group_created_by_user_id_fk ... ON DELETE no action` and :530 `report_reporter_id_user_id_fk ... ON DELETE no action`, matching community.ts:27 and :88 where `.references(() => user.id)` is written without a cascade option; every other FK to user in the schema is explicitly onDelete cascade. account.ts:34 is a bare `delete(user)` and its comment at :28 claims everything cascades. Both writers are reachable by any signed-in account and neither is behind discoveryEnabled(), which gates only community.ts:88 and :147.
- **P1** `OPS-NEW2` ai_log and audit_log sit outside the delete cascade and outside the GDPR export, so plaintext injuries, medical flags, height and weight survive a hard delete forever - `ai_log.user_id` and `audit_log.user_id` are bare `text` columns with no foreign key to `user`, so they are not part of the cascade that `deleteAccount` relies on. `ai_log` stores the prompt verbatim - up to 20,000 characters of system text and 50,000 characters of user text - and for the intake, coach and letter tasks that text is the user's profile JSON including `injuries`, `medicalFlags`, `heightCm`, `weightKg` and `age`, unencrypted, in a table with no retention policy. The same rows are also absent from the GDPR export. So the one copy of a user's health free-text that is neither encrypted, nor exportable, nor deletable is the one in ai_log.
  - where: `packages/db/src/schema/meta.ts:6`
  - fails when: A member types "left ACL reconstruction, on beta blockers" into the intake and later asks the coach about it. Each call writes that text into ai_log.input as plaintext jsonb. They then exercise both GDPR rights: the export at /api/account/export returns a document that contains none of it, so they are told it is not held; the delete removes the profile row and its AES-GCM blob but leaves every ai_log row, because there is no foreign key to cascade through and no retention job. The plaintext medical note stays in the production database indefinitely, keyed to an id that no longer resolves to a person. I rate this P1 on the same footing as OPS-NEW1: the data is the user's own and lawfully collected, so the defect is failed erasure and storage limitation rather than unlawful disclosure.
  - fix: Delete the user's ai_log and audit_log rows explicitly inside `deleteAccount`'s transaction (keeping an anonymised audit row, which account.ts:33 already writes with `userId: null`), add the retention job ARCHITECTURE.md:336 already specifies for ai_log, and stop writing the raw prompt for the tasks that carry health free-text - the token counts, task, model and validity are what OPS-5 needs; the 50,000-character prompt is not.
  - ruling: meta.ts:6 and :22 declare userId as bare `text("user_id")` with no .references(), unlike every other user-scoped table; ai_log's only index is on (task, created_at). ai/client.ts:62 stores `system` sliced to 20,000 chars and `input.user` sliced to 50,000 chars verbatim, and for coach_chat that string is `Context:\n${JSON.stringify(ctx)}` where ctx.profile is built at coach.ts:95 with `injuries` and `medicalFlags` - the same fields crypto.ts exists to encrypt inside profile.sensitive. exportAccount (account.ts:8-25) selects from ten tables and never reads ai_log or audit_log. No deletion of either table exists anywhere.
- **P1** `OPS-NEW3` Superseded production deployments stay publicly live against the production database, and their URLs are published through the public GitHub deployments API - A deploy does not retire the build it replaces. The previous production deployment is still serving on its own `*.vercel.app` URL with a working connection to the production Neon database, Vercel Deployment Protection is off, and every one of those URLs is readable without authentication from the public repository's GitHub deployments API. There is no deployment-retirement or protection step anywhere in docs/DEPLOY.md, so shipping a fix does not remove the thing it fixed.
  - where: `apps/web/vercel.json:1`
  - fails when: The owner upgrades Next to close the unauthenticated image-optimization RCE (OPS-NEW5) and pushes. kettleworth.vercel.app is fixed; the previous build is still live on its own URL, still running the vulnerable version, still holding DATABASE_URL, HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY, BETTER_AUTH_SECRET and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY in its environment, and its URL is one unauthenticated GitHub API call away from anyone who finds the public repository. The patch is cosmetic. The same mechanism undoes a rollback in the other direction and keeps every since-removed endpoint online.
  - fix: Turn on Vercel Deployment Protection (standard protection, or at minimum protection for all deployments other than the production alias) so only the canonical domain is public, and add a line to DEPLOY.md's release section saying so. Separately, narrow auth.ts:18's `https://*.vercel.app` to the project's own hosts - that wildcard trusts every Vercel subdomain on the internet, which is a dimension 2 question and is noted for that auditor.
  - ruling: I verified this live. `gh api repos/bbweareark/kettleworth/deployments` returns 51 deployments unauthenticated. The current production deployment (8029777410) resolves to kettleworth-r2fxrd2hg-basil-s-projects11.vercel.app and the one it replaced (8014625590, ref 29c7d61b) to kettleworth-gutefb3if-basil-s-projects11.vercel.app; I fetched GET /api/library?limit=1 on both and both answered 200 with live exercise rows, so the superseded build is still running and still connected to the production database. auth.ts:18 lists "https://*.vercel.app" in trustedOrigins, so sessions work there. Nothing in vercel.json or DEPLOY.md sets deployment protection or retires a build.
- **P1** `OPS-NEW4` The runbook documents a deploy path that is not the one in use: production ships automatically on push, so its "run the migration first" instruction cannot be honoured - DEPLOY.md's release step is `git commit` then `vercel --prod`, with repository-connected deploys offered as a parenthetical alternative. The repository is connected: all 51 GitHub deployments are created by `vercel[bot]` and 40 of them are Production, triggered by the push. That inverts the runbook's ordering instruction - "New migrations: run step 5's migrate command first" - because the push that is supposed to come after the migration is itself what starts the production build. Nobody operating from this document can get the order right, and nothing in the repository records which migration is deployed where.
  - where: `docs/DEPLOY.md:21`
  - fails when: The next release adds migration 0009 and code that reads the new column. The operator follows the runbook literally: commit, push. The push is what starts the production build, which is serving in about three minutes while they are still opening a terminal to run the migrate command against Neon. For that window production code queries a column that does not exist, every affected route throws, api.ts:27 turns each into a 400, and the offline queue deletes the queued sets it was carrying as permanently rejected. Nothing in the repository records which migration a given deployment expects, so the window's length is whatever the manual step's delay happens to be.
  - fix: Rewrite DEPLOY.md's release section around the path actually in use: push to main triggers the production build, so the migration runs before the push, not after it, and the runbook should say that and say which database. Better, move the migrate into the pipeline as a job that runs on the merge commit before Vercel's build is promoted, so the ordering is enforced rather than remembered. Either way state the expand/contract rule (migrations are additive today - OPS-6) so a deploy and its migration are independently safe.
  - ruling: All 51 GitHub deployments were created by vercel[bot], 40 of them Production, so the repository is connected and production ships on push - while DEPLOY.md:21 offers that only as a parenthetical and instructs "New migrations: run step 5's migrate command first". For the current head the commit is 13:46:29Z and the Production deployment 13:49:25Z, about three minutes later. ci.yml has no deploy job and no migrate step, so nothing orders the two.
- **P1** `OPS-NEW5` The pipeline has no dependency audit and nothing watches advisories: `pnpm audit --prod` fails today with four criticals, including an unauthenticated RCE in the installed Next.js that is reachable on the live site - No step in the workflow runs a dependency audit, no renovate or dependabot configuration exists, and no document says who checks advisories or how often. The consequence is not hypothetical: the installed dependency set has 4 critical and 11 high advisories, among them three Next.js criticals that the installed 16.3.5 is inside the vulnerable range for, including an unauthenticated remote code execution in Image Optimization - and the image optimizer answers unauthenticated on the production domain.
  - where: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:24`
  - fails when: Nobody runs an audit because nothing does, and no document names an owner for advisories. next 16.3.5 stays in the lockfile. A stranger reaches the unauthenticated image-optimization endpoint on the live domain and exercises the RCE against the function that holds DATABASE_URL, HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY, BETTER_AUTH_SECRET and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. With no error tracker (OPS-3) neither the attempt nor the aftermath is observable, and with superseded builds still online (OPS-NEW3) the upgrade does not remove the exposure. P1 is the rating the severity page gives this class explicitly: "Known-vulnerable dependency with a reachable path".
  - fix: Upgrade next to >=16.4.1 and better-auth to >=1.7.7, re-run the suite, and deploy. Then add `pnpm audit --prod --audit-level high` as a CI step (after OPS-1's pnpm fix, or it will never run) and a dependabot or renovate configuration so advisories arrive as pull requests rather than as nothing.
  - ruling: I ran `pnpm audit --prod` myself: {low 4, moderate 21, high 11, critical 4} over 192 production dependencies. The criticals include next "Unauthenticated Remote Code Execution in Image Optimization" (vulnerable >=16.0.0 <=16.4.0), next "Unauthenticated Remote Code Execution in `use cache`" (same range), next "Remote Code Execution in next/og ImageResponse" (>=16.2.0 <16.3.6) and better-auth "OAuth state can be used as a Magic Link to sign in as another user" (>=1.4.0-beta.18 <1.7.7). Installed: next 16.3.5 and better-auth 1.7.4, inside every one of those ranges. The workflow has no audit step, .github contains only workflows/ci.yml, and there is no renovate.json or dependabot.yml. next.config.ts:10 configures images.remotePatterns, so the optimizer route is in use.
- **P1** `PERF-2` audit_log has no index at all and is sequentially scanned on every Today page render, while the same table gains one row per logged set - Four tables that grow with usage are queried on hot paths by columns that carry no index: audit_log (no index whatsoever, scanned on every Today render), match (no index on user_a/user_b, scanned twice per Community render), comment (no index on post_id, scanned per group feed), and training_session, whose (user_id, scheduled_on) index cannot filter `status`, so every one of a user's session rows is read from the heap and discarded.
  - where: `packages/db/src/schema/meta.ts:20`
  - fails when: Four tables that grow with usage are queried on hot paths by columns with no index. audit_log is declared with no index argument at all (packages/db/src/schema/meta.ts:20-27) and is read by (userId, action, target) on every Today render - diversifyProgramme's idempotency probe at programme.ts:117, reached from apps/web/app/app/page.tsx:33 - while the same table gains a row per logged set from adjustRemainingLoads (session.ts:429), which the player fires after every set. match has no index on user_a/user_b and is scanned twice per Community render (community.ts:111 and :126); comment has no index on post_id and is scanned per group feed (community.ts:171); and training_session's only user index is (user_id, scheduled_on), so the many queries filtering `status = 'completed'` read every one of that user's session rows from the heap and discard them. The first symptom at the design point is not one slow request but the database saturating on audit_log heap I/O while Today is the screen every user lands on.
  - fix: Add `index("audit_log_user_action_idx").on(t.userId, t.action, t.target)` to audit_log, `index("match_user_a_idx").on(t.userA)` and `index("match_user_b_idx").on(t.userB)` to match, `index("comment_post_idx").on(t.postId, t.createdAt)` to comment, and extend training_session's index to `(user_id, status, scheduled_on)`; four CREATE INDEX statements in a new migration, no schema change to any column.
  - ruling: I read all four table declarations in packages/db/src/schema and the four query sites. Three tables genuinely have no secondary index and training_session's cannot filter status. 'A missing index on a query that grows with the table' is the P1 bullet, and the fix is four CREATE INDEX statements with no column change.
- **P1** `PERF-3` The Today page issues 39 database queries per render, most of them serial, and re-runs two write-capable "one-time" passes on every single load - /app is `dynamic = "force-dynamic"` and its server component awaits eight stages in sequence before it can render, costing a measured 39 database round trips in steady state. Two of those stages - the weekly adaptation and the programme "diversify" catch-up pass - are maintenance jobs with their own cron and their own idempotency marker, re-executed inside the user's page render every time.
  - where: `apps/web/app/app/page.tsx:29`
  - fails when: /app is force-dynamic and its server component is a serial chain before it can render: getProfile, then `await runWeeklyAdaptation(user.id)` (page.tsx:29), then currentWeekState, then `await diversifyProgramme(user.id, todayIso)` (page.tsx:33), then questBoard, then an eight-way Promise.all, then getSessionDetail, then two more.
