Round-trip latency sets the floor, so the page waits on roughly two dozen serial statements before anything is rendered. Two of those stages are maintenance jobs that have their own cron and their own idempotency marker and are nonetheless executed inside the user's page render every single time; worse, on each user's first render after a Monday their own request performs the full weekly adaptation, including updates to every planned exercise_instance and an audit_log insert - so the Monday-morning gym peak is also the moment every adaptation runs inline in a user-facing request, racing the cron doing the same work.
  - fix: Move runWeeklyAdaptation and diversifyProgramme off the render path (they already have cron and audit-log markers respectively; a cheap marker read, or next/server `after()`, is enough), and collapse the remaining serial stages into the existing Promise.all - getProfile, currentWeekState and questBoard have no data dependency on each other.
  - ruling: I read page.tsx:26-40 and confirmed the serial awaits and that getProfile, currentWeekState and questBoard have no data dependency on each other. I did not reproduce the measured 39 queries, but the shape and the two inline write-capable maintenance passes are in the source and are sufficient.
- **P1** `PERF-4` liftSessions reads a user's entire logged history for the requested exercises with no LIMIT and no date bound, on every session and Today render - The benchmark query pulls every exercise_instance row the user has ever had for the requested exercises, with both jsonb columns, and then throws most of it away in JavaScript. It is called from getSessionDetail (Today page and every session page) for all six to eight of the day's exercises, and from liftsOverview for every exercise the user has ever logged.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:363`
  - fails when: liftSessions (session.ts:360-366) selects every exercise_instance row the user has ever had for the requested exercises - including both the plannedSets and loggedSets JSONB columns - with no LIMIT and no date predicate, then throws most of it away in JavaScript (exerciseHistory keeps 40, liftsOverview keeps 12). It is on the hottest read path: getSessionDetail calls benchmarksFor at session.ts:63 for all of the day's exercises, and getSessionDetail is called by the Today page and by every session page; liftsOverview calls it for every exercise the user has ever logged. A user who trains four times a week for two years has thousands of instance rows, so opening Today transfers megabytes of JSONB from Postgres to the function and sorts it to produce six small benchmark objects - and the cost grows linearly for the life of the account, so the users the product most wants to keep are the ones for whom it gets slowest.
  - fix: Add a date floor and a row cap to liftSessions (the callers never need more than the last ~40 sessions per exercise: `gte(trainingSession.scheduledOn, <18 months ago>)` plus `.limit(exerciseIds.length * 60)`), and stop selecting `plannedSets` for the overview path, which only uses it to identify warm-up set numbers.
  - ruling: Read the query and confirmed no limit, no date bound, and both jsonb columns in the projection; confirmed the call from getSessionDetail at line 63. A hot-path read whose cost grows with the table is the P1 performance-cliff bullet.
- **P1** `PERF-5` Logging one set costs seven to eight serial database round trips, one of which reads the user's entire history for that exercise - logSet is the app's hottest write path - every set in every gym, plus the offline replay - and it issues seven to eight statements one after another, including historyFor, which selects every exercise_instance row the user has ever had for that exercise with no LIMIT.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:172`
  - fails when: logSet is the app's hottest write - every set in every gym, plus the offline replay - and issues seven to eight statements one after another: the instance+session join (session.ts:139), the best estimatedMax (:142), the instance update (:149), historyFor (:153), getProfile inside the PR branch (:160), an estimatedMax insert on an e1RM PR (:164), and a select plus a delete/update/insert on personal_record inside syncSessionRecord (:303-314). historyFor itself selects every exercise_instance row the user has ever had for that exercise with no limit and no date bound and flattens every logged set, and that list is handed to detectPR twice. The player then fires a second request per set to /api/instance/[id]/adjust, which adds three more statements plus an audit_log insert. A 24-set session is hundreds of statements; a reconnect that replays 30 queued sets serially is thirty unbounded history reads back to back from one phone, and every phone on that gym's wi-fi reconnects at the same moment.
  - fix: Bound historyFor the way the engine actually needs it (the best prior set per exercise, which is a single aggregate or the existing estimated_max row) and compute it once per logSet instead of passing the full list to both detectPR calls; fold the plausibility read at line 142 and the history read into one statement.
  - ruling: I read the whole function, historyFor and syncSessionRecord and confirmed the statement sequence and the unbounded history read. Rated P1 rather than the P0 the challenge argued for: the cost is bounded by one user's own history for one exercise and degrades rather than falling over, so it is the P1 hot-path bullet, not a proven total failure.
- **P1** `PERF-6` Progress serves full-resolution 8 MB body photos out of Postgres to paint 96x128 thumbnails, with no resizing, no pagination and no thumbnail column - Body photos are stored as up to 8 MB of bytea in Postgres and served whole through a route handler, and the UI renders them as plain <img> at thumbnail size. listPhotos has no LIMIT, so the page asks for every photo the account has ever uploaded at full resolution. Separately, three service functions select the bytea column when they only need metadata.
  - where: `apps/web/components/progress/body-check.tsx:69`
  - fails when: Body photos are up to 8 MB of bytea in Postgres (MAX_BYTES at photos.ts:14, no downscaling anywhere in the repo) and are served whole: readPhoto does an unprojected `db().select().from(progressPhoto)`, so the full data column crosses the wire, and apps/web/components/progress/body-check.tsx:69 renders each one as a raw `<img src={`/api/photos/${p.id}`} className="h-32 w-24 object-cover">` - a 96x128 box. listPhotos has no LIMIT, so the page asks for every photo the account has ever uploaded, at full resolution, one request each. A year of monthly three-pose check-ins is 36 full-size originals read out of Postgres through 36 invocations to draw 36 thumbnails, and it only grows. The write side repeats the waste: deletePhoto and applyAnalysis also use the unprojected select, so DELETE transfers the whole 8 MB row purely to test whether row.data is null.
  - fix: Store a downscaled thumbnail at upload time (a second bytea or storage key, ~40 KB at 256 px) and point the thumbnail grid and the before/after slider at it; project the columns in readPhoto/deletePhoto/applyAnalysis so the bytea is fetched only by the image route; add a LIMIT and paging to listPhotos. The <img> markup change is a UI file and is reported here, not fixed - it goes through the sensational-ui pipeline.
  - ruling: I read body-check.tsx:69, the image route, readPhoto, deletePhoto, applyAnalysis and listPhotos, and confirmed there is no thumbnail column, no image pipeline and no limit. The `<img>` markup is a UI file behind the sensational-ui hook, so that half is reported, not fixed here; the projections, the thumbnail column and the listPhotos limit are server-side and are not.
- **P1** `PERF-7` No timeout on any wearable-provider HTTP call, so one slow provider holds a sync request and its database connection until the platform kills it - Every outbound call to the six wearable providers goes through two helpers that pass no `signal` and no timeout, and syncProvider awaits up to four of them in sequence inside the request that triggered it. Node's fetch has no total-request deadline, so a provider that accepts the connection and stalls holds the function, the Postgres connection it checked out and the `sync_job` row marked "running".
  - where: `packages/integrations/src/oauth.ts:31`
  - fails when: Both outbound helpers in packages/integrations/src/oauth.ts pass no signal and no timeout - `fetch(url, { headers: { Authorization: ... } })` at line 31 and the token request at line 14 - and every one of the six provider adapters funnels through them; whoop's fetchSamples makes four sequential getJson calls. syncProvider awaits them inside the request that triggered it, after inserting a sync_job row with status 'running' (integrations.ts:66). Node's fetch has no total-request deadline, so a provider that accepts the connection and stalls holds the invocation, the pool connection it checked out and the running job until the platform kills the function - at which point the catch block at integrations.ts:84-88 never runs, so connected_provider keeps its old status and the sync_job row stays 'running' for ever with nothing to reap it. During an upstream incident every manual retry occupies a function and a connection for the full platform limit instead of failing in eight seconds.
  - fix: Pass `signal: AbortSignal.timeout(8000)` in oauth.ts getJson and tokenRequest (the same call that nutrition.ts:126 already makes), and set an explicit maxDuration on the sync route so the handler's own deadline is above the sum of the per-call timeouts.
  - ruling: I read both helpers and confirmed neither passes an AbortSignal, and that the same codebase already gets this right for Open Food Facts (`signal: AbortSignal.timeout(8000)`, nutrition.ts:126). 'A synchronous call to a third party in a request path with no timeout' is the P1 bullet verbatim.
- **P1** `PERF-8` The Anthropic client's 120 s timeout with two retries is longer than any route's own deadline, so slow AI calls are billed up to three times and never logged - One `structured()` call can take up to 360 s before it gives up, while every AI route except /api/nutrition/estimate runs under the platform's default function duration. The platform therefore kills the function first, which means the user gets a gateway error, the ai_log row that records the spend is never written, and the retries have already paid for up to three generations.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:14`
  - fails when: ai/client.ts:14 constructs the Anthropic client with `timeout: 120_000, maxRetries: 2`, so one structured() call can occupy up to three attempts of two minutes, while of the customer-facing AI routes only /api/nutrition/estimate declares a maxDuration at all. The platform therefore kills the function long before the SDK's own timeout bites: the user gets a gateway error after a long spinner and retries by hand, restarting the whole budget. Because the ai_log insert is written after the try/catch completes (client.ts:61-62), a killed invocation records nothing - so the only per-user spend record the product has systematically under-counts exactly the calls that cost the most, which is the evidence any later spend cap would be sized from.
  - fix: Set the client timeout per task below the route's own deadline (e.g. 20-25 s for chat and intake, with the route declaring maxDuration above it) and write the ai_log row from a finally block or next/server `after()` so the spend is recorded even when the attempt is abandoned.
  - ruling: Read the client construction and the ordering of the log write. Rated P1, as the challenge argued: a third-party call on a request path whose timeout cannot bite before the host's own deadline, with the spend record unreachable on the failure path. Raising it further would overlap the absent per-account cap, which is the known-broken item.
- **P1** `PERF-NEW1` POST /api/life-mode lets any account rewrite its whole future programme, one statement per row, with a user-controlled horizon, no transaction and no rate limit - setLifeMode's window is `until ?? today+14` where `until` is any valid date string the client sends, so a far-future date makes the window the entire remaining programme. Every session and every exercise instance in that window is then updated with one statement each, and switching mode first calls restoreUpcoming, which deletes every future session one statement at a time and re-inserts them one at a time with RETURNING. None of it is wrapped in a transaction and the route has no rate limit, so one free account can issue ~500 serial writes per request in a loop, and a kill mid-way leaves the programme permanently missing sessions.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/life-mode.ts:25`
  - fails when: setLifeMode's window is `until ?? addDays(today, 14)` (life-mode.ts:24) and the route validates `until` only as `z.string().date().nullable().optional()`, so a client can send 2099-01-01 and make the window the entire remaining programme. Every session and every exercise instance in that window is then written with one statement each: travel mode selects the instances per session and updates them one by one (lines 34-44), ill mode updates one session at a time (line 30), busy/newborn update or delete one row at a time (lines 48-53). Switching mode first runs restoreUpcoming, which deletes every future session one statement at a time and re-inserts them one at a time with RETURNING. One POST therefore issues hundreds of serial writes against the shared database, the route has no rate limit (I read the whole route() helper - session check, zod parse, error shaping, nothing else), and alternating two modes in a loop is the cheapest request an attacker can make and the most expensive one the database can serve.
  - fix: Cap the horizon server-side (reject or clamp `until` beyond, say, 28 days, which is what the comment at life-mode.ts:12-16 says the feature is for); wrap setLifeMode and restoreUpcoming in one `db().transaction` so a kill cannot leave the programme half-deleted; replace the per-row loops with set-based statements (one UPDATE ... WHERE id = ANY($1) per group, one multi-row INSERT with client-side uuids as photos.ts:20 already does); add a per-account rate limit to the route.
  - ruling: Read the route schema and every branch of setLifeMode and confirmed the client-controlled horizon, the per-row loops and the absent limiter. The missing transaction in the same function is CODE-4's confirmed defect and is not double-counted here; what is confirmed here is the unbounded horizon and the write amplification, whose fix is to clamp `until` server-side to the 28 days the feature's own comment describes and to replace the loops with set-based statements.
- **P1** `PERF-NEW2` getReadiness reads every health_sample row the account has ever stored, unprojected, on every Today render - no index can serve (user_id, start_at) - recentSamples selects unprojected rows filtered on (user_id, start_at), but health_sample's two indexes are (user_id, metric, start_at) and (user_id, provider, metric, provider_record_id), so only the user_id prefix is usable and the date bound is applied as a heap filter after every one of that account's samples has been read. The projection also carries the encrypted `raw` column, which the caller never uses.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:92`
  - fails when: recentSamples (integrations.ts:92) selects unprojected health_sample rows filtered on (userId, startAt), but the table's two indexes are (userId, metric, startAt) and the (userId, provider, metric, providerRecordId) dedupe index, so only the userId prefix is usable as an index condition and the date bound is applied after the rows have been read. Every readiness computation therefore reads every sample the account has ever stored in order to use the last eight days - a read amplification that grows linearly and for ever - and it is on the hot path twice: getReadiness is one of the eight calls in the Today page's Promise.all, and chatContext calls it on every coach message. The projection also carries `raw`, the AES-GCM ciphertext of the provider's whole record, which the mapping at line 95 never reads: the wasted bytes are the largest column in the row.
  - fix: Add `index('health_sample_user_time_idx').on(t.userId, t.startAt)` (a new migration, no column change) or make recentSamples metric-aware so the existing (user_id, metric, start_at) index applies, and project the nine columns the caller actually maps instead of `select()`.
  - ruling: I read the query, both index declarations in packages/db/src/schema/integrations.ts and the mapping that ignores `raw`. health_sample is the brief's second-largest table at ~365M rows a year, so this is 'a missing index on a query that grows with the table' on the hottest screen: P1, with a one-line index addition and a column projection as the fix.
- **P1** `SCALE-10` The community Growth board recomputes 200 members' lifetime Growth from raw history on every page render, with no aggregation on the largest table and no cache - `growthBoard` takes up to 200 members and calls `growthForUsers`, which runs eight queries across the whole history of all of them - including one over `exercise_instance` (the 500M-row/year table) and one that returns a row per completed session with no aggregation - then does O(members x rows) `filter`/`find` work in JavaScript to produce a 20-row board. Nothing is cached or precomputed, so the cost grows with every member's tenure.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:129`
  - fails when: At the design point a three-year member has hundreds of completed sessions and thousands of exercise_instance rows. One render of /app/community aggregates on the order of a million exercise_instance rows, pulls six figures of session rows into the function, and then does O(members x rows) linear scans in JavaScript - to display ten names. The cost rises every month every member stays, so the page gets slower precisely as retention improves, and more application instances do not help because the work is in the one database. Separately, `.limit(200)` with no ORDER BY means the "Growth board" at scale is an arbitrary 200 rows, so the ranking shown is not a ranking of anything.
  - fix: Precompute Growth per user (a periodically refreshed total, or incrementally on the writes that change it) and have the board read the top N by that stored value with an ORDER BY and LIMIT; stop recomputing lifetime history per render, and aggregate the session count in SQL rather than filtering in JS.
  - ruling: growthBoard (community.ts:240) takes up to 200 members with `.limit(200)` and no ORDER BY and calls growthForUsers, which I read at photos.ts:119-141: eight queries over the whole history of all of them, including an unaggregated select returning one row per completed session (:129) and a jsonb_array_length aggregate over exercise_instance (:131), followed by per-member filter/find work in JavaScript. It is awaited in the force-dynamic community page's Promise.all. Nothing caches or precomputes any of it.
- **P1** `SCALE-2` The cron's user query filters on a tautology and has no bound, loading every profile row into the function's memory - `where(eq(profile.onboardingStep, profile.onboardingStep))` compiles to `onboarding_step = onboarding_step`, which is true for every row, so the query the comment describes as 'every onboarded user' is in fact every profile, unbounded and unpaginated, materialised as one array in the function.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:10`
  - fails when: At the design point the cron's first statement materialises every profile row as one array inside a single serverless function, and the loop then spends an Opus-tier Claude call and a real email on accounts that never finished onboarding - for whom weekStats is empty and the letter says nothing. A stranger who signs up and abandons intake is enrolled in the weekly AI fan-out for life, so the cost of the job scales with signups rather than with members, on a product where nobody pays.
  - fix: Replace the predicate with `isNotNull(profile.onboardingCompletedAt)` and add keyset pagination (`where(gt(profile.userId, cursor)).orderBy(asc(profile.userId)).limit(batch)`), which is also what SCALE-1 needs.
  - ruling: `where(eq(profile.onboardingStep, profile.onboardingStep))` is there verbatim at cron/weekly/route.ts:10 and compiles to `onboarding_step = onboarding_step`, true for every row, with no bound and no pagination - while the comment above it says "every onboarded user". The column the code almost certainly meant, onboardingCompletedAt, is what app/page.tsx:27 checks. This is a distinct defect from SCALE-1 with a distinct fix (correct the predicate and bound the query), so I rule it separately.
- **P1** `SCALE-3` Offline queue flush has no mutual exclusion and deletes a job only after its response, so concurrent flushes replay every queued write twice - `flush()` lists the queue keys, then POSTs each job and only deletes the key after the response. Nothing prevents two flushes running at once, and two independent triggers fire on the same connectivity event, so each queued set is delivered twice, in parallel, to a handler that is not safe under concurrency (SCALE-4, SCALE-5).
  - where: `apps/web/lib/offline-queue.ts:15`
  - fails when: A lifter logs sets 1-3 with no signal; all three sit in IndexedDB. Walking out, the phone reconnects: the online event calls flush(), and within the same second background sync posts kw-flush to the open client, calling flush() again. Both read the same key list - nothing has been deleted yet - and POST the same jobs in parallel; the installed PWA plus a browser tab does the same from two clients over one shared IndexedDB. Each queued set is therefore delivered twice, concurrently, into a handler that is a read-modify-write with no lock, which is precisely the precondition for the lost update in SCALE-4 and the duplicate record row in SCALE-5.
  - fix: Serialise flush: keep a module-level in-flight promise and return it to concurrent callers, and delete (or mark) each key before awaiting its fetch, re-queuing on failure. A per-job idempotency key sent as a header would also let the server deduplicate.
  - ruling: flush() at offline-queue.ts:13-25 lists the keys, then POSTs each job and only deletes the key after the response, with no lock, no claim and no in-flight set. Two triggers fire on the same connectivity event: offline-warmup.tsx:22 registers window 'online' -> on(), and :24 registers a serviceWorker 'message' handler that calls the same on() when the worker posts kw-flush, which sw.ts:32 does from its sync handler for every client. player.tsx adds a third 'online' handler and calls flush() again at session completion.
- **P1** `SCALE-4` logSet read-modify-writes the loggedSets JSON array with no transaction or row lock, so a concurrent write silently drops a logged set - The handler reads the whole `loggedSets` array into JavaScript, merges the new set, and writes the whole array back in a separate statement. There is no transaction, no `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` and no version check, so two concurrent logs for the same exercise instance are a classic lost update: the second write overwrites the first set with an array that never contained it.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:149`
  - fails when: Two logs for the same exercise instance arrive concurrently - the duplicate flush in SCALE-3, two tabs, or a queued replay racing a live log. Request A reads loggedSets = []; request B reads [] before A's UPDATE lands. A writes [set1]; B writes [set2]. The row ends as [set2]: set 1 is gone from the database, a 200 OK went back to the client, and the IndexedDB job was deleted, so the lifter's work is unrecoverable from the device. The same race computes allDone from the stale array, so completedAt is wrong, and it hands an incomplete set list to syncSessionRecord, so the session's personal record is derived from data that is missing a set. I keep this at P1 rather than P0: the loss is per-set and degraded, not total.
  - fix: Wrap the read, merge and write in `db().transaction()` with `select ... for update` on the `exercise_instance` row (or do the merge in SQL with `jsonb_set`/a single `UPDATE ... SET logged_sets = <expression over the current value>`), so the second writer sees the first writer's array.
  - ruling: session.ts reads the row at :139, merges in JavaScript at :146 (`inst.inst.loggedSets.filter(l => l.setNumber !== parsed.setNumber).concat(parsed)`) and writes the whole array back in a separate statement at :149, with no transaction, no SELECT FOR UPDATE and no version column. db() returns a plain drizzle instance; the transaction helper is used in programme.ts:37 and not here.
- **P1** `SCALE-6` runWeeklyAdaptation guards idempotency with a check-then-act on week.adaptedAt and is called on every Today render, so concurrent runs apply the week's adaptations twice - The 'idempotent per week' guard reads `week.adaptedAt` at the start and writes it at the very end, with every plan mutation in between and no lock. The function is invoked on every render of the Today page and by the Monday cron, so two overlapping runs both see a null `adaptedAt` and both rewrite the week's prescribed sets.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/adaptation.ts:43`
  - fails when: The cron is scheduled for 06:00 Monday (vercel.json) and the gym peak is 06:00-08:00, so the cron processes a user in the same minute that user opens /app. Both calls read adaptedAt as null. The page render applies "reduce load 10%" and writes 0.9x; the cron's run reads after that write and applies 10% again, writing 0.81x - the lifter's whole week is prescribed about 19% lighter than the engine decided. With a swap adaptation the exercise is rotated twice and originalExerciseId is overwritten, so restore no longer returns the original movement, and week.adaptations and audit_log each gain a duplicate entry. The cron is not required: refreshing /app while the first render is still issuing its dozens of sequential queries is enough.
  - fix: Claim the week before doing the work with a conditional update - `update week set adapted_at = now() where id = ? and adapted_at is null returning id` - and return early when it claims no row; wrap the instance rewrites in the same transaction.
  - ruling: adaptation.ts reads the guard at :43 (`if (target.adaptedAt && !extra.length) return ...`), mutates planned sets at :69-70, and writes the adaptedAt marker last at :74, with no transaction, advisory lock or conditional update. Callers: app/app/page.tsx:29 on every render of a page declared `export const dynamic = "force-dynamic"` at :23, the Monday cron at cron/weekly/route.ts:12, and weeklyCheckIn at adaptation.ts:88.
- **P1** `SCALE-7` The only rate limiter in the codebase keeps its counters in per-instance memory, so it does not bind across instances or survive a cold start - better-auth is configured with `rateLimit: { enabled: true, window: 60, max: 30 }` but no `storage` and no `secondaryStorage`, so the library falls back to an in-process `Map`. On a horizontally scaled serverless host each instance counts separately and every cold start resets the count, so the configured limit is not the limit anyone actually gets.
  - where: `packages/api/src/auth.ts:24`
  - fails when: Vercel autoscales the app to K concurrent instances. An attacker hammering /api/auth/sign-in gets 30 attempts per minute per instance their requests happen to land on, so the effective ceiling is 30K per minute and rises with the autoscaler; spreading traffic to force cold starts resets counters to zero as well. Nothing else in the application is rate limited at all, so this is the entire volume defence for an unauthenticated sign-up product.
  - fix: Give better-auth shared storage - `rateLimit: { ..., storage: "database" }` (its own rate-limit table) or a `secondaryStorage` adapter over the Redis/Valkey that docker-compose.yml already declares - so one counter is shared by every instance.
  - ruling: auth.ts:24 is `rateLimit: { enabled: true, window: 60, max: 30 }` with no storage key and no secondaryStorage anywhere in build(). I checked the installed library: better-auth 1.7.4's create-context.mjs:175 is `storage: options.rateLimit?.storage || (options.secondaryStorage ? "secondary-storage" : "memory")`, and the memory backend is a module-scope Map. This is the only rate limit in the codebase. The severity page names this case exactly: "Rate limits that exist but are per-instance".
- **P1** `SCALE-8` Every Today page render probes audit_log, a table with no index of any kind that grows by tens of millions of rows a year - `diversifyProgramme` is called on every render of /app and starts with a three-predicate lookup against `audit_log`. `audit_log` has no index other than its uuid primary key, so that lookup is a sequential scan over a table that only grows, on the application's busiest page.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:117`
  - fails when: week.adapted alone adds one row per user per week - about 21M rows a year at the stated 400,000 MAU, before quest, programme, photo and account rows. Every Today render then sequentially scans that table to discover that the one-time diversify pass already ran, which it always has, because generateAndSaveProgramme writes the exact marker at creation (programme.ts:63). At 200,000 DAU opening Today several times a day that is hundreds of thousands of full scans a day against the single Neon primary, and every other query sharing that database degrades with it. Today the table has a few hundred rows and the scan is free, which is exactly why it is invisible.
  - fix: Stop asking audit_log on the hot path: skip `diversifyProgramme` for programmes created after the variety rules landed (the programme row is already loaded on this page, and generateAndSaveProgramme marks every new programme as diversified), leaving the pass for a one-off backfill. The durable fix is an index on audit_log(user_id, action, target), which needs a migration and so is an escalation for this run.
  - ruling: programme.ts:117 opens diversifyProgramme with a three-predicate lookup against audit_log, and I confirmed audit_log has no index of any kind beyond its uuid primary key: meta.ts:20-27 passes no index argument and a grep over packages/db/drizzle/*.sql returns no audit_log index. The caller is app/app/page.tsx:33 inside a force-dynamic page, and the table is written by sixteen call sites including one per user per week at adaptation.ts:75.
- **P1** `SCALE-9` listChallenges puts every challenge participant id into one IN list, which exceeds Postgres's bind-parameter ceiling and fails the whole community page - For each active challenge the code selects every participant id, then passes that unbounded array into `inArray(...)` twice to build a 20-row leaderboard. Each id becomes one bind parameter, and the Postgres wire protocol caps a statement at 65,535 parameters, so a popular global challenge makes the query - and the page that awaits it - fail outright.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:207`
  - fails when: The default "Show up this week" challenge is created for everyone and shown with a Join button. Once more than 65,535 participants have joined, inArray emits more than 65,535 bind parameters, Postgres rejects the Bind message, listChallenges throws, and because the community page awaits it inside Promise.all the entire page fails for every user, as does GET /api/community/challenges. Below that threshold it still pulls every participant id and every matching community_profile row into the function on each page load - two 50,000-row result sets to render 20 lines - and it does so three queries per challenge inside a for loop.
  - fix: Score the board in SQL instead of in the function: join challenge_entry to training_session, aggregate, order by score and `limit 20`, and fetch community profiles only for those 20 ids. Hoist the per-challenge queries out of the loop.
  - ruling: community.ts:207-209 selects every challengeEntry row for the challenge, then passes the unbounded id array into inArray twice before slicing to 20, and one bind parameter is emitted per id. The default challenges are created with groupId: null (:196) and joinChallenge (:215) is reachable by any authenticated account through /api/community/challenges/join, which is not behind discoveryEnabled(). listChallenges is awaited inside the Promise.all of app/app/community/page.tsx, which is force-dynamic.
- **P1** `SCALE-NEW1` Every progress photo is served full size out of Postgres through a serverless function, one request per thumbnail, on a response the CDN cannot cache - The only way a photo reaches the browser is GET /api/photos/<id>, which reads the whole bytea row into the function and returns it with `Cache-Control: private`. There is no thumbnail, no resize, no object storage, no streaming and no shared-cache header, and the Progress page renders one such request per photo the user has ever uploaded, at full resolution, into a 96x128 px box.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/photos/[id]/route.ts:9`
  - fails when: A member with two years of monthly front/side/back photos has about 72 rows. Opening /app/progress issues 72 requests to /api/photos/<id>, each a separate function invocation that pulls its full multi-megabyte row out of Neon and buffers it in memory, to paint a 96x128 px thumbnail - and because the response is marked private it repeats in full on every visit, with no CDN able to help. At the stated 200,000 DAU the photo path alone saturates database egress before any other component, and more application instances do not relieve it because the bytes come from the one database. On the poor gym connection the brief names as the primary condition, the page simply never finishes loading.
  - fix: Store and serve a derived thumbnail (one extra bytea column is a schema change, so for this run generate it at upload time into the existing row's analysis-adjacent storage or add an `?w=` branch that downscales before responding), and make the response cacheable - `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable` is safe because the id is content-addressed and the handler already authorises by userId, or move to a signed-URL object store as ARCHITECTURE.md:336 plans. At minimum stop sending the original to a 96x128 box.
  - ruling: I verified the whole path. photos/[id]/route.ts:7-9 reads the row through readPhoto - `db().select().from(progressPhoto)` returning `data` up to 8 MB - and returns the full bytes with `Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600`, which forbids the shared edge cache. There is no resize or thumbnail code anywhere. The grid renders one `<img src={`/api/photos/${p.id}`} className="h-32 w-24 object-cover" />` per photo at body-check.tsx:69 and two more at physique.tsx:36-37.
- **P1** `SCALE-NEW2` The Today page runs two unbounded lifetime scans of training_session and one of body_measurement on every render, to produce a count, a streak and the last eight rows - getProgress and questBoard each select every completed training_session a user has ever had, with no date bound and no aggregation, and getProgress additionally selects every body_measurement row ever written. All three run on every render of /app, which is force-dynamic. The cost of opening the home screen therefore grows for the rest of each user's life, and the values they feed are a count, a streak and `slice(0, 8)`.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/progress.ts:11`
  - fails when: A member who has trained four times a week for three years has around 600 completed sessions and, following the product's own daily weigh-in ritual, around 1,000 measurement rows. Every Today render reads the sessions twice over and ships the whole measurement array to the browser, to display a count, a streak number and eight recent rows. At the stated 200,000 DAU that is on the order of a billion row reads a day for data three integers would answer, on the page the brief identifies as hot - and the per-render cost rises every week a user stays, so the home screen degrades exactly as retention improves, with no number of application instances helping.
  - fix: Replace the count with `count(*)` in SQL, bound the streak query to the window weeklyStreak actually needs (it cannot look back further than the longest streak it will report), add `.orderBy(desc(...)).limit(8)` for recentSessions, and bound or paginate the measurements select to what the page draws. Do the streak once per render instead of in both getProgress and questBoard.
  - ruling: progress.ts:11 selects every completed training_session for the user with no date bound and no aggregation, and is consumed only as sessions.length, weeklyStreak(...) and slice(0, 8); progress.ts:17 selects every body_measurement row ever written and returns it whole. quests.ts:38 repeats the first query for weeklyStreak alone. Both run in the same render: app/app/page.tsx:34 (questBoard) and :35 (getProgress), under `export const dynamic = "force-dynamic"`. The bounded 120-day query at progress.ts:7 in the same function shows the pattern was known and not applied here.
- **P1** `SCALE-NEW4` sendEmail throws away Resend's error result, so a rate-limited or rejected send is recorded as sent and never retried - The Resend SDK does not throw on an API error: it resolves with `{ data: null, error }`. sendEmail awaits the call and ignores the return value, so every caller treats a 429 or a 5xx as success. The weekly letter then stamps `emailedAt` on a letter that was never delivered and will never be retried, and a failed magic-link send leaves the user unable to sign in with nothing surfaced anywhere.
  - where: `packages/api/src/email.ts:11`
  - fails when: Monday 06:00 the cron calls generateWeeklyLetter with { email: true } once per user as fast as the loop goes. Resend enforces a send rate, so from the second or third user every send comes back { data: null, error: rate_limit_exceeded }. sendEmail returns normally, coach.ts:70 writes emailedAt, and the cron increments `sent`. The headline weekly feature silently stops arriving while the database and the cron response both report success, and because emailedAt is set nothing will ever retry. The same swallow breaks sign-in: during any sign-up burst the magic-link sends are rate-limited, better-auth returns 200, the UI tells the user to check their inbox, no email exists and nothing is logged.
  - fix: Make sendEmail return the `{ data, error }` result and throw (or return a typed failure) when `error` is set, give the fetch a timeout, and make the letter write `emailedAt` only on a confirmed send so the next cron pass retries. Throttle the letter fan-out to the provider's documented rate and record per-user outcomes in the job row SCALE-1's fix introduces.
  - ruling: email.ts:4-12 awaits `resend.emails.send(...)` with a Promise<void> signature and never inspects the result, and the Resend SDK resolves with { data: null, error } on a non-ok response rather than throwing. Two callers depend on the opposite: coach.ts:70 stamps emailedAt immediately after the send, and auth.ts:28-30's sendMagicLink returns normally regardless. There is no timeout, no retry, no queue and no dead letter in the file.
- **P1** `SCALE-NEW7` One free account can exhaust the shared Resend and wearable-provider quotas: the letter endpoint accepts force+email in a loop and the sync endpoint has no lease - Two authenticated endpoints call shared third-party dependencies once per request with no per-account cap, no lease and no rate limit. POST /api/coach/letters/generate takes `{ force: true, email: true }`, which bypasses the idempotency check and sends another email every call. POST /api/integrations/<provider>/sync starts another provider fetch and another sync_job row every call. Both third parties are shared by every tenant, so one account's loop degrades or disables the feature for everyone.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/coach/letters/generate/route.ts:4`
  - fails when: A hostile free account - the brief's primary threat actor - loops POST /api/coach/letters/generate with {"force":true,"email":true}. Each iteration is one top-tier Claude call and one Resend send, with no per-account cap and no rate limit. Within minutes that one account has consumed the shared Resend allowance; because sendEmail ignores the error result (SCALE-NEW4) nothing alerts, and from then on no magic-link sign-in email is delivered to anybody - one free account has disabled authentication for the whole product. The same loop against /api/integrations/oura/sync burns the shared OAuth client's provider quota, so every other member's sync starts failing and their connected_provider.status is written to 'error', while sync_job fills with orphaned running rows. I rate this P1 rather than P0 because the uncapped AI spend itself is dimension 3's P0 and I will not double-count it; what is rated here is the shared third-party quota denial.
  - fix: Put a per-account quota in front of the shared dependencies: a daily cap on forced letter generation (and drop `force` from the public schema entirely - it exists for operators, not callers), and a lease on syncProvider keyed on (userId, provider) so a second concurrent sync returns the running job instead of starting another. Both need a shared counter store, which is the same dependency SCALE-7's fix needs.
  - ruling: Both endpoints are as described. letters/generate/route.ts:4 lets the caller supply force and email, and generateWeeklyLetter's only idempotency guard is coach.ts:48 `if (existing && !opts.force) return existing;`, so force: true always re-runs the structured() call at :66 and always re-sends at :70. integrations/[provider]/sync/route.ts:4 calls syncProvider with no lease and no in-flight check; integrations.ts:66 inserts a fresh running job and :72 calls fetchSamples. route() supplies auth and zod and nothing else, there is no middleware, and the only limiter in the repo covers /api/auth/* (SCALE-7).
- **P1** `SEC-12` The service worker caches body-photo responses and rendered /app pages, and neither sign-out nor account deletion purges them - GET /api/photos/* responses (the progress photographs themselves) are stored in a Cache Storage bucket for 24 hours and /app/* navigations for seven days, while sign-out and account deletion clear neither Cache Storage nor the IndexedDB write queue, so health data outlives the session on the device.
  - where: `apps/web/app/sw.ts:24`
  - fails when: Someone signs in on a shared or borrowed phone, looks at their progress photographs, and signs out. The next person picks up the phone, switches on airplane mode and opens /app/progress: the network attempt fails, so the service worker serves the previous user's rendered page from kw-app-pages and their photograph bytes from the API cache, to a visitor with no session at all. The same cache holds every authenticated GET API response the first user triggered, and a hard-deleted account's data is still on the device after the server rows are gone.
  - fix: On sign-out and after account deletion, iterate `caches.keys()` and delete the kw-* caches, and clear the kw-queue: keys from IndexedDB. Keep the photo rule out of the service worker, or add a CacheableResponsePlugin that refuses `Cache-Control: private`.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/sw.ts:24 caches GET /api/photos/* and /api/activity for 24 hours and :21 caches /app/* navigations for 7 days, and the surface is larger than those two rules because :25 spreads defaultCache. I read that array in the installed @serwist/next 9.5.12 (dist/index.worker.mjs:155-196): it adds NetworkFirst caches for every same-origin GET under /api/ (cacheName "apis", 24h), for every same-origin RSC request and for every same-origin text/html response. Nothing clears Cache Storage: sign-out does not, and neither does account deletion (packages/api/src/services/account.ts:29-35).
- **P1** `SEC-4` GDPR hard delete throws for any user who has filed a community report, and leaves their free-text health data behind in ai_log either way - deleteAccount relies entirely on ON DELETE CASCADE from the `user` row, but report.reporterId has no ON DELETE clause (Postgres default NO ACTION), so deleting a user who ever pressed Report raises a foreign-key violation and the whole delete aborts. ai_log and audit_log carry user_id as a plain column with no foreign key, so even a successful delete leaves identifiable rows containing injuries, medical flags and coach-chat text.
  - where: `packages/db/src/schema/community.ts:88`
  - fails when: A member presses Report on a post, then later uses Settings to delete their account. The DELETE on user violates report_reporter_id_user_id_fk, the exception travels through apps/web/lib/api.ts:23-27 as a 400, and the settings screen tells them it could not be deleted - their account and all their health data are still there, with no partial progress and no path through the UI. The same happens to anyone who has created a group. A data-subject erasure request that the product cannot honour.
  - fix: Delete or anonymise the user's report, ai_log and audit_log rows inside deleteAccount before deleting the user row (the brief forbids changing the migration chain, so do it in the service, not with a new ON DELETE). Null the reporter id rather than dropping the report if the moderation record must survive.
  - ruling: packages/db/src/schema/community.ts:88 is reporterId ... .references(() => user.id) with no onDelete, so Postgres defaults to NO ACTION, and packages/api/src/services/account.ts:34 is a bare db().delete(user) with the comment 'everything cascades'. I grepped every user.id reference in the schema: exactly two omit onDelete. needs_debate is set because the fix scope is disputed and the second one matters - community.ts:27 group.createdBy has the same shape and createGroup is reachable by any authenticated account (apps/web/app/api/community/groups/route.ts:5) - as do the two tables that carry user_id with no foreign key at all (meta.ts:6 ai_log, meta.ts:22 audit_log).
- **P1** `SEC-5` The only rate limiter covers /api/auth/* and is memory-backed, so it is per-lambda-instance; the other 57 handlers have none - `rateLimit: { enabled: true, window: 60, max: 30 }` applies only to better-auth's own endpoints, and because no secondaryStorage or customStorage is configured it resolves to better-auth's in-memory store, which on Vercel means one counter per warm lambda instance. No application route handler has any limit at all.
  - where: `packages/api/src/auth.ts:24`
  - fails when: Two outcomes. (a) The sign-in limiter lives in each warm lambda's memory, so an attacker spreading a breached-password list across connections gets a fresh allowance on every cold start and every scaled-out instance, and the per-IP key means a rotating-proxy run never shares a counter. (b) Every non-auth handler - photo upload, nutrition log, community writes and all eight AI endpoints - has no limit whatsoever, which is the mechanism that makes AI-1 and SEC-2 exploitable at speed.
  - fix: Give better-auth a shared store (`secondaryStorage` on Redis, or `rateLimit.storage: "database"`) so the counter is global, and add one rate-limit helper inside apps/web/lib/api.ts keyed on the session user id so every route() handler inherits a per-account limit at the existing choke point.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/auth.ts:24 sets rateLimit { enabled, window 60, max 30 } and nothing else in the tree limits anything. I verified both halves in the installed better-auth 1.7.4: create-context.mjs:175 resolves storage to "memory" when no secondaryStorage is configured (none is), and rate-limiter/index.mjs:6/245 keys the counter on IP+path in a module-level Map. apps/web/lib/api.ts does session plus zod only, and there is no middleware.ts, so the other route handlers have no limit at all.
- **P1** `SEC-7` Next.js 16.3.5 with images.remotePatterns configured is exposed to the Image Optimization SSRF advisory, and the bundled sharp is below the version that closes the RCE advisory - The deployment runs next 16.3.5 and sharp 0.35.4. GHSA-cjq9-62q9-8jv4 (CVE-2026-94483, SSRF in Image Optimization, fixed in 16.3.8) states that an application is affected when `images.remotePatterns` is configured, which this one is. GHSA-2464-fhr4-w9xw (unauthenticated RCE via image processing on glibc Linux, affects <=16.4.0) names sharp >= 0.35.5 as the workaround, and the installed sharp is 0.35.4.
  - where: `apps/web/next.config.ts:10`
  - fails when: An unauthenticated request drives the image optimizer to fetch and decode bytes the attacker published to a public GitHub repository, on a Next version that both sides report as flagged critical/high by the advisory database, with the image library below the version named as the workaround. The same endpoint is also an unmetered paid-transformation vector: no session, no limiter, attacker-chosen source and dimensions. Upgrade next and sharp and re-run the audit before ship.
  - fix: Bump next to >=16.4.1 and let the package manager pull sharp >=0.35.5; bump zod to >=4.6.6. All three are patch/minor bumps within the declared caret ranges, so only the lockfile changes.
  - ruling: Verified on the wire rather than from the manifest: next is installed at 16.3.5, apps/web/next.config.ts:10 configures images.remotePatterns for raw.githubusercontent.com and image.mux.com, and GET https://kettleworth.vercel.app/_next/image?url=<raw.githubusercontent.com PNG>&w=256&q=75 returns 200 image/png unauthenticated while a non-allowlisted host returns 400. So the optimizer is live, open to anyone, and decoding remote images on the installed version. The allowlist buys nothing: anyone can publish arbitrary bytes to a public GitHub repository and have them served from raw.githubusercontent.com, so the bytes reaching the image pipeline are attacker-chosen. I could not verify the advisory identifiers or their first patched versions from this checkout.
- **P1** `SEC-NEW2` POST /api/nutrition/log takes an unbounded free-form jsonb `detail` object with no size cap and no rate limit, so SEC-2's photo quota would not close the unbounded-storage hole - The food-log route validates every scalar field tightly and then accepts `detail: z.record(z.string(), z.unknown()).nullable().optional()` with no depth, key-count or byte limit, and writes it straight into a jsonb column. App Router route handlers carry no default body-size limit, so each request can push the platform's whole 4.5 MB payload allowance into the primary Postgres, and there is no per-account rate limit or row quota anywhere.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/nutrition/log/route.ts:6`
  - fails when: A stranger signs up and loops POST /api/nutrition/log with a perfectly valid five-field body plus a detail object of several megabytes of generated keys, ten connections at a time. Every request is a legitimate food-log entry, so nothing refuses it, and the account writes gigabytes of jsonb into the same Neon instance that serves every read path. It is the same unbounded-volume mechanism as SEC-2 at a second entry point, which is the point: a photo quota alone does not close it, so the fix has to be a per-account write quota and rate limit that covers both.
  - fix: Cap the serialised size of `detail` in the schema (e.g. a `.refine` that rejects over ~4 KB of JSON, or replace the open record with the handful of fields the client actually sends, which are the barcode/photo estimate shapes in packages/api/src/services/nutrition.ts). Then add the per-account rate limit SEC-5 recommends inside apps/web/lib/api.ts so every route() handler inherits one, and a per-account row cap in logFood.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/nutrition/log/route.ts:6 validates every scalar tightly and then accepts detail: z.record(z.string(), z.unknown()).nullable().optional() with no depth, key-count or byte limit, and logFood writes it into a jsonb column. There is no body-size limit in next.config.ts and no middleware, so the request is bounded only by the platform's payload allowance, and no per-account rate limit or row quota exists anywhere.
- **P1** `SEC-NEW8` GET /api/library is the only unauthenticated endpoint and validates nothing: limit and offset go through bare Number(), offset is never clamped, and every distinct q is a CDN cache miss that runs an ilike scan plus a count(*) on the Neon primary - The one route with no session check also has no zod schema: it reads six string params and two numbers straight off the query string and passes them to a service that clamps only the upper bound of limit. `Number("abc")` is NaN and `Math.min(NaN, 100)` is NaN, so a NaN LIMIT reaches the driver; offset is passed through with `p.offset ?? 0`, which does not catch NaN, and is never checked for being negative. Each request costs two queries including an unindexed `count(*)` over the whole filtered set, and because the response is cached per URL, a varying q defeats the cache and sends every request to the database. No rate limit exists on this or any other handler.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/library/route.ts:6`
  - fails when: One unauthenticated GET with limit=abc defeats the 100-row cap and returns the entire table in a half-megabyte response; every distinct q is a CDN miss, so an attacker varying the query string drives a full scan plus a count(*) on the Neon primary and half a megabyte of egress per request, with no session and no rate limit anywhere in the path. offset=-1 is an unhandled driver error surfacing as a 500 from the only public endpoint in the product.
  - fix: Validate the query string with zod in apps/web/app/api/library/route.ts: coerce limit and offset with `z.coerce.number().int()`, give limit `.min(1).max(100)` and offset `.min(0).max(5000)`, and constrain q to a sane length and character set. In packages/api/src/services/library.ts:31-37, clamp offset the way limit is clamped and skip the count(*) when an offset is supplied (or cache it). Give the endpoint a rate limit - it is the one handler with no session to key on, so key it on IP at the edge rather than in the lambda.
  - ruling: I measured this against the live site rather than reasoning about it. apps/web/app/api/library/route.ts:4-7 is the one handler with no session check and no zod schema; limit and offset go through bare Number(), and packages/api/src/services/library.ts:31 clamps with Math.min(p.limit ?? 40, 100), which is NaN for NaN, while :34 passes offset through unchecked. GET /api/library?limit=abc returns 200 with 523,120 bytes - the whole exercise table, eight times the 62,792 bytes that ?limit=99999 returns correctly clamped - and GET /api/library?offset=-1 returns 500. Each request also runs an unindexed ilike scan plus a count(*) over the filtered set.
- **P1** `SECRET-1` Injuries and medical flags are written in plaintext to ai_log, which has no cascade and so survives account hard delete - Every AI call writes the full prompt into `ai_log.input` in plaintext, including the user's injuries, medicalFlags and free-text answers. `ai_log.user_id` is a bare text column with no foreign key to `user`, so `deleteAccount()`'s `delete(user)` leaves those rows behind for ever, and `exportAccount()` does not return them. Both facts contradict the published privacy policy, which promises those exact fields are AES-256-GCM encrypted at rest and that deleting the account removes the user's records.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:62`
  - fails when: A user types 'my left shoulder impingement is worse and I am on sertraline' into the coach chat. That sentence is stored twice: once in coach_message.content and once inside ai_log.input.user, in clear, next to a column the product encrypts with AES-256-GCM precisely because it is special-category data. The user then exercises their right to erasure; user and every cascading table go, and the ai_log rows stay on the Neon database indefinitely with the deleted account's id still in user_id, outside the GDPR export as well, so neither the export nor the delete tells the truth about them.
  - fix: Two application-level changes, no schema change (the migration chain is frozen this run): (1) in `structured()` log a redacted prompt - store `task`, token counts and a hash or a field-allowlist instead of the raw `t.user`/`output`, or encrypt them with `encryptField` from packages/db/src/crypto.ts; (2) add `await db().delete(aiLog).where(eq(aiLog.userId, userId));` to `deleteAccount()` in packages/api/src/services/account.ts before the `delete(user)`, and include the user's ai_log rows in `exportAccount()` for as long as they are retained.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:62 writes input: { user: t.user.slice(0, 50000) } and the system prompt into ai_log with no redaction, and the prompts carry the decrypted sensitive fields: coach.ts:95 puts injuries and medicalFlags into the chat context and :103 embeds the whole JSON plus the last twelve turns as the user string, and tasks.ts:38 does the same for the intake free text. packages/db/src/schema/meta.ts:6 declares user_id as a bare text column - I grepped the schema and ai_log and audit_log are the only two user_id columns with no reference to user.id - so account.ts:34's delete(user) never reaches them, and there is no retention job anywhere. needs_debate is set because the fix scope is disputed: audit_log (meta.ts:22) has the identical shape and deleteAccount leaves the user's existing rows there too.
- **P1** `SECRET-2` sendEmail falls back to console.log, so with RESEND_API_KEY unset (documented as optional in production) magic-link sign-in URLs and weekly letters are written to the server log - `sendEmail()` prints the whole message, recipient address included, to stdout when `RESEND_API_KEY` is absent. The deploy runbook lists that key as optional for the production Vercel project, so the fallback is a documented production state, not a dev-only path. The three things that travel through `sendEmail` are a magic-link URL (a bearer credential that signs the holder in as that account), the weekly coach letter (health and training narrative addressed to a named email), and community moderation reports.
  - where: `packages/api/src/email.ts:7`
  - fails when: Anyone, signed in or not, submits an email address to the magic-link endpoint. better-auth mints a fifteen-minute token and the server writes the entire sign-in URL - a bearer credential that creates a session for that account - into the platform's runtime log, where it sits for the retention window and is readable by anyone with log access or any log drain, including for the owner's own address. The weekly letter goes the same way, so a member's training, weight trend and sleep narrative is logged against their email address. The second half is a plain functional break: the user is told the link was sent and no email exists.
  - fix: Make the credential required when NODE_ENV is production: throw at boot if RESEND_API_KEY is missing, and in the fallback log only the recipient and subject, never `msg.text`/`msg.html` (the magic link lives in the body). Update docs/DEPLOY.md:15 to move RESEND_API_KEY out of the optional list.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/email.ts:5-9 is const key = process.env.RESEND_API_KEY; if (!key) { console.log(...msg.text...); return; } - there is no NODE_ENV test anywhere in the file, so the [email:dev] label is decoration. The magic-link body at packages/api/src/auth.ts:29 is the full sign-in URL inside msg.text, which is the field the fallback prints. And this is the live configuration, which I checked at the dashboard rather than inferring from .env: RESEND_API_KEY is not among the nine variables in the production environment.
- **P1** `TEST-3` The whole server layer has no runnable test tier: api passes with --passWithNoTests, db/types/web declare no test task at all - All 21 test files live in packages/core (18) and packages/integrations (1) plus one e2e spec. packages/api - better-auth config, 15 services and the single AI funnel - has zero test files and a script that exits 0 anyway; packages/db (field encryption), packages/types (the Zod contracts) and apps/web (63 route handlers plus the shared auth wrapper) declare no `test` task, so a test written there would not be run by `pnpm test` or by CI's `pnpm -r test`.
  - where: `packages/api/package.json:12`
  - fails when: The coach.ts:39 bug is the scenario, already shipped: every Sunday letter promises what stalled, and the staleness engine is handed an empty array, so a member whose bench has not moved in four weeks is never told and never re-programmed. detectStaleness has a passing unit test; its only caller sits in a package with no runnable test tier, and nothing in the suite joins the two. The same shape covers 63 route handlers and packages/db/src/crypto.ts, where a test cannot even be placed in a file that would be executed.
  - fix: Add `"test": "vitest run"` plus a vitest.config.ts to packages/api, packages/db and apps/web (the unmerged branch already adds packages/api/vitest.config.ts), drop `--passWithNoTests` so an empty suite is red, and seed each with the service-level tests named in TEST-4, TEST-5 and TEST-8.
  - ruling: Verified by enumeration: all 19 unit test files live in packages/core (18) and packages/integrations (1), plus one e2e spec. packages/api - better-auth config, fifteen services and the single AI funnel - has no test file and its script is `vitest run --passWithNoTests`, so it exits 0; packages/db, packages/types, packages/ui and apps/web declare no test script at all, so a test written there would not run under `pnpm test` or CI's `pnpm -r test`. The cost is already on main and I checked it: coach.ts:39 declares `const trends: ... = []` and line 40 passes it to detectStaleness, so that input is always empty.
- **P1** `TEST-4` Nothing anywhere asserts an auth refusal: no test sends an unauthenticated or cross-user request - The boundary between one member's health data and another's is two lines of code - the session check in the shared route wrapper and a per-row ownership check in each service - and no test in the repository exercises either negatively. The one e2e signs in as a single account and only ever asserts success.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/api.ts:10`
  - fails when: A refactor of route() moves the session read inside the try block, or returns before the `if (!s)` line. Every one of the 63 handlers now answers unauthenticated requests. `pnpm test` stays green because it never touches apps/web, and the e2e still passes every assertion, because an endpoint that no longer requires a session still answers a request that happens to have one. The same silence covers dropping the ownership check: user B POSTs to /api/instance/<A's instance id>/log and writes into A's training history with the suite green.
  - fix: With the test task from TEST-3 in place, add two tests to packages/api: `logSet(userB, instanceOfUserA, set)` rejects, and a route-level test that `route()` returns 401 with no session and still 401s when the body is valid. Both are the negative case 09-tests requires for a confirmed security control.
  - ruling: api.ts:10 is the only session gate for 56 of the 63 route files, and session.ts:140 `if (!inst || inst.sessionUser !== userId) throw new Error("Not found")` is the only ownership gate on a logged set. No test file in the repository issues or mocks an HTTP request - there is no msw, nock, supertest or fetch in any of them - and no test asserts a 401 or 403. core-flow.spec.ts creates exactly one account and asserts only success; there is no second user anywhere in the suite.
- **P1** `TEST-5` The two contracts this run may not change - GDPR export/delete and field encryption - are pinned by no test, in a package where a test cannot run - AES-256-GCM field encryption for health samples and OAuth tokens, and the GDPR export format and hard delete, are the behaviours the brief forbids changing, and no test covers a round trip, a tampered blob, a rotated key, or the shape of an export. packages/db has no test task (TEST-3), so the encryption test cannot even be placed where the code is.
  - where: `packages/db/src/crypto.ts:18`
  - fails when: HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY is rotated, or a row was written under a different key - the CI workflow hardcodes one at ci.yml:17. decryptField throws for those rows, exportAccount propagates the throw, and api.ts:27 turns it into a 400 with the raw message. The member's export returns nothing and their hard delete fails, which is exactly the contract the run is told to protect, and the suite is green because nothing calls either function. A round-trip test plus one tampered-tag test would have failed first.
  - fix: Add packages/db test task + a crypto test: encrypt/decrypt round trip, null and empty-string passthrough, a flipped byte in the tag rejected, and a wrong key rejected with a named error rather than a raw throw. Add one packages/api test that exportAccount returns every documented top-level key and that deleteAccount leaves no row for the user id.
  - ruling: crypto.ts:18-24's decryptField will throw from d.final() on a wrong key or a tampered tag, and no test exercises a round trip, a tampered blob or a rotated key - packages/db has no test script, so the test cannot even be placed beside the code. The GDPR surfaces are one line each (account/export/route.ts:4 and account/delete/route.ts:4) with no test of the export's shape or the delete's effect. These are the two contracts the brief forbids changing.
- **P1** `TEST-6` The e2e suite can be pointed at production and has been: it signs up a real account, writes health data, and never cleans up - `baseURL` is taken straight from E2E_BASE_URL with no check that the target is a local or staging host, and the spec has no cleanup hook, so a run against kettleworth.vercel.app creates a permanent account on the production database and drives real intake, programme generation and AI calls through it. The brief records that this has already happened.
  - where: `apps/web/playwright.config.ts:6`
  - fails when: An operator wants to check the live deploy and runs the suite with E2E_BASE_URL set to the production URL - the documented way to point it at a deployed host. Both Playwright projects run, so production gains two accounts with encrypted profile rows including the injury and medical free-text fields, two programmes, training sessions and logged sets, plus a dozen Claude calls on the production key. Those accounts persist, are counted in every metric, and are fanned out to by the Monday 06:00 letter cron until someone remembers the purge script - which, per OPS-NEW1, now fails for any of them that touched community.
  - fix: Refuse a non-local target unless an explicit opt-in is set: in playwright.config.ts, throw unless the resolved baseURL host is localhost/127.0.0.1 or E2E_ALLOW_REMOTE is set; and add a `test.afterAll` that deletes the account it created (POST /api/account/delete with confirm:"DELETE" while signed in) so a run cleans up after itself even locally.
  - ruling: playwright.config.ts:6 takes baseURL straight from E2E_BASE_URL with no host check of any kind, and :8 stops the local server whenever it is set, so one environment variable retargets the suite at any host. The suite is not read-only: core-flow.spec.ts signs up for real, fills age, sex, height and weight, clicks through the AI intake follow-ups, generates a programme and logs sets. There is no test.afterAll, no API teardown and no delete call in the file; the only cleanup is scripts/purge-test-accounts.ts, run by hand with DATABASE_URL pointed at production. The brief records that stray @kettleworth.test accounts are on the production database now.

## Backlog - confirmed P2 and P3

- **P2** `AI-9` Timed-out and retried provider calls are billed but never recorded, so ai_log systematically under-reports spend and no route bounds its own duration - The SDK is configured with a 120 s timeout and two retries, but the log row is written once per `structured()` call and takes `usage` only from a successful response. Every retried or timed-out attempt is work the provider bills and the application has no record of, which also means any future cap built on `ai_log` under-counts. Separately, only one AI route sets `maxDuration`, so the platform can kill the handler while the billed generation continues.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:14`
  - fails when: A user taps 'read my photos' on a slow connection. The platform's default function duration expires before the vision call returns, so the handler is killed and the browser gets an error; the provider finishes the generation and bills it; client.ts:62 never runs, so ai_log has no row at all. The user retries twice. Three generations are paid for, the product showed three errors, and an operator querying ai_log sees nothing - which also means any per-user cap built on ai_log counts low by every retry and every timeout.
  - fix: Write the `ai_log` row per attempt (or record attempt count and the token usage carried on the error), set `maxRetries` to 1 for the long vision tasks, and export a `maxDuration` on every AI route that is comfortably above the SDK timeout for that task so the platform does not kill a call that is already being billed.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:14 constructs the SDK with timeout 120_000 and maxRetries 2, so one logical call is up to three billed attempts; usage is initialised empty at :41 and only assigned at :50 after a successful parse, and the single log row at :62 writes inputTokens: usage.input_tokens ?? null. The handler-duration half also checks out: maxDuration is exported from one route only (nutrition/estimate:4, set to 60s, itself below the SDK's own 120s timeout), and not from the vision route that base64-encodes up to three 8 MB photos (photos.ts:53-55).
- **P2** `AI-NEW3` A second model-driven write path, unguarded: extractFromAnswer's output is written straight into the profile, with injury severity that can only escalate and feeds the engine's exercise-exclusion rule - AI-10 treats the coach chat as the only place model output drives a state change. There is a second, and it has fewer guards: the intake extraction writes injuries, medical flags, disliked foods, timeline, sleep, stress and notes into the profile with no confirmation step, no audit row, no length bound on the schema, and a merge rule that lets injury severity rise but never fall. A `severe` injury written this way changes what the deterministic programme engine is allowed to prescribe.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/onboarding/followup/route.ts:10`
  - fails when: During intake a user types 'my knee was wrecked years ago but it is completely fine now'. The extraction returns { region: "knee", severity: "severe" }; it is written with no confirmation step, and from then on the deterministic engine refuses every knee-loading movement in the generated programme. Because the merge only escalates, a later answer saying the knee is fine cannot undo it through the intake path - the user has to find the field in settings. A second model-driven write path with fewer guards than the chat one.
  - fix: Bound the Extraction schema (`z.array(z.string().max(200)).max(20)` for medicalFlags and dislikedFoods, `.max(20)` on injuries, `notes: z.string().max(500).nullable()`), truncate the concatenation at route.ts:24 to the stored field's cap, and treat a model-written injury or medical flag as a proposal: return it for the user to accept in the intake UI the way photos.ts:64 applyAnalysis already requires explicit confirmation, rather than writing it. Add an auditLog row for every model-driven profile write, and let the merge at :16 take the model's severity only when the user confirms it.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/onboarding/followup/route.ts:10-25 writes the model's extraction straight into the profile with no confirmation and no audit row, and the injury merge at :16 keeps the previous entry whenever its severity rank is greater or equal, so severity can rise and never fall through this path. packages/core/src/programme/selection.ts:22-23 then treats a severe injury as a hard exclusion of every exercise whose contraindicatedRegions include that region. The Extraction schema (tasks.ts:27-35) bounds the numbers but not the strings or the array lengths.
- **P2** `AI-NEW6` Nothing in the test suite touches the AI boundary, and `--passWithNoTests` makes a package with zero tests report green - packages/api holds the entire AI layer, every service and auth, and contains no test file at all. Its test script is `vitest run --passWithNoTests`, so `pnpm -r test` in CI reports success for it. The invariant the whole product rests on - that structured() returns null and every caller falls back to deterministic copy - is pinned by nothing, and the caps the other findings ask for will land with no test that they bite.
  - where: `packages/api/package.json:1`
  - fails when: The invariant the whole product leans on - structured() returns null on any failure and every caller falls back to deterministic copy - is pinned by no test, so a change that makes a caller throw instead reaches production green. The same gap applies to the caps the other findings ask for: a per-account counter merged with the window arithmetic or the key field wrong is indistinguishable from today's state in CI, and the discovery mechanism is the invoice.
  - fix: Add packages/api/vitest.config.ts and a first test file that mocks @anthropic-ai/sdk and asserts: (a) structured() returns null and the client is never constructed when aiAvailable() is false; (b) an over-long `t.user` is refused before messages.parse is called; (c) once the cap from AI-1 exists, that the request after the limit is refused and that ai_log records the refusal. Drop `--passWithNoTests` from packages/api's test script once a test exists, so an empty suite cannot pass again.
  - ruling: packages/api holds the AI layer, every service and auth and contains no test file at all (I counted: 0 under packages/api, 18 under packages/core, 1 under packages/integrations, 1 e2e spec under apps/web), and its test script is vitest run --passWithNoTests, so pnpm -r test (CI step .github/workflows/ci.yml:27) reports success for it.
- **P2** `CODE-10` GDPR export runs a discarded query and then one query per training session - exportAccount fetches the exercise instances of the first session, throws that result away in a `.then`, and then loops over every session id issuing a separate query. The export is a single unbatched N+1 with no bound on N and no transaction, so it gets slower in proportion to a user's history and can be killed mid-document.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/account.ts:23`
  - fails when: account.ts:23 queries exercise_instance for the first session id, discards the rows inside a `.then`, and then loops over every session id issuing one query each, sequentially. A user two years in has roughly 400 sessions, so the GDPR export issues ~400 serial round trips before it starts serialising, and the cost grows linearly with account age; at a 5 ms round trip that is seconds of pure waiting added to a regulator-facing endpoint that has no paging and no streaming, and a long-standing account can exceed the function's duration limit with no partial result. The discarded first select doubles the cost for a single-session account.
  - fix: One query: `where(inArray(exerciseInstance.sessionId, sessionIds))`, and drop the discarded select.
  - ruling: Read the expression; `inArray` is used for exactly this shape three times in the same package (session.ts:40, session.ts:57, photos.ts:129-134), so the loop is an oversight rather than a constraint.
- **P2** `CODE-11` The OAuth callback starts the first provider sync as a floating promise, which a serverless host kills at response - completeConnect fires syncProvider without awaiting it and immediately returns, after which the callback route redirects. On Vercel the function is frozen or torn down when the response is sent, so the first sync is cut off part-way: a sync_job row is left in `running` for ever, `lastSyncAt` stays null and only the chunks already inserted survive.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:38`
  - fails when: integrations.ts:38 calls `syncProvider(userId, p.info.id).catch(...)` with no await and no `after()`, and completeConnect returns immediately so the callback route can redirect. syncProvider inserts a sync_job row with status "running" first (line 66) and only marks it done at line 80. On a serverless host the invocation is frozen or torn down once the response is sent, so a first 30-day backfill is cut off part-way: lastSyncAt stays null, only the chunks already committed survive, and the orphaned `running` sync_job row is indistinguishable from a sync in flight - nothing reaps it and nothing times it out. Readiness is then computed from a partial window until the user syncs again by hand.
  - fix: Schedule it with `after(() => syncProvider(...))` in the route handler, or insert a queued sync_job row and let a worker pick it up; either way record the failure on connected_provider.lastError instead of a console.warn.
  - ruling: Read the floating call and syncProvider's job bookkeeping. The failure is not the dedupe (onConflictDoNothing makes a re-read safe) but the unreapable `running` row and the silent partial backfill.
- **P2** `CODE-12` Check-then-insert without constraint handling turns ordinary races into raw Postgres errors shown to the user, or into duplicate rows - Three community writes assume they are the only writer and do not handle the unique constraints that exist. The route helper returns the raw exception message to the client (api.ts:27), so the user is shown Postgres internals instead of a usable error - and where there is no constraint, the race silently duplicates rows.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:82`
  - fails when: Three community writes assume they are the only writer against constraints that exist. upsertCommunityProfile (community.ts:82) conflict-targets userId while `handle` carries its own unique constraint (schema/community.ts:6), so a new member who picks a taken handle gets `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "community_profile_handle_unique"` in the error toast - api.ts:27 returns the exception message verbatim - with no way to proceed. toggleReaction (community.ts:184-185) is a select-then-insert against `uniqueIndex("reaction_pk").on(postId, userId)`, so a double tap on a phone surfaces the index name as a 400. ensureDefaultChallenges (community.ts:190-196) selects one row and inserts two if none exists with no constraint at all, so two users opening Challenges at the same moment create four challenges and the `if (any) return` guard then freezes the duplicates for everyone.
  - fix: Catch the unique violation (Postgres code 23505) and map it to a field-level message: pre-check the handle and return "that handle is taken"; make the reaction an upsert/delete keyed on (postId,userId) with `onConflictDoNothing`; seed the default challenges in a migration or guard them with a unique key on (name, startsOn).
  - ruling: I checked each constraint in packages/db/src/schema/community.ts and the error exit in apps/web/lib/api.ts. All three sites are reachable by an ordinary signed-in member with no race-specific skill.
