- **P2** `CODE-13` completeOnboarding is not idempotent: a repeated call duplicates the intake maxes and the intake weigh-in and regenerates the AI summary - The function has no guard on `onboardingCompletedAt` and the route exposes it as a plain POST. Calling it twice inserts a second set of `estimated_max` rows and a second `body_measurement` row for today, and makes a second AI summary call.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/profile.ts:36`
  - fails when: completeOnboarding (profile.ts:36-45) has no check on `rec.onboardingCompletedAt` before inserting one estimated_max row per baseline max (line 41) and a body_measurement row for today (line 42), and neither table carries a unique key. The route is a plain POST. A slow response - it makes an AI summary call - plus a second tap or a connection retry therefore writes two intake weigh-ins for the same day and two competing intake estimated_max rows with the same recordedAt, which the "latest wins" loop in generateAndSaveProgramme (programme.ts:18-20) then resolves arbitrarily, and the duplicate weigh-in is exactly the two-rows-one-day input that makes the weekly weight trend in CODE-7 wrong.
  - fix: Return early when `onboardingCompletedAt` is already set (or use `onConflictDoNothing` with a unique key on (userId, measuredOn, source) and (userId, exerciseId, source)).
  - ruling: Read the function and confirmed from packages/db/src/schema/profile.ts that body_measurement has only a non-unique (userId, measuredOn) index; estimated_max has no uniqueness either. A second AI summary call is also paid for.
- **P2** `CODE-6` measuredActiveKcalPerDay divides total active calories by the number of samples, not by days, so calorie targets are refined off a wrong number - The value named "per day" is a per-sample mean over a 14-day window. Providers that write one `active_calories` sample per workout rather than one per day therefore produce a number several times the true daily average, and days with no sample never pull the mean down. That number overrides the calculated calorie target and is quoted back to the user as a daily figure.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/nutrition.ts:15`
  - fails when: nutrition.ts:14-15 selects every `active_calories` sample in a 14-day window and divides the sum by `kcal.length`, the number of samples, then names the result measuredActiveKcalPerDay. Providers that write one sample per activity rather than one per day (whoop and strava adapters, and logManualActivity at integrations.ts:120) therefore produce a per-activity mean, not a daily mean, and days with no sample never pull it down. The number is not advisory: targets.ts:15 recomputes the calorie target as `bmr * 1.15 + measuredActiveKcalPerDay`, replaces the baseline target when the two differ by more than 100 kcal, and targets.ts:19 tells the user "Your wearable shows about N active kcal a day". A Strava user with eight 700 kcal rides in a fortnight is credited 700 kcal/day against a true 400, and a user logging two activities a day gets the mirror error and is told to eat too little.
  - fix: Sum the samples per calendar day, then divide by the number of days in the window (not the number of days with data), in the user's time zone; keep the >=5 guard on distinct days rather than samples.
  - ruling: Confirmed the division by sample count at nutrition.ts:15 with no grouping by day and no division by the window, and traced the value into targets.ts:14-21 where it overrides the target and is quoted back as a daily figure.
- **P2** `CODE-7` The weekly weight trend divides by max(1, days), turning a one-day gap between two weigh-ins into a 7x weekly claim that is emailed to the user - weekStats computes kg/week from only the first and last weigh-in in a 21-day window, with the day count clamped to a minimum of 1. Two weigh-ins a day or a day apart - normal for a new user and for anyone who weighs daily - produce a trend seven times the raw difference, which is stated as fact in the Sunday letter.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:36`
  - fails when: coach.ts:36 computes the weekly weight trend from only the first and last weigh-in in a 21-day window and clamps the day count with `Math.max(1, ...)`. Two weigh-ins a day apart - ordinary for anyone who weighs daily, and the normal case for a new account - are multiplied by 7: 80.4 kg on Sunday and 79.6 kg on Monday is reported as -5.60 kg a week. That figure is rendered verbatim in the fallback letter (coach.ts:62) and handed to the model as the week's ground truth (coach.ts:66), so the user is emailed it as fact. Two weigh-ins on the same day give the same arithmetic with the clamp standing in for a zero gap.
  - fix: Require a span of at least 7 days (as measuredInputs already does, nutrition.ts:22) or fit the trend over daily means, and return null when the span is shorter than the period being reported.
  - ruling: Read the expression at coach.ts:35-36 and its two consumers. measuredInputs in nutrition.ts:22 already requires a 7-day span for the same calculation, so the correct guard exists one file away.
- **P2** `CODE-8` Staleness detection in the weekly letter is always called with an empty array, so the "what stalled" half can never fire - weekStats declares the per-exercise trend list, never populates it, and passes it to detectStaleness. The adaptations attached to every letter are therefore only the ones adaptWeek derives from feedback, although the letter prompt and the product copy promise what stalled.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:39`
  - fails when: coach.ts:39 declares `const trends: Parameters<typeof detectStaleness>[0] = [];`, never assigns to it, and spreads `detectStaleness(trends)` into the letter's adaptations at line 40. The second spread is therefore always empty: the "what stalled" half of the Sunday letter can never fire. Meanwhile runWeeklyAdaptation does compute it properly (adaptation.ts:57, `detectStaleness(await exerciseTrends(userId, role), ...)`) and swaps the exercise out, so the plan changes for a reason the only place that explains the plan never mentions - and the model is told the week's adaptations are the volume ones alone.
  - fix: Call the existing `exerciseTrends(userId, role)` (exported from adaptation.ts) and pass its result, or delete the `detectStaleness(trends)` spread and take the adaptations from the stored `week.adaptations` the adaptation run already wrote.
  - ruling: Confirmed the empty array and the never-written comment above it, and confirmed the real computation exists and is exported from adaptation.ts.
- **P2** `CODE-9` The queued-block cleanup in programme generation matches on createdAt within one second, so it never runs and ghost planned sessions survive - When a second block is queued, the code archives the previously queued programme and then tries to delete its planned sessions by selecting archived programmes created in the last second. The programme it wants was created earlier, so the select always returns nothing and the delete never executes - the old queued block's planned sessions stay in the user's schedule.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:43`
  - fails when: programme.ts:42 archives the previously queued programme, then line 43 tries to find what it just archived with `status = 'archived' and createdAt > now() - interval '1 second'`. The programme it wants was created minutes or days earlier, and the new one is not inserted until line 46, so the select can never match and the delete on line 44 never runs. A user who extends twice before finishing the current block keeps block B's planned sessions in their schedule alongside block C's: todayState (session.ts:36) and upcomingSessions select by userId and date only, so two sessions sit on the same future date and planDay picks whichever sorts first.
  - fix: Capture the archived programme's id from a `.returning()` on the update (or select the scheduled programme before archiving it) and delete its planned sessions by that id.
  - ruling: Read the transaction body at programme.ts:37-45. The predicate is on createdAt rather than on the rows the preceding UPDATE touched, and a `.returning()` on that UPDATE is the obvious correct source of the ids.
- **P2** `CODE-NEW3` The photo upload's 3-file and 8 MB limits are applied after the entire multipart body has been buffered into memory - The handler calls `req.formData()` and collects every part before any limit is consulted; the `Math.min(files.length, 3)` cap and savePhoto's 8 MB check both run afterwards, on data already resident in the function's heap. Neither bounds the request, so the stated limits cannot protect memory.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/photos/route.ts:16`
  - fails when: apps/web/app/api/photos/route.ts:16 calls `await req.formData()`, which parses and buffers every part of the multipart body, before any limit is consulted: the three-file cap is on line 22 and the 8 MB per-file check is deeper still, in photos.ts:19, by which point line 24 has already run `Buffer.from(await f.arrayBuffer())` on that file too. There is no Content-Length check, no streaming and no rate limit above it (only getSession on line 13). A free account that POSTs twenty 8 MB JPEG parts has all twenty materialised in the function's heap so the handler can discard seventeen of them; the app's own stated limits cannot protect memory or transfer because they are evaluated after the data has arrived.
  - fix: Reject on Content-Length before reading the body (3 x 8 MB plus overhead), and read the multipart stream part by part, counting parts and bytes and aborting on the fourth part or the first over-size one, instead of calling req.formData(). Move the type and size checks to that boundary so savePhoto's checks become assertions rather than the only gate.
  - ruling: Read the handler and savePhoto. Rated P2 rather than P1 because the practical ceiling is set by the host's own request-body limit, which I could not verify from the repository - what is certain and in the code is that the limits are enforced after buffering rather than at the boundary.
- **P2** `CODE-NEW4` The public library endpoint drops its own row cap when limit is not a number, and 500s on a negative offset - `Number(s.get("limit"))` yields NaN for any non-numeric query value; `??` does not catch NaN, so `Math.min(NaN, 100)` is NaN, and Drizzle 0.45.2 omits a NaN limit from the compiled SQL entirely - the 100-row cap disappears and the full 876-row exercise table is returned. A negative offset survives into the statement and Postgres rejects it, and this handler has no try/catch, so the caller gets an unhandled 500. The endpoint is unauthenticated and the cache key includes the query string, so both are cheap to repeat.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/library/route.ts:6`
  - fails when: apps/web/app/api/library/route.ts:6 passes `Number(s.get("limit") ?? 40)` into searchExercises; `??` catches a missing parameter, not an unparseable one, so `?limit=x` yields NaN and library.ts:31's `Math.min(NaN, 100)` is NaN. I compiled that against the project's own Drizzle: `.limit(NaN).offset(NaN)` emits `select "id" from "x"` with no limit and no offset clause at all, while `.limit(100).offset(5)` emits both. The 100-row cap therefore disappears and the endpoint - unauthenticated, by its own comment "library is public" - returns the whole exercise table, and because Cache-Control keys on the query string every `?limit=x1`, `?limit=x2` is a fresh miss. The same compile shows `.offset(-1)` surviving into the statement as `offset $1` with -1, which Postgres rejects; this handler is a bare GET with no try/catch, so that escapes as a 500 rather than a 400.
  - fix: Parse the query string with a Zod schema at the route boundary (`z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).catch(40)` and `.min(0).catch(0)`), the way every `route()`-based handler already does, and clamp in library.ts with `Number.isFinite` rather than `??`. The same NaN hazard applies to any other `Number(searchParams.get(...))` call.
  - ruling: I did not take the Drizzle behaviour on trust - I compiled NaN, negative and normal cases with the version in packages/db and got exactly those three outputs. Rated P2: the response is still bounded by a small static table, so the consequence is uncapped egress and a 500 rather than a cliff.
- **P2** `CODE-NEW7` The route helper turns every server fault into HTTP 400 with the exception message as the body, so no 5xx is ever emitted and internals reach the user - The single error exit in the shared route helper is a 400 carrying `e.message` verbatim. A Postgres constraint violation, a Neon connection failure, a missing row and a user mistake are therefore indistinguishable to every client, to the offline queue and to any monitoring that watches status codes - and the raw message, including constraint names, is returned to the caller.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/api.ts:27`
  - fails when: apps/web/lib/api.ts has exactly one error exit besides the needs_confirmation 409: line 27 returns HTTP 400 with `e.message` verbatim. There is no 500 branch anywhere in the helper, and every route() handler funnels through it. So a Neon connection failure, a unique-constraint violation, a missing row and a user mistake are the same status code to every client and to anything watching status codes, and the raw message - including Postgres constraint names such as `community_profile_handle_unique` - is handed to the caller. Nothing in the response tells a client whether a retry could succeed, which is what makes the offline queue's deletion-on-400 (CODE-1) unfixable from the client alone.
  - fix: Classify at the choke point: keep 400 for a Zod failure and for errors explicitly tagged as client faults (give service errors a `status` or a typed class), map 'Not found' to 404, unique violations (Postgres 23505) to 409 with a field-level message, and return 500/503 with a generic body plus a request id for everything else. One edit to api.ts fixes nine route entry points at once and is the precondition for fixing CODE-1 properly.
  - ruling: Read the catch block. Rated P2 on its own consequences - internals disclosed to an authenticated caller and a status code that carries no retryability - rather than P1, because errors are still visible in logs via the console.error on line 26. It is the server half of CODE-1 and the precondition for fixing it properly.
- **P2** `CODE-NEW8` The manual activity endpoint accepts an unvalidated timestamp, which crashes on garbage, poisons every rolling window when dated in the future, and raises a raw unique violation when reused - `at` is typed `z.string().optional()` with no date validation and no range. A non-date throws out of `toISOString()`; a far-future date writes a health_sample that stays inside every rolling window for ever, permanently skewing readiness and the calorie target; and reusing the same `at` hits the dedupe index on an insert that - unlike the one on the next line - has no conflict handling.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/activity/route.ts:5`
  - fails when: apps/web/app/api/activity/route.ts:5 types `at` as `z.string().optional()` with no format and no range - the only string in that schema without either. integrations.ts:115-118 then does `new Date(a.at)` and `at.toISOString()`, so `{"at":"soon"}` throws RangeError and api.ts:27 returns 'Invalid time value' as a 400. A valid but far-future date is worse: the workout and active_calories samples are written dated 2099 and both rolling windows that read them have only a lower bound - recentSamples (integrations.ts:92) and measuredInputs (nutrition.ts:14) - so that one row is inside every readiness computation and every calorie recalculation for ever, and CODE-6's division by sample count turns a 9,500 kcal entry into a permanent daily average. There is no UI to delete a manual activity. Reusing the same `at` hits `health_sample_dedupe_idx`, and the workout insert at integrations.ts:119 has no onConflictDoNothing although the active_calories insert on the very next line does, so the constraint name is returned to the user.
  - fix: Validate at the boundary: `at: z.string().datetime().optional()` plus a server-side range check rejecting anything in the future or more than, say, a year old; add `.onConflictDoNothing()` to integrations.ts:119 to match line 120; and give both rolling windows an upper bound (`lte(healthSample.startAt, new Date())`).
  - ruling: I verified the schema, the unguarded Date construction, the asymmetry between lines 119 and 120, and that neither rolling window has an upper bound. Self-inflicted only - it is the caller's own data - which is why this is P2 and not higher.
- **P2** `LEAN-2` Four byte-identical copies of `addDays` and two of `mondayOf` across the service layer, with no date module anywhere - `addDays` is declared, character for character, in four service files; `mondayOf` in two. Calendar dates are this app's primary key type (`scheduledOn`, `measuredOn`, `loggedOn`, `takenOn`, `weekStartsOn`), yet `packages/core` has `units.ts` for unit conversion and nothing for dates, so every service invented its own.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:10`
  - fails when: `const addDays = (d, n) => { const x = new Date(d + "T00:00:00Z"); ... }` is declared character for character in four service files - programme.ts:10, adaptation.ts:10, life-mode.ts:10 and coach.ts:16 - and `mondayOf` in two (coach.ts:15, nutrition.ts:10), while packages/core exports twenty modules and none of them owns dates. All six copies hard-code UTC. Calendar dates are this app's primary key type (scheduledOn, measuredOn, loggedOn, takenOn, weekStartsOn) and local-date handling is already a live problem - see localDate in session.ts:24 and localTodayIso in apps/web/lib/local-date.ts, and CODE-5 - so the day the week-start or local-midnight rule is corrected it will be corrected in whichever file the fixer opened, and the letter's week, the programme's schedule and the life-mode expiry will then disagree by a day.
  - fix: Create `packages/core/src/dates.ts` exporting `addDays(iso, n)`, `mondayOf(d)` and `todayIso()`, export it from packages/core/src/index.ts, and delete the six local declarations. ~12 lines added, 6 lines deleted, no behaviour change, so the existing 137 core tests are the regression net and a new dates.test.ts pins the moved behaviour first.
  - ruling: I diffed the four addDays declarations and the two mondayOf declarations myself. Four copies of one rule, with a known pending change to that rule, is the P2 duplication case.
- **P2** `LEAN-3` The "profile exists" invariant has three different implementations and is enforced in 0 of 62 route handlers, so `POST /api/coach/chat` on a fresh account throws a TypeError - One precondition - the signed-in account has completed intake - is written three ways: a 3-line copy-paste in 8 page components, `throw new Error("No profile")` in 6 services, and a bare non-null assertion `!` in 4 places. None of the 62 route handlers checks it, and the `!` sites turn a missing profile into a TypeError instead of a handled error. There is no shared `requireOnboarded`/`requireProfile`.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:88`
  - fails when: One precondition - this account has a profile - is written three ways: a bare non-null assertion (`const rec = (await getProfile(userId))!` at coach.ts:88, repeated at coach.ts:116 and twice in profile.ts), `if (!rec) throw new Error("No profile")` in six services, and a page-level redirect copied into seven /app pages. getProfile is declared `Promise<ProfileRecord | null>` and genuinely returns null for an account that has not started intake. No route handler checks it. So POST /api/coach/chat on a fresh signed-up account writes the user's message to coach_message (coach.ts:99), then reads `rec.profile.primaryGoal` on undefined, and api.ts:27 returns 400 with the TypeError text as the body, leaving an orphan message with no reply. The UI never hits it because the gate lives on /app/coach/page.tsx, not on the endpoint that page calls.
  - fix: Two small moves. (1) Add `export async function requireProfile(userId: string)` to packages/api/src/services/profile.ts that throws a stable `Error("Complete your intake first")`, and replace the 4 `!` assertions and 6 inline throws with it. (2) Add an `onboarded: true` option to the `route()` helper in apps/web/lib/api.ts that performs the same check once, and set it on the handlers whose services need a profile (coach/*, nutrition/*, programme/generate, photos/[id]/analyse, quests/*). Both are behaviour-preserving for onboarded users.
  - ruling: Read coach.ts:88 and :95 against profile.ts:18-22 and confirmed the assertion is unchecked and the route helper performs no onboarding check. The defect is the forgotten site produced by having the invariant in three hand-written forms.
- **P2** `LEAN-4` The exercise-instance ownership guard is copy-pasted four times and the fifth site forgot it - Four functions that take an `instanceId` carry the identical two-line join-and-compare ownership guard. The fifth, `substitutesForInstance`, selects the instance by id alone and never compares it to `userId` - the textbook consequence of a guard that lives in five hand-written copies instead of one helper.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:218`
  - fails when: Four functions that take an instanceId carry the identical two-line guard - join exercise_instance to training_session and compare sessionUser to userId, throwing 'Not found' (logSet at session.ts:139-140, swapExercise at :179-180, and the same pair in resetInstance and adjustRemainingLoads at :421-422). The fifth, substitutesForInstance (session.ts:217-220), selects the instance by id alone and never compares it to userId, so any signed-in account calling GET /api/instance/<another account's instance uuid>/substitutes is served that stranger's exercise substitutes. The disclosed facts are which exercise another account has planned and that the id exists.
  - fix: Extract the four copies into one private helper in session.ts - `async function ownedInstance(userId: string, instanceId: string)` returning the joined row or throwing `Error("Not found")` - and call it from all five functions including `substitutesForInstance`. Five call sites, ~8 lines net deletion, no behaviour change for the four that already check.
  - ruling: I read all five sites and confirmed the fifth has neither the join nor the comparison. Rated P2, not higher: the id is a v4 uuid (`uuid("id").primaryKey().defaultRandom()`) and I found no path that discloses another user's instance id, so there is no practical enumeration - which is exactly why the missing guard survived. The altitude defect is the guard existing in five hand-written copies with no helper.
- **P2** `LEAN-5` `entitlements()` has zero call sites in the whole repository - a gate that gates nothing, documented as "one line later" - `entitlements()` computes four feature flags from a `subscription` row and `BILLING_ENFORCED`, and nothing calls it. It is exported from the package barrel and never imported. Worse than dead: `docs/BRIEF.md:11` states the helper was "shipped so gating is one line later", which is false - there are 23 LLM call sites and 62 route handlers and not one of them has the line.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/entitlements.ts:6`
  - fails when: entitlements() computes four feature flags from the subscription row and BILLING_ENFORCED and has no call site in the repository: a grep over apps and packages returns only the declaration and `export * from "./services/entitlements"` at packages/api/src/index.ts:15. It is dead code that reads as a control, and docs state the gate is 'one line later' - which it is not: the four flags it returns include no AI flag at all, so wired up as written it would still gate none of the coach chat, the photo reads or the meal estimates, the exposures the brief calls most likely to hold a P0. A reviewer who believes the doc sizes the AI-abuse work wrongly.
  - fix: Either delete the file and the barrel export, or - the better choice given billing is Phase 1.5 - keep it, add the `aiCoach` flag ARCHITECTURE.md:337 already specifies, and call it from the one place that matters (`structured()` in packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:36). Whichever is chosen, correct docs/BRIEF.md:11 so it says the gate is unwired rather than ready.
  - ruling: I ran the grep over source (excluding .next build output, where the symbol does appear) and found no caller and no dynamic access. Dead abstraction plus a false claim in the docs that misleads the sizing of the work that matters: P2.
- **P2** `LEAN-6` The goal-calorie-delta rule is written twice and the two copies have already drifted: the `strength` branch is missing from one - `computeBaseline` and `nutritionTargets` each carry their own copy of the goal-to-calorie-delta rule, the safe-calorie floor and the fat-gram rule. The delta copies are already out of step: one has four branches, the other three. `nutritionTargets` then overwrites the baseline's number with its own, so the drift wins.
  - where: `packages/core/src/nutrition/targets.ts:16`
  - fails when: The goal-to-calorie-delta rule exists twice and the copies have already drifted. packages/core/src/metrics.ts:99 has four branches - `fat_loss ? -0.18 : muscle ? 0.1 : strength ? 0.05 : 0` - and packages/core/src/nutrition/targets.ts:16 has three, with `strength` falling through to 0. nutritionTargets then overwrites the baseline's number with its own whenever wearable data moves it by more than 100 kcal (targets.ts:18-21), so the drifted copy wins: a strength user who connects a wearable silently loses their 5% surplus, and the rationale pushed onto the screen attributes the change to their wearable rather than to a missing branch. The same pair also duplicates the calorie floor and the fat-gram rule.
  - fix: Export the rule once from metrics.ts - `export const goalDelta = (goal: Goal) => ...` - and have targets.ts:16 call it. Same for the floor and the fat-gram rule. Pin it first with a test asserting that `nutritionTargets` for a `strength` profile with and without wearable input both keep the +5%.
  - ruling: I read both expressions and confirmed `strength` is a real enum member and that targets.ts has no branch for it. One domain constant with two owners and no shared export.
- **P2** `LEAN-8` The photos routes hand-roll the auth-and-error scaffolding `route()` already provides, and their 401 shape has drifted from every other endpoint - 55 of 62 route handlers are 3-4 lines because `route()` in apps/web/lib/api.ts does auth, validation, error mapping and logging once. The two photos routes re-implement the auth half by hand in three places - and the copies have already diverged: one returns the 401 as plain text while the helper and the other copies return JSON.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/photos/route.ts:6`
  - fails when: The three photos handlers re-implement by hand what route() does once, and the copies have diverged: apps/web/app/api/photos/[id]/route.ts:6 answers an expired session with `new NextResponse("Unauthorised", { status: 401 })` - text/plain - while the DELETE eleven lines below it, both handlers in photos/route.ts, and the shared helper all answer the same condition with JSON. A client that does `r.json()` on the image route's 401 gets a parse exception instead of `{error}`. The hand-rolled POST also rebuilds the helper's catch block without its console.error, so photo-upload failures are the one entry point that leaves no server log line.
  - fix: Convert all three photos handlers to `route(undefined, async ({ userId, req, params }) => ...)`, returning the image `NextResponse` directly from the GET - the helper passes a `Response` through unchanged. That deletes the three hand-rolled auth blocks and the duplicated catch, and makes the 401 uniform across all 62 handlers.
  - ruling: I read all three handlers and the helper. The other non-helper routes each have a stated reason to opt out (public library, CRON_SECRET, Mux HMAC, better-auth catch-all, OAuth redirect); multipart is not one, since route() accepts `schema: undefined`, hands the handler the raw req and passes a returned Response through unchanged.
- **P2** `LEAN-NEW2` DAY_OFFSETS is written three times and the third copy is read against a different week origin, so meal-plan training days do not match the days the programme scheduled - The training-day grid `{1:[0],2:[0,3],3:[0,2,4],4:[0,1,3,4],5:[0,1,2,4,5],6:[0,1,2,3,4,5],7:[0,1,2,3,4,5,6]}` appears in three files. programme.ts:9 and life-mode.ts:9 apply it as offsets from `programme.startDate`, which is whatever day the user pressed Generate. nutrition.ts:47 inlines the same literal and hands it to buildWeeklyMealPlan as day indexes inside a Monday-based week. The two copies are already interpreted against different origins, so the drift is shipped, not hypothetical.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/nutrition.ts:47`
  - fails when: The training-day grid is written three times. programme.ts:9 and life-mode.ts:9 use it as day offsets from `startDate`, which is `opts.startDate ?? new Date().toISOString().slice(0,10)` - the day the user pressed Generate, never forced to a Monday. nutrition.ts:47 inlines the same literal and hands it to buildWeeklyMealPlan as day indexes inside a Monday-based week (getOrBuildMealPlan defaults weekStartsOn to mondayOf(), and mealplan.ts:39-40 iterates `day = 0..6` testing `trainingDays.has(day)`; the UI labels those Mon..Sun). A user on a 3-day programme generated on a Thursday trains Thursday, Saturday and Monday, while the meal plan puts the training-day calories (+8%, carbs around the session, a post_workout slot) on Monday, Wednesday and Friday - so they eat the surplus on two rest days and the deficit on two of their three training days, every week, and the grocery list and daily totals are built from the same wrong set. Only users who generated on a Monday are aligned.
  - fix: Export the grid once - `export const DAY_OFFSETS` in packages/core (beside the day-plan module that already owns day arithmetic) - and have programme.ts and life-mode.ts import it. For nutrition.ts, stop re-deriving the days at all: the sessions are already in the database, so compute trainingDays from them, e.g. `select scheduled_on from training_session where user_id = $1 and scheduled_on between weekStartsOn and weekStartsOn+6` mapped to a Monday-based 0..6 index. That removes the third copy and the origin mismatch in one move, and it is the only version that stays correct after a life-mode flex has moved sessions (restoreUpcoming/setLifeMode rewrite scheduled_on, which no copy of the grid knows about).
  - ruling: I traced both origins: programme.ts's offsets are relative to a free-floating startDate, nutrition.ts's indexes are relative to Monday. The drift is shipped, not hypothetical, and it is persisted into meal_plan.plan.
- **P2** `LEAN-NEW3` The Growth total is aggregated twice and the copies have drifted: the community board shows a member a lower total and sometimes a lower level than their own Progress page - `physiqueProgress` (photos.ts:81, one user) and `growthForUsers` (photos.ts:119, many users) each assemble the ten GrowthInputs for the same `growthPoints()` rule. The many-user copy hard-codes `photoSets: 0`. Both totals are shown to the same person in the same app, and `growthBoard` flags the viewer's own row with `me: true`, so a member can read two different Growth figures for themselves on two screens.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:149`
  - fails when: The ten GrowthInputs for growthPoints() are assembled twice in one file. physiqueProgress (photos.ts:81) counts body-check days into `photoSets` (line 93); growthForUsers (photos.ts:119) hard-codes `photoSets: 0` (line 149). Both totals are shown to the same person: /app/progress calls physiqueProgress, and /app/community calls growthBoard, which flags the viewer's own row `me: true` (community.ts:244). A member who has done several analysed body checks therefore reads one Growth figure and level on Progress and a lower one on Community, and the board is mis-ranked for everyone, because it sorts on a total that systematically under-counts exactly the members who use the body-check feature - the same under-counted total that appears on every match card and partner card.
  - fix: Give the two paths one input builder. The cheapest correct version is to make growthForUsers compute photoSets per user the way it already computes every other row - one grouped query, `select user_id, count(distinct taken_on) from progress_photo where user_id = any($1) and analysis is not null group by 1` - and then have physiqueProgress call growthForUsers([userId]) for the total rather than assembling its own argument object, keeping its extra per-user detail (timeline, trends, compare) local. That deletes one of the two argument lists instead of adding a third. Pin it with a test asserting the two paths return the same total for a user with analysed photos.
  - ruling: I read both argument lists and confirmed the hard-coded zero and that both paths reach the same user through two pages. One rule, two input builders, already drifted.
- **P2** `LEAN-NEW6` The 'is this member blocked from my view' predicate has three implementations, two bidirectional and one not, so a blocked member keeps seeing the blocker's posts in shared groups - One safety control is written three times in one file with three different meanings: acceptInvite checks the pair in both directions, findMatches hides both directions, and groupFeed filters only the people the viewer has blocked - so the block is one-directional on the group surface. growthBoard applies no block filter at all. There is no shared helper.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:168`
  - fails when: 'Is this member hidden from my view' is written three times in one file with three meanings. acceptInvite (community.ts:60) checks the pair in both directions; findMatches (community.ts:92-93) builds a bidirectional hide set; groupFeed (community.ts:168) selects only `block.blockedId where blockerId = userId` - one direction - and growthBoard (community.ts:239-245) consults the block table at all. So after B blocks A, B's group feed hides A but A's feed is unaffected: A still sees and can reply to every post and comment B writes in a shared group, and A and B both still see each other on the Growth board with handle and display name, on a screen whose stated promise is 'private by default'. DMs do stop, but by a fourth mechanism - blockUser sets the match to 'declined' and sendMessage requires 'accepted'.
  - fix: One helper in community.ts - `async function hiddenFor(userId: string): Promise<Set<string>>` returning both directions, which is findMatches' query verbatim - and call it from groupFeed:168, from growthBoard:243 and from findMatches:92; acceptInvite keeps its single-pair check or calls the helper and tests membership. That is one query shape in one place instead of three, and it is the dimension's 'copy-pasted security logic that should be one helper' case with the forgotten site attached. Pin it with a test asserting that after B blocks A, neither A's nor B's group feed contains the other.
  - ruling: I read all four sites and confirmed the missing `or(eq(block.blockedId, userId))` in groupFeed and the absent filter in growthBoard. Rated P2 rather than P1: growthBoard is reachable today and does leak the blocked pair to each other, but the group-feed half needs A and B in a shared group, and with COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY off listGroups returns an empty list, so that surface is hard to reach. The defect is one safety predicate in three hand-written forms with a forgotten site; nobody is told either, because MODERATION_EMAIL is unset.
- **P2** `OPS-10` The CI workflow, even once it runs, tests the dev server rather than the build it just produced, and runs no lint, dependency audit or secret scan - The workflow builds the web app and then runs the e2e suite against `pnpm dev`, so the artefact that was built is never exercised. Because the service worker is compiled only in production, the PWA and offline-replay paths - the brief's central reliability mechanism - are absent from every CI run by construction. The workflow also has no lint step, no dependency audit and no secret scan, and `pnpm -r test` silently covers no code in apps/web, which has no test script.
  - where: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:31`
  - fails when: A change to apps/web/app/sw.ts breaks the precache manifest or the kw-flush message handler. Every check that could see it runs against `next dev`, where the service worker is not built at all, so the file is permanently green in CI. The break ships, and the service worker is the only thing that replays a queued set for a user whose tab was backgrounded in a basement gym: queued writes stop flushing and, per OPS-4, get deleted. I keep this at P2 rather than P1 because the dependency-audit half of the claim is ruled separately and at P1 as OPS-NEW5.
  - fix: Run the e2e suite against `next start` on the build (set the Playwright webServer command to `pnpm --filter @kettleworth/web start` with NODE_ENV=production), and add `pnpm lint`, `pnpm audit --prod` and the secret scan as steps.
  - ruling: Verified: ci.yml:32 sets E2E_BASE_URL to the empty string, which is falsy, so playwright.config.ts:8 starts its own `pnpm dev` server - `next dev` - and next.config.ts:4 disables Serwist whenever NODE_ENV is not production, so apps/web/app/sw.ts is never compiled in CI. The step list is checkout, pnpm, node, install, two typechecks, `pnpm -r test`, migrate+seed, playwright install, build, e2e: no lint, no dependency audit, no secret scan. apps/web/package.json has no `test` script, so `pnpm -r test` covers no code in the web app.
- **P2** `OPS-NEW6` Seven route handlers bypass the shared `route()` wrapper and with it the only error channel in the app; the OAuth callback logs nothing at all and puts the error text in a URL - OPS-3 and OPS-4 are both scoped to `route()` in apps/web/lib/api.ts, but 7 of the 63 route.ts files do not import it, so they are outside both the `console.error` line and the uniform error contract. They are not the quiet ones: they are the photo upload and read (the 8 MB bytea writes that saturate Postgres first), the Mux webhook, the weekly cron, the public library search, better-auth's catch-all, and the wearable OAuth callback - which catches every failure and redirects with the message in a query string, emitting no log line whatsoever.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/integrations/[provider]/callback/route.ts:12`
  - fails when: A provider rotates something, or HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY is wrong so encryptJson throws inside completeConnect. Every attempt by every user ends at callback/route.ts:13 as a redirect to /app/connected?error=..., so the user sees a banner and the server records nothing - not a console line, not a counter, nothing for an error tracker to pick up once OPS-3 is fixed. Wearable connection is broken for everyone and the only evidence is in users' browser history and referrer headers. The photo upload has the same shape: when Postgres fills it answers 400 "Upload failed" with no log line.
  - fix: Put the logging and error-shaping in one place these handlers also go through - a small `handle()` helper for the non-session routes (webhook, cron, callback, public search) that logs with the same prefix, request id and release as `route()` will after OPS-3, and move the two photo routes onto `route()` proper since both already call `getSession()` first. Add `export const maxDuration` to photos/route.ts while there.
  - ruling: Verified by enumeration: 7 of the 63 route.ts files do not import @/lib/api - webhooks/mux, cron/weekly, library, photos, photos/[id], auth/[...all] and integrations/[provider]/callback - and there is no middleware.ts to back them. callback/route.ts:12-13 catches every failure and redirects with the message in a query string, with no console.error at all. photos/route.ts:27-28 returns 400 with the message and logs nothing. photos/[id]/route.ts and library/route.ts have no try/catch, and webhooks/mux/route.ts:17 calls JSON.parse(body) unguarded.
- **P2** `OPS-NEW7` No health or readiness endpoint and no post-deploy smoke test, so a half-configured deploy cannot be detected and no uptime check can exist - There is no /api/health, /api/status, /api/ready or equivalent anywhere in the app, and the release step is a bare deploy with nothing afterwards that asserts the deployment works. The dimension requires health checks and readiness, and this is the control that would have caught OPS-2's scenario - a build that succeeds with a missing or mistyped key and only fails when the first user reaches the encrypted write.
  - where: `docs/DEPLOY.md:18`
  - fails when: A deploy goes out with HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY set on the wrong Vercel environment. The build succeeds, the landing page renders, /library renders from the edge cache, and no step in the release procedure asks a question that would fail. The first new user to finish onboarding gets a 400 naming the config key (OPS-2). Because there is no readiness endpoint there is also nothing an uptime monitor could watch, nothing to point a future alert at, and no single call the operator can make to answer whether a deployment is actually serving.
  - fix: Add one unauthenticated `GET /api/health` that returns 200 with the release sha and 503 otherwise, after checking `select 1` against the database and that the required keys parse (reusing OPS-2's schema, so it is readiness, not liveness), with `Cache-Control: no-store`. Then add two lines to DEPLOY.md: curl it after every release, and point whatever uptime checker the owner uses at it. It is also the natural target for the first alert OPS-3 asks for.
  - ruling: There is no health, status, ready or ping route among the 63 route.ts files, and a grep for /api/health across apps/web returns nothing. DEPLOY.md ends at `vercel --prod` with no verification step, and ci.yml has no deploy or post-deploy job. The two things a deploy silently gets wrong - DATABASE_URL resolving (db/src/index.ts:12) and HEALTH_DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY being present and 32 bytes (crypto.ts:6-8) - are both checked lazily at first use, and the only unauthenticated endpoint that touches the database is /api/library, which is served with Cache-Control public, s-maxage=300 and so can answer 200 from a cache while the database is unreachable.
- **P2** `PERF-11` The Community leaderboard fetches every completed session row of up to 200 members, three times per page render - growthForUsers computes streaks in JavaScript, so it selects one row per completed session for every user in the batch with no date bound. growthBoard hands it 200 member ids, and the Community page calls growthForUsers three times for three different id sets in the same render.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:129`
  - fails when: growthForUsers computes streaks in JavaScript, so photos.ts:129 selects one row per completed session for every user in the batch with no limit and no date floor, and the status filter has no index support. growthBoard hands it up to 200 member ids (community.ts:240), and one /app/community render calls growthForUsers three times for three different id sets - growthBoard, findMatches and partners are all awaited in the same Promise.all at community/page.tsx:16. A board of 200 long-standing members is tens of thousands of heap rows read and shipped to the function per render, to draw 20 rows, and weeklyStreak is then computed twice per user over the same array (photos.ts:149-150). The work is also arbitrary: the `.limit(200)` at community.ts:240 has no ORDER BY, so the 'board' is whichever rows the heap returns first, sorted in memory and sliced to 20.
  - fix: Store the derived growth total and streak on community_profile (or a small materialised table) updated when a session completes, and have the board read it with an ORDER BY and a LIMIT; failing that, aggregate the streak in SQL rather than shipping one row per session, and add `status` to the training_session index (PERF-2).
  - ruling: Read growthForUsers, growthBoard and community/page.tsx and confirmed the three call sites, the missing limit and date bound, the unordered limit(200) and the duplicated streak computation.
- **P2** `PERF-12` OfflineWarmup adds a service-worker message listener it never removes, so each return to Today multiplies the offline replay into concurrent duplicate POSTs - The effect registers a `message` listener on navigator.serviceWorker but the cleanup removes only the window `online` listener. ServiceWorkerContainer outlives the component, so every remount leaves another live listener, and one `kw-flush` message then starts that many concurrent flush() passes over the same IndexedDB queue.
  - where: `apps/web/components/app/offline-warmup.tsx:24`
  - fails when: offline-warmup.tsx:24 registers `navigator.serviceWorker?.addEventListener("message", ...)` with an inline arrow, and the cleanup at lines 51-55 removes only the window `online` listener and cancels the idle callback - so the message listener is never removed and there is no reference to remove it with. ServiceWorkerContainer outlives the component, and the effect's dependency array is `[sessionHref, images]` while the caller builds `images` inline on every render of a force-dynamic server component (page.tsx:111), so every router.refresh() of Today leaves another live listener. flush() has no re-entrancy guard: it lists keys, then loops fetch-then-delete, so overlapping passes read the same keys and all POST every job. One kw-flush broadcast therefore starts as many concurrent passes as there are leaked listeners, each replaying the whole queue - and those land as concurrent read-modify-writes on the same exerciseInstance.loggedSets array, which is the lost-update window in CODE-15.
  - fix: Keep a reference to the message handler and remove it in the effect cleanup, and guard flush() with a module-level in-flight promise so concurrent callers share one pass.
  - ruling: Read the whole component and confirmed the asymmetric cleanup, the unstable dependency and the absent in-flight guard in flush(). This is a UI file behind the sensational-ui hook, but the defect is behavioural, not visual.
- **P2** `PERF-14` ai_log stores up to 70 KB of prompt and output text per AI call in the primary database with no retention policy - Every AI call writes the system prompt (to 20,000 characters) and the user payload (to 50,000 characters) plus the parsed output into ai_log on the same Postgres as the hot training tables, and nothing ever deletes a row or reads one back.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:62`
  - fails when: Every AI call writes the system prompt truncated at 20,000 characters, the user payload at 50,000, and the parsed output into ai_log on the same Postgres as the hot training tables (client.ts:62). The payloads are not small in production: the coach-chat row is the whole JSON context plus twelve messages of history, and the letter row is the full weekStats object. Nothing ever reads the table - a grep for `from(aiLog)` across apps and packages returns nothing - and nothing deletes from it; the only index is (task, created_at). At the brief's volumes that is gigabytes a day written into the primary database, sharing the buffer cache, the WAL, the backups and the replica with exercise_instance and training_session, and added to every dump and restore, for text nobody reads back.
  - fix: Keep the metrics and drop the text: store task, model, token counts, latency, valid and userId, and either omit system/input/output or truncate them hard (1-2 KB) behind a debug flag; add a retention delete (e.g. 30 days) to the weekly cron once it is batched.
  - ruling: I read the insert and confirmed the slice sizes, and confirmed by grep that the table is write-only. ARCHITECTURE.md already lists the unbounded ai_log as a gate-1 problem, so this is auditing against the agreed design point, not re-litigating it. Keep the metrics, drop or hard-truncate the text, and add retention.
- **P2** `PERF-NEW3` Three separate queries read the account's entire completed-session history on one Today render, each only to produce a count and recompute the same streak - Two of the stages in the Today render each select every completed training_session row the account has ever had, with no limit and no date bound, and a third does the same on Progress. The results are used for `sessions.length` and for weeklyStreak over the date list - both of which SQL can answer - and the streak is then recomputed in JavaScript once per query.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/progress.ts:11`
  - fails when: Three stages read the account's entire completed-session history with no limit and no date bound, to produce a count and a streak. progress.ts:11 selects every completed training_session row and uses it only for `sessions.length` and `weeklyStreak(sessions.map(s => s.scheduledOn))`; quests.ts:38 selects the same set again for weeklyStreak alone; photos.ts:89 repeats it a third time on Progress. The first two are both inside one Today render, so an account training four times a week for two years ships its ~400-row session list twice and computes the streak twice, plus the whole bodyMeasurement history at progress.ts:17, for two integers and one streak number - and the status filter has no index support, so each is a bitmap scan plus a heap filter.
  - fix: Return the count with `count(*)` and bound the streak query to the window weeklyStreak actually needs (it cannot extend a streak across a gap, so the last ~60 weeks is sufficient); compute the streak once per request and pass it, rather than once per query.
  - ruling: Read all three queries and their only consumers and confirmed there is no limit, no date bound and no aggregate. Same shape as PERF-4 in different files, which is why it survived the first pass; a count(*) and a bounded window answer both numbers.
- **P2** `PERF-NEW4` getActiveProgramme runs four times in one Today render, two queries each, and performs writes - there is no request-scoped memoisation anywhere in the API package - Four of the Today page's stages each call getActiveProgramme independently, and each call is at least two statements (the programme select plus the planned-session probe), so roughly eight of the auditor's measured 39 queries are the same lookup repeated. getActiveProgramme is also not a pure read: it promotes the queued programme with two UPDATEs, so the repetition puts four copies of a write-capable function in one render, two of them concurrent inside a Promise.all.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:69`
  - fails when: getActiveProgramme (programme.ts:68-79) is at least two statements - the programme select plus a planned-session probe - and is called four times independently in one Today render: adaptation.ts:36 via runWeeklyAdaptation (page.tsx:29), adaptation.ts:93 via currentWeekState (page.tsx:30), programme.ts:114 via diversifyProgramme (page.tsx:33) and page.tsx:35 directly. A grep for `cache(` across packages/api returns nothing, so nothing dedupes them. It is also not a pure read: when the current block has no planned sessions left it promotes the queued programme with two UPDATEs, so on that boundary render four unsynchronised copies - two of them concurrent inside the Promise.all - race to flip the same two programme rows between 'completed' and 'active', with no lock and no transaction.
  - fix: Wrap getActiveProgramme (and getProfile, which has the same shape) in React `cache()` for the render path so the lookup is resolved once per request, and move the queued-programme promotion out of the getter into the cron or an explicit call so a read path stops writing.
  - ruling: I read the function, confirmed the two writes, confirmed all four call sites reach one render and confirmed no React cache() is used anywhere in packages/api. The read path performing a write is the part worth fixing first.
- **P2** `SCALE-11` GDPR export issues one query per training session (plus a discarded throwaway query) and builds the whole document in memory - The export fetches exercise instances with a sequential query per session id, after a first query whose result is thrown away, and accumulates every row plus every decrypted health sample into one in-memory JSON string.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/account.ts:23`
  - fails when: A three-year member with a connected wearable has hundreds of sessions and tens of thousands of health samples. The export makes one sequential round trip per session for instances alone, plus one wasted query, and holds every row, every decrypted raw wearable payload and a pretty-printed copy of all of it in the function's heap at once. Several years and several providers push that string into the hundreds of megabytes and the request into the function's memory and duration limits, at which point a user cannot exercise a legal right and the only signal is a generic 400 from api.ts:27.
  - fix: Replace the loop with one `inArray(exerciseInstance.sessionId, sessionIds)` query (chunked at a few thousand ids), drop the discarded first query, and stream the JSON instead of stringifying it in memory. The export's field names and shape stay exactly as they are.
  - ruling: account.ts:23 is exactly as quoted: a first `select ... where sessionId = sessionIds[0]` whose result is discarded by `.then()`, followed by a loop issuing one round trip per session id, and the response is built as one pretty-printed string in apps/web/app/api/account/export/route.ts:4 including every decrypted health sample.
- **P2** `SCALE-12` Photo uploads fall back to the local filesystem whenever VERCEL is unset, so any multi-container deploy serves 500s for photos stored on another instance - Storage is chosen from the host rather than from configuration: unless `PHOTO_STORAGE=db` or `process.env.VERCEL` is set, bytes are written to a local directory and later read back from that same directory by path. Two instances behind a load balancer do not share that directory, so a read on the instance that did not receive the upload fails.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:23`
  - fails when: ARCHITECTURE.md gate 5 requires the app to run on a plain VM under Docker Compose. Run two web containers there without PHOTO_STORAGE=db: a lifter's three photos land on container A's filesystem with progress_photo.data null, the next GET /api/photos/<id> is balanced to container B, readFile throws ENOENT and the photo is permanently unreadable from half the fleet. deleteAllPhotos on account deletion then removes only the copy on whichever container served the delete, so a GDPR hard delete leaves photographs behind.
  - fix: Decide storage from configuration only (`PHOTO_STORAGE=db|s3|disk`, defaulting to `db`), drop the `process.env.VERCEL` host check from business logic, and treat the local-disk branch as a single-process development convenience that refuses to start when more than one instance is configured. The S3/R2 branch ARCHITECTURE.md:336 plans belongs behind the same three functions.
  - ruling: photos.ts:13 chooses storage from the host rather than from configuration - `PHOTO_STORAGE === "db" || (!!process.env.VERCEL && !process.env.STORAGE_DIR)` - writes to a local directory at :23 when that is false and reads it back by path at :35, and deleteAllPhotos at :44 only rm's that directory. Setting STORAGE_DIR on Vercel also flips it to false and sends 8 MB uploads to an ephemeral lambda disk. Today's deploy sets PHOTO_STORAGE=db per DEPLOY.md:13, which is why this is P2 and not higher.
- **P2** `SCALE-13` ai_log stores the full prompt and output for every AI call with no retention and no user index, so it grows without bound and cannot answer a per-user spend question cheaply - Every AI call writes a row holding up to 20 KB of system prompt and 50 KB of user input plus the output JSON. Nothing prunes, partitions or archives the table, and its only index is on (task, created_at), so a per-account spend lookup - the query a spend cap needs - has no index to use.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:62`
  - fails when: At the stated volumes the table reaches hundreds of gigabytes a year inside the same Postgres that serves the session player, inflating backups, the pg_dump exit path and the cost per user, and eventually pushing the hot tables out of cache. Then, when the AI spend cap lands, the question it must answer cheaply - how much has this account spent this month - becomes a predicate on user_id and created_at against a table of tens of millions of rows with no matching index, i.e. a sequential scan on the path that is supposed to refuse an abusive request quickly. The index half of this overlaps OPS-5; the retention and growth half is its own fix.
  - fix: Keep the full prompt for a short window and then reduce rows to task, model, token counts and validity (a scheduled delete/update in the batch worker SCALE-1 introduces), and stop writing the full system prompt, which is a constant per task. An index on (user_id, created_at) is the companion fix but needs a migration, so it is an escalation for this run.
  - ruling: ai/client.ts:62 writes up to 20,000 characters of system prompt and 50,000 characters of user input plus the output JSON per call; meta.ts:18 gives the table one index, on (task, created_at), and user_id carries neither index nor foreign key. No retention, prune or partition code exists anywhere in packages or apps. ARCHITECTURE.md:51 prices the table at about 600 GB a year as found and lists retention as not started.
- **P2** `SCALE-14` Photo rows are selected with no column list, pulling the up-to-8 MB bytea through the connection for operations that never touch the bytes - `deletePhoto` and `applyAnalysis` use `select()` with no projection against `progress_photo`, whose `data` column holds the whole image when photos live in Postgres (the deployed configuration). Each call therefore transfers up to 8 MB over the pooled connection and buffers it in the function, to read a storage key or a JSON column.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:38`
  - fails when: A lifter deletes three 8 MB progress photographs from Settings. Each DELETE moves its full 8 MB row out of Postgres, across the pooler, into the function's heap, and discards it - 24 MB of database egress and function memory to execute three row deletes - and applying a body-photo analysis does the same. At scale that multiplies Neon egress and holds a pooled connection for the transfer, and a delete-all is the most likely place for an out-of-memory kill.
  - fix: Give both queries an explicit projection - `select({ storageKey, data: sql\`data is not null\` })` for the delete and `select({ analysis })` for the apply - reusing the existing `cols` pattern.
  - ruling: deletePhoto (photos.ts:38) and applyAnalysis (photos.ts:65) both use `select()` with no projection against progress_photo, whose data column holds the whole image up to MAX_BYTES = 8 MB in the deployed configuration, and the only fields either function reads are storageKey, a null test on data, and analysis. The same file already defines the `cols` projection at :27 and uses it in listPhotos.
- **P2** `SCALE-15` The first wearable sync is started and not awaited after the OAuth callback responds, so the work dies with the instance and leaves sync_job rows running forever - `completeConnect` fires `syncProvider` without awaiting it and then returns, so the provider fetch, decrypt, chunked insert and status update continue after the HTTP response. A serverless instance is free to freeze or terminate at that point, cutting the work off at an arbitrary statement and leaving the `sync_job` row it created stuck in `running` with nothing to reap it.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:38`
  - fails when: A user connects Whoop. The callback redirects and the platform is free to freeze or terminate the instance a moment later, because no request is in flight. The sync is cut at an arbitrary statement - say after the refresh wrote new credentials but before lastSyncAt was set - and the sync_job row stays `running` for ever. Over time the table accumulates one permanently-running row per interrupted connect, so any future status UI or alert built on sync_job reads a fleet of phantom jobs, while the user sees a connected provider with no data and no error.
  - fix: Either await the first sync inside the callback (it is already a redirect the user waits on) or enqueue it as a job the batch worker from SCALE-1 drains; in both cases stamp `sync_job` with a heartbeat or expiry so an interrupted run can be identified and retried.
  - ruling: integrations.ts:38 is `syncProvider(userId, p.info.id).catch(e => console.warn(...))` with no await, immediately before `return { userId }`, and the callback route then redirects - so the provider fetch, token refresh, chunked encrypted insert and status update all continue after the HTTP response has been sent. syncProvider has already inserted a `status: "running"` sync_job row at :66 and nothing reaps it.
- **P2** `SCALE-16` One connection string serves every read and write, with no replica routing and no statement or idle timeout on the pool - The app has a single `DATABASE_URL` and a single memoised pool of 10 connections per instance, with no read/write split and no timeouts. ARCHITECTURE.md's gate-1 claim that Postgres 'meets it at 8 vCPU with a replica' therefore cannot be realised without a code change, and one slow statement can hold a pooled connection for the life of the instance.
  - where: `packages/db/src/index.ts:13`
  - fails when: One statement with no server-side timeout - the audit_log sequential scan in SCALE-8 is the obvious candidate - occupies one of an instance's ten connections until it finishes. Ten such requests on one instance exhaust its pool and every later request on that instance queues behind them, so a single slow query becomes an instance-wide stall rather than ten slow responses. With idle connections never closed, every warm instance holds ten client connections against the pooled endpoint whether or not it is serving traffic. The read-replica half of this finding is an architecture question for stack-architect; the missing timeouts and the single pool are the part that is fixable here.
  - fix: Add `statement_timeout`, `idle_timeout` and `connect_timeout` to the `postgres()` options so a slow query cannot hold a connection, and introduce a `dbRead()` seam alongside `db()` reading an optional `DATABASE_REPLICA_URL` (falling back to the primary) so reads can be moved without rewriting callers. Which reads are safe on a replica is a per-call decision: anything following a write in the same request needs the primary.
  - ruling: db/src/index.ts:13 is `postgres(url, { max: 10, prepare: false })` behind a module-scope `let _db`, with one DATABASE_URL, no read/write split, no statement_timeout, no idle_timeout and no connect_timeout. The read-replica assumption in ARCHITECTURE.md's gate 1 has no seam in the code to be realised through.
- **P2** `SCALE-17` Joining or leaving a group recounts the whole membership and writes the denormalised count back to a single hot row - Every join and leave runs `count(*)` over that group's membership and then updates the group row's `memberCount`. The count is O(members) per join and the update serialises all concurrent joins on one row.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:162`
  - fails when: Groups are user-created and public. Each further join scans the whole membership to recount and then takes a row lock on the single group row, so a burst of joins after a group is shared serialises on that row; and because the count and the write are separate statements, two interleaved joins can both write the same stale total, leaving memberCount permanently short of reality - which is also the column listGroups orders by.
  - fix: Apply the delta instead of recounting - `update "group" set member_count = member_count + 1 where id = ?` in the same transaction as the insert (and `- 1` on delete, guarded by whether the insert/delete actually affected a row) - which is both O(1) and atomic.
  - ruling: community.ts:162-163: joinGroup inserts or deletes the membership row, then runs `count(*)` over the whole group membership and writes the result into group.memberCount in a separate statement, with no transaction and no atomic increment. Both joinGroup and createGroup are reachable by any signed-in account through /api/community/groups and /api/community/groups/join, neither behind discoveryEnabled().
- **P2** `SCALE-5` syncSessionRecord checks for a personal_record row then inserts, with no unique constraint, so a concurrent replay creates two record rows for one session - The function's contract is one `personal_record` row per exercise per session, but it enforces it with a SELECT followed by an INSERT in a separate statement, and `personal_record` has only a non-unique index on (user_id, exercise_id). Two concurrent `logSet` calls both see no row and both insert.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:303`
  - fails when: The duplicate flush in SCALE-3 replays a PR-setting set twice in parallel. Both executions reach the select, both find no row, both insert. Progress then reports two records for one performance and growthPoints counts PRs, so the user's Growth total and their place on the community board are inflated by a race any user can trigger by logging a PR on a flaky connection. P2 rather than P1 because nothing is lost and rebuildPersonalRecords (session.ts:315) repairs it idempotently.
  - fix: Run the record sync inside the same transaction and row lock as SCALE-4's fix, so the two executions serialise on the `exercise_instance` row. (A unique index on (user_id, exercise_id, session_id) plus `onConflictDoUpdate` would be the durable fix but needs a migration, which is out of scope for this run.)
  - ruling: syncSessionRecord at session.ts:303 selects for an existing personal_record by (userId, exerciseId, sessionId) and then inserts in a separate statement, and the only index on the table is `CREATE INDEX "personal_record_user_ex_idx" ON "personal_record" ("user_id","exercise_id")` (0000_init.sql:543) - non-unique and not including session_id. The doc comment at :293-297 asserts one row per exercise per session.
- **P2** `SCALE-NEW3` No per-request memoisation in the service layer: one Today render repeats getProfile at least five times and getActiveProgramme at least four, and getActiveProgramme writes on that read path - The service functions take a userId and go straight to the database with no request-scoped cache, so a single Today render issues the same profile query (plus an AES-GCM decrypt) five or more times and the same active-programme lookup four or more times. getActiveProgramme is not even read-only: it performs a check-then-act status promotion, so a read path writes, and two concurrent renders race on it. The pattern to fix this is already in the codebase and was applied to exactly one function.
  - where: `apps/web/app/app/page.tsx:29`
  - fails when: Every Today render pays five identical profile selects and five AES-GCM decrypts plus eight or more queries for the same active programme, all serial because they sit inside sequential awaits at page.tsx:27-40 - on the order of twenty avoidable round trips per render against the primary the brief already says saturates first. Separately, because a read path writes, two tabs loading Today at the moment a programme runs out both execute the promotion; the writes are idempotent today, but a read path that writes forecloses both the read-replica routing SCALE-16 calls for and any caching of the page.
  - fix: Wrap the read-only service entry points that take only a userId in React `cache()` (or thread the already-loaded `rec`/`prog` through, which several of these functions already accept as arguments), and split the status promotion in getActiveProgramme out of the read into an explicit call made by the writers, so the read path stops writing.
  - ruling: cache() is used once in the codebase, for the session (apps/web/lib/session.ts:7), and no service function is wrapped. I traced one Today render: getProfile is called at page.tsx:27, adaptation.ts:37, programme.ts:119, quests.ts:70 and nutrition.ts:43, each a profile select plus an AES-GCM decrypt of the sensitive column; getActiveProgramme at adaptation.ts:36, programme.ts:114, nutrition.ts:46 and page.tsx:35. getActiveProgramme is not read-only - programme.ts:68-78 is a check-then-act status promotion with two writes.
- **P2** `SCALE-NEW8` AI route handlers set no maxDuration while the Anthropic client is given a 120 s timeout and two retries, so the platform kills the invocation mid-call and the spend is never logged - The Anthropic client allows up to 120 s per attempt with `maxRetries: 2`, i.e. roughly six minutes of wall clock, but only one of the 63 route files declares a maxDuration. Every other AI entry point inherits the platform default, which is far shorter, so the function is terminated while the request to Anthropic is in flight: the tokens are spent, the ai_log row at client.ts:62 is never written, and the user gets a hard failure instead of the deterministic fallback the module was designed around.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:14`
  - fails when: A member uploads three photographs and taps the body read. The handler base64-encodes the images and calls Anthropic with a 120-second client timeout; the platform kills the invocation first. The request fails with no JSON body, body-check.tsx:38's r.ok check shows a generic error instead of the deterministic fallback the module was designed around, and because client.ts:62 never executed there is no ai_log row at all - so the one record that would show those tokens were billed does not exist. The retry budget can never be used, so a transient provider 529 is a user-visible failure rather than a retried call, and AI spend is systematically under-recorded in exactly the slowest, most expensive cases, which is the opposite of what a spend cap needs.
  - fix: Set an explicit maxDuration on every route that can reach `structured()`, and set the client timeout below it (not above) so the SDK's own timeout fires first and the catch at client.ts:58-60 runs, letting the deterministic fallback and the ai_log write happen. Reduce maxRetries to what fits inside the budget, or move the retry to the queue the letter fan-out needs.
  - ruling: ai/client.ts:14 configures the client with timeout 120_000 and maxRetries 2, and the ai_log insert that records the spend is the statement after the awaited call, inside the same invocation (client.ts:43-49 then :61-62). A grep for maxDuration over apps/web/app returns exactly one hit out of 63 route files, so coach/chat, photos/[id]/analyse, onboarding/followup, programme/generate and cron/weekly all inherit the platform default with no bound reconciled against that client budget.
- **P2** `SCALE-NEW9` Programme generation holds one interactive transaction across ~300 sequential statements, and any account can trigger it repeatedly with no cap - generateAndSaveProgramme opens a transaction and then inserts row by row inside it - one statement per week, one per session, one batch per session's exercises - so a 24-week, 7-day plan runs roughly 300 sequential round trips with the transaction open the whole time. It is reachable by any signed-in account through POST /api/programme/generate with no cap, and each call writes a fresh full programme into the brief's largest table.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:37`
  - fails when: Ten members tap "generate a 24-week programme" in the same minute, which is ordinary after a launch or a push notification. Each request holds one transaction open across hundreds of sequential round trips, so with a pool of ten per instance one instance is wholly occupied and every other request on it queues behind them, while behind a transaction pooler those ten hold ten server-side connections for the duration. The hostile version is worse: an account loops the endpoint with {"weeks":24} and, with no rate limit anywhere, writes roughly 170 training_session and about a thousand exercise_instance rows per call into the table the brief sizes at 500M rows a year.
  - fix: Batch the inserts - build all weeks, then all sessions, then all exercise instances as three multi-row inserts using returned ids - so the transaction is three statements instead of three hundred, and add a per-account rate limit (and a programme count cap) to the route.
  - ruling: programme.ts:37-66 opens `db().transaction` and then inserts row by row inside it - one mesocycle, one week, one trainingSession per session with .returning(), one exerciseInstance batch per session - each its own awaited round trip, with the transaction open throughout. The request controls the size: programme/generate/route.ts:4 accepts weeks up to 24 and DAY_OFFSETS allows up to 7 sessions a week. The pool is max 10 with no statement timeout, and nothing caps programmes per account.
- **P2** `SEC-1` Cron authentication is optional: with CRON_SECRET unset, any stranger can trigger the whole-estate AI letter fan-out and mutate every user's week - The only guard on /api/cron/weekly is skipped entirely when CRON_SECRET is absent from the environment, so a missing variable silently turns a privileged all-users job into an unauthenticated public endpoint. The working-tree .env, which mirrors the production variable set, does not define CRON_SECRET.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:9`
  - fails when: The defect is the shape, not a live hole: one dashboard deletion of CRON_SECRET, or a value saved blank, turns the whole-estate AI-and-email fan-out into an unauthenticated GET with no code change and no alert. A guard written as if (secret) check cannot distinguish 'misconfigured' from 'open to the internet'; it should refuse to run without the secret.
  - fix: Fail closed: `if (!secret || req.headers.get("authorization") !== `Bearer ${secret}`) return 401;` and compare with a constant-time compare. Fix the tautological WHERE (`isNotNull(profile.onboardingCompletedAt)`) so the job only fans out to onboarded users.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/cron/weekly/route.ts:8-9 is a configuration-dependent guard: const secret = process.env.CRON_SECRET; if (secret && ...) return 401. An empty or absent value removes the check and the handler has no session check of its own. But the P0 premise does not hold on what ships: I listed the production environment myself (vercel env ls production, project basil-s-projects11/kettleworth) and CRON_SECRET is one of its nine variables, so the guard is active today and an unauthenticated GET gets a 401. The auditor's inference from apps/web/.env is invalid - that file is a localhost dev file (BETTER_AUTH_URL=http://localhost:3100). I deliberately did not probe the live endpoint, because if the stored value were empty the probe itself would run the fan-out.
- **P2** `SEC-10` Group reads and writes check neither membership nor group.visibility, so any authenticated user with a group id can read its feed, join it and comment in it - createPost is the only group operation that verifies membership. groupFeed returns a group's posts and comments to any caller, joinGroup inserts a membership into any group regardless of its visibility column, and addComment and toggleReaction act on any post id with no membership or visibility check at all.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:165`
  - fails when: A member joins a group, learns its id, then leaves (or is removed). They keep full read access to the group's feed and comments through GET /api/community/groups?id=<id>, can re-join with a single request, and can comment into it with no membership check at all. The schema carries a visibility column with a 'private' value that createGroup never sets, so the day a private group exists it is readable and joinable by any account that has the id.
  - fix: Add one membership-and-visibility helper and call it at the top of groupFeed, joinGroup, addComment and toggleReaction (resolving the post's group for the latter two), the way createPost already does.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/community.ts: groupFeed (:165-174) selects the group by id and returns 50 posts with comments and reaction counts, computing joined only for display and never reading g.visibility; joinGroup (:159) inserts a membership for any group id with no visibility check; addComment (:182) and toggleReaction (:183) act on any post id with no lookup of the post's group at all. createPost (:176) is the only one that gates on membership. The routes expose all four to any session with only z.string().uuid() in between (apps/web/app/api/community/groups/route.ts:4, groups/join/route.ts:4, comments/route.ts:4, reactions/route.ts:4).
- **P2** `SEC-11` entitlements() has no call sites, so there is no tier authorisation anywhere and BILLING_ENFORCED controls nothing - The function documented as "the single gate to flip" is exported but never called by any route handler or service, so the premium/free distinction is not enforced on any path and setting BILLING_ENFORCED=true would change no behaviour.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/entitlements.ts:6`
  - fails when: The function documented as 'the single gate to flip' is dead code. Setting BILLING_ENFORCED=true would change no behaviour at all: every free account would keep unlimited coach chat, photo reads, meal-photo estimates and programme generation, because nothing consults it. The same absence is why there is nowhere to hang a per-tier cap, which is the seam the AI findings need.
  - fix: Call entitlements() in apps/web/lib/api.ts and give route() an optional required-entitlement argument, so the gate is enforced once at the choke point rather than remembered per handler.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/entitlements.ts:6 is the only definition and the only other hit in the tree is the barrel re-export at packages/api/src/index.ts:15 - no route handler and no service calls it. BILLING_ENFORCED appears only at entitlements.ts:9 and is absent from the production environment. apps/web/lib/api.ts stops at session plus zod, so there is no tier check on any path.
- **P2** `SEC-3` Wearable OAuth callback is unauthenticated and never checks the session against the state owner, so an attacker can attach a victim's provider account to the attacker's account - completeConnect derives the account to link purely from the `state` value presented in the callback URL and never compares it with the session of the browser completing the flow; the callback route itself has no session check at all. The state therefore authorises linking rather than binding the flow to the user who started it.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:30`
  - fails when: Latent account-linking CSRF: the state value authorises the link instead of binding it to the session that started the flow. The day any provider's client id and secret are added to the dashboard - a config change with no code change, and the connect UI already ships - an attacker can start a connect flow on their own account, send the authorize URL to a victim, and have the victim's wearable data sync into the attacker's account.
