  - fix: Require a session on the callback and reject when `session.user.id !== state.userId`; in completeConnect take the authenticated userId as an argument and compare it with the stored one before inserting.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:33 takes userId from the state row and :36 writes the provider credentials against it; apps/web/app/api/integrations/[provider]/callback/route.ts has no getSession and does not use route(), so nothing binds the flow to the browser that completes it. The attack needs step 1 though, and step 1 cannot run on what ships: beginConnect refuses at :19 unless p.configured(), every adapter's configured() is an env check on CLIENT_ID/CLIENT_SECRET (adapters/oura.ts:10, whoop.ts:10, strava.ts:7, fitbit.ts:8, polar.ts:8, garmin.ts:9; device.ts:7 is hardcoded false), and the nine production variables contain no provider credential. With no verification row ever written, completeConnect throws 'Connection request expired' at :31 before any code exchange.
- **P2** `SEC-6` better-auth 1.7.4 carries a critical account-takeover advisory whose only unmet precondition is a social provider, which is one environment variable away - The shipped better-auth is 1.7.4, affected by GHSA-965c-763c-88jm (CVE-2026-114607, CVSS 9.1): an OAuth state value can be redeemed as a magic-link token to sign in as any user whose email address the attacker knows. Two of the three preconditions are already true in this configuration; the third (at least one social or generic OAuth provider enabled) is false in production today only because GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID and APPLE_CLIENT_ID happen to be unset.
  - where: `packages/api/src/auth.ts:26`
  - fails when: Known-vulnerable dependency with the abuse path closed by a single absent environment variable rather than by code. Adding GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID and GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET is a product decision that needs no code change (the sign-in UI already renders the button when they are set), and at that moment the finding becomes the P1 the auditor described: an attacker who knows a member's email address redeems an OAuth state value at the magic-link verify endpoint and gets that member's session. Upgrade before any social provider is enabled.
  - fix: Upgrade better-auth to >=1.7.7 and re-pin the lockfile. Until then, set `account: { storeStateStrategy: "cookie" }` (the advisory's stated workaround) so enabling a social provider cannot open the path.
  - ruling: better-auth is installed at 1.7.4 (node_modules/.pnpm/better-auth@1.7.4...) and two of the advisory's three stated preconditions are true in this configuration: packages/api/src/auth.ts:26 enables magicLink with no storeToken option, and :19 configures the drizzle adapter, which is what selects the database state strategy. The third is false on what ships - :7-9 build socialProviders only when a client id and secret are present, and the production environment contains no GOOGLE_* or APPLE_* variable, which I confirmed against the dashboard rather than inferring it. I cannot verify the advisory text or its patched version from this checkout, only the version installed and the configuration around it.
- **P2** `SEC-8` trustedOrigins includes https://*.vercel.app, so any attacker-deployed Vercel site is a trusted origin and a valid post-sign-in redirect target - The wildcard entry `https://*.vercel.app` is matched with a glob whose `*` crosses dots, so every hostname under vercel.app, including one an attacker creates in minutes, passes better-auth's origin check and its callbackURL/redirectTo validation.
  - where: `packages/api/src/auth.ts:18`
  - fails when: An attacker deploys a look-alike page at some-name.vercel.app in minutes and sends a victim a sign-in request against the genuine site with callbackURL pointing at it. The origin check and the callback validation both pass, so after a successful sign-in on the real domain the browser is handed to the attacker's page, which continues the flow and asks for whatever it likes. The session cookie itself does not travel (sameSite lax, httpOnly, secure in production), which is why this is a phishing-continuity and trust-boundary defect rather than a takeover.
  - fix: Replace the wildcard with the exact hostnames that must work (the canonical URL plus any named preview alias), or narrow it to a single-label pattern that cannot match an unrelated project. Preview deployments can be added from VERCEL_BRANCH_URL at build time instead.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/auth.ts:18 includes the literal "https://*.vercel.app" in trustedOrigins. I checked the matcher in the installed package rather than assuming: trusted-origins.mjs routes a pattern containing * and :// to wildcardMatch(pattern)(getOrigin(url)), and wildcard.mjs:26 sets wildcard = "[^/\\\\]" for the default separator, so * matches any run of non-slash characters - dots included. Every hostname under vercel.app is therefore a trusted origin.
- **P2** `SEC-9` Mux webhook skips signature verification when the secret is unset, and never checks the timestamp, so it accepts unauthenticated writes to exercise_video - Verification is wrapped in `if (secret)`, so with MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET absent any unauthenticated POST is trusted; the body is then JSON.parse'd with no schema and its fields written straight into exercise_video. Even with the secret present the extracted `t=` timestamp is never compared against now, so a captured signed request replays indefinitely.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/webhooks/mux/route.ts:10`
  - fails when: Anyone on the internet POSTs {"type":"video.asset.ready","data":{"id":"<asset id>","playback_ids":[{"id":"<their own>"}]}} with no signature. The handler writes status ready, isPlaceholder false and thumbnail/preview URLs built from the attacker's string into the matching row, so the public library and the session player present media the attacker controls, or a broken tile, to every user. Reachability is limited by needing a real asset id to match on, which keeps this below the unauthenticated-write blockers.
  - fix: Return 401 when the secret is unset, reject a `t` older than about five minutes, and parse the body with a zod schema before touching the database.
  - ruling: apps/web/app/api/webhooks/mux/route.ts:10 wraps the whole verification in if (secret), so with MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET absent the signature check is skipped entirely; the t= timestamp is parsed at :12 and fed into the HMAC at :14 but never compared with now, so even a signed request replays for ever; :17 JSON.parses the body with no schema and :20 writes status, playbackId, durationSeconds, thumbnailUrl, previewGifUrl and isPlaceholder into whichever exercise_video row matches. MUX_WEBHOOK_SECRET is not among the nine variables in the production environment, which I checked at the dashboard, so the fail-open branch is the live one.
- **P2** `SEC-NEW5` Field-level encryption covers fewer paths than the brief and the code claim: the same injury and medical free text is stored in plaintext in profile.intake_transcript and profile.ai_summary beside the AES-GCM profile.sensitive - upsertProfile goes to the trouble of splitting injuries, medicalFlags and notes out of the profile and encrypting them into profile.sensitive, and in the same object literal writes the intake transcript - the conversation in which the user typed those very things - into a plaintext jsonb column. completeOnboarding then writes an AI-generated prose summary of the full profile, injuries included, into the plaintext ai_summary column. So the control the brief credits ("Field-level AES-256-GCM encryption exists for health samples and OAuth tokens") and that split() implements is defeated by two sibling columns in the same table, and a database-level compromise or an unredacted dump reads the sensitive fields without a key.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/profile.ts:31`
  - fails when: The intake asks about injuries and medication conversationally, so a user types 'herniated disc L4/L5, and I am on beta blockers' into the chat. The structured copy lands in profile.sensitive under AES-256-GCM, exactly as the privacy policy says; the sentence itself lands verbatim in profile.intake_transcript in clear, in the same row, and a sentence naming the injury regions lands in profile.ai_summary in clear. Anyone with a read of that table - a leaked connection string, a restored snapshot, a support query - reads the special-category text the encryption exists to protect, and the published claim that these fields are encrypted is not true of them.
  - fix: Encrypt profile.intake_transcript and profile.ai_summary with the same encryptJson/encryptField helpers at packages/api/src/services/profile.ts:31 and :40, decrypting in getProfile beside the existing `decryptJson<Sensitive>(row.sensitive)` at :21. Both columns are already nullable text/jsonb, so this needs no migration - the brief's ban on schema changes is not in the way. Then state in the architecture record which columns the control actually covers, so the next audit can check it.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/profile.ts goes to the trouble of splitting injuries, medicalFlags and notes out (:10-13) and encrypting them into profile.sensitive (:31 encryptJson), and in the same object literal writes intakeTranscript - the conversation in which the user typed those very things - into a plaintext jsonb column. completeOnboarding (:40) then writes a model-generated prose summary of the whole profile into the plaintext ai_summary column. So the field-level encryption control covers one copy of the data and not the other two.
- **P2** `SEC-NEW6` POST /api/community/partner bypasses both the COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY flag and the block list, and growthBoard hands out the user ids that make it usable against strangers - requestPartner inserts a match row against any user id supplied in the request body with no discovery check and no block check, while every neighbouring function has one or both. The file's own header comment says the stranger-facing half is "behind a flag that is off unless an operator turns it on", and this route is the hole in that flag. growthBoard, also not behind the flag, returns the raw userId of every member whose community profile is not private, which supplies the ids.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:101`
  - fails when: A stranger signs up, opens /app/community and reads real user ids out of the growth board and challenge board payloads, then loops POST /api/community/partner over them. Each victim gets an unsolicited 'X wants to train with you' request from an account they have never heard of - the exact stranger-to-stranger contact COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY is off to prevent - and because there is no block check, a user who has blocked that account before any match row existed receives the request anyway.
  - fix: Add the two checks requestPartner is missing, at packages/api/src/services/community.ts:101: refuse when `!discoveryEnabled()` unless an accepted invite already links the pair, and refuse when a block row exists in either direction, reusing the lookup acceptInvite already performs at :60. Put growthBoard behind the same discovery gate as findMatches, or stop returning `userId` from it and key the board on handle.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/community.ts:101-105 requestPartner inserts a match row against any user id in the request body with no discovery check and no block check, while its neighbours have one or both (findMatches:88 gates on discoveryEnabled and filters blocks, acceptInvite:60 checks blocks). apps/web/app/api/community/partner/route.ts:4 takes a bare z.string() userId from any session. The ids are obtainable from the same screen: growthBoard (:239-243) returns the raw userId of every non-private community profile and is not behind the flag, and the challenge boards do the same for anyone who joined a challenge.
- **P2** `SECRET-3` A committed script creates accounts on the production site with a password hardcoded in the repository - A tracked script points at the live production URL and signs accounts up there with a fixed password that is in the repository, and the same password is in the tracked e2e spec. The brief confirms stray `@kettleworth.test` accounts are still on the production database, so a password committed to the repo authenticates real accounts on a live, health-data-holding system.
  - where: `apps/web/live-multi.mjs:5`
  - fails when: Anyone who reads the repository knows the password for every account these scripts have created on the production site. They still need an address, and the local part is a millisecond timestamp (the e2e spec adds a random suffix), so it has to leak rather than be guessed - a pasted terminal screenshot, a CI log, a support thread. When one does, that person signs in to a real account on a live system that holds health data and can call every uncapped AI endpoint as a legitimate user.
  - fix: Read the password from an env var with no default (`process.env.E2E_PASSWORD`) or generate a random one per run in both files, drop the hardcoded production URL in live-multi.mjs:3 in favour of a required `E2E_BASE_URL`, and run the existing packages/api/scripts/purge-test-accounts.ts --yes against production to remove the accounts the committed password still opens.
  - ruling: apps/web/live-multi.mjs is tracked (git ls-files confirms it; apps/web/test-results is not). Line 3 hardcodes const B = "https://kettleworth.vercel.app" with no env override and no guard on the target, :4 mints multi-check-${Date.now()}@kettleworth.test, :5 fixes the password, and :23-27 drive sign-up against the live site. The same literal password is at apps/web/e2e/core-flow.spec.ts:9. Password sign-in is enabled (auth.ts:20). The brief's third known-broken item - stray @kettleworth.test accounts on the production database - is this script's output.
- **P2** `SECRET-4` The shared route handler returns the raw exception message to the client, which discloses the production database hostname and username - The helper that wraps 56 of the 63 route handlers serialises `e.message` into the JSON response body. Driver-level failures put infrastructure identifiers in that message: the Neon endpoint hostname on a DNS failure and the database role name on an authentication failure.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/api.ts:27`
  - fails when: Any unhandled database error on any of the wrapped handlers hands the caller the statement that failed and the values bound into it - user ids, and for an upsert of provider credentials the stored ciphertext. A signed-up account can provoke one cheaply (a unique-constraint collision on a community handle is enough) and read back the product's table and column layout from its own browser network tab, which is reconnaissance the application should never volunteer.
  - fix: Return a generic message plus a correlation id for anything that is not a deliberately user-facing error: keep `msg` only for the errors the app throws on purpose (the `needs_confirmation` style codes already special-cased at line 25), and send `{ error: "Something went wrong", id }` for everything else while the detail goes to `console.error` as it already does.
  - ruling: apps/web/lib/api.ts:23 takes e.message from any thrown error and :27 returns it to the caller as the 400 body, with no error id and no allowlist, for everything except the needs_confirmation code. The mechanism needs correcting though, and I checked it in the installed driver rather than taking either account: drizzle 0.45.2 wraps every query failure, and errors.js:10-19 builds the message as `Failed query: ${query}\nparams: ${params}`, which pg-core/session.js:41 throws. So what reaches the client is the SQL text and the bound parameter values, not the Neon hostname the finding traced.
- **P2** `TEST-2` The e2e step cannot pass in CI even once pnpm is fixed: E2E_BASE_URL="" makes every page.goto an invalid URL - CI sets `E2E_BASE_URL: ""` and the config falls back with `??`, which does not treat an empty string as absent, so `baseURL` is `""` and every relative navigation in the only end-to-end suite throws before the first assertion. This is the second lock on the same door as TEST-1: fixing the pnpm conflict alone leaves CI red.
  - where: `apps/web/playwright.config.ts:6`
  - fails when: Someone removes the pnpm version input to fix OPS-1 and pushes. The job now reaches the e2e step and all six cases - three tests across the chromium and mobile projects - fail at their first navigation with an invalid-URL error that has nothing to do with the product. CI stays red for a reason nobody can act on, and the cheapest way to make it green is to delete the only test that covers sign-up, intake, programme generation and set logging.
  - fix: Remove the `E2E_BASE_URL: ""` override from ci.yml:32 (unset is what the config wants), or make the config treat blank as absent: `process.env.E2E_BASE_URL || "http://localhost:3000"`. Fix both so neither spelling can break it again.
  - ruling: ci.yml:32 sets E2E_BASE_URL to the empty string and playwright.config.ts:6 uses `??`, which does not treat "" as absent, so baseURL is "" while :8's webServer still starts because "" is falsy. Every test in the only e2e spec opens with a relative goto (core-flow.spec.ts:43, 52, 59), and `new URL("/sign-up", "")` is an invalid URL. retries: 1 in CI then doubles a guaranteed failure.
- **P2** `TEST-7` No AI is covered by any test, and no provider mock exists, so dimension 3's cap cannot be tested without spending money - All 23 LLM call sites funnel through one function with no test, no fake and no fixture, and every environment that runs tests has the key unset, so even the e2e exercises only the deterministic fallbacks. 09-tests requires every cap to have a test at the limit and past it with the provider mocked; none of that harness exists on main.
  - where: `packages/api/src/ai/client.ts:36`
  - fails when: A change to the parse path - res.parsed_output becoming a getter that throws, or zodOutputFormat moving - makes structured() throw instead of returning null. Coach chat, intake follow-ups, the body-photo read, the meal estimate and the Sunday letter all stop falling back to deterministic copy and surface as 400s through api.ts:27. Nothing in the suite notices, because no test has ever taken the branch where a provider replies, and the per-account AI cap this run must add has no mocked provider to be tested against at the limit or past it.
  - fix: Inject the client (or stub `getClient()`) so packages/api can test `structured()` with a fake transport: one test for a valid parse, one for a refusal stop_reason, one for an APIError, each asserting null-or-output and one ai_log row. Merge the cap tests that already exist on branch ai-policy-and-cron-batching (packages/api/src/ai/policy.test.ts, 70 lines, asserts allow at 599/2,999,999 and refuse at the cap) rather than writing them again.
  - ruling: client.ts:36 is the single funnel for all AI call sites, with `if (!aiAvailable()) return null` at :37 and the documented contract that it returns null on any failure so callers fall back. No test file mentions msw, nock, fetch or supertest, so no fake provider exists, and aiAvailable() is false in CI (the workflow sets no ANTHROPIC_API_KEY) - so the branch at client.ts:43-57, where a provider actually replies, has never been executed by any test in any tier.
- **P2** `TEST-8` The offline replay path has no test at all; its idempotency is a comment and its 400 handling deletes the user's set - Queued sets replayed from IndexedDB are the brief's second threat actor, and the queue has no test in any tier - apps/web has no test task, and the e2e never goes offline. The module's own header asserts replays are idempotent, and its flush treats a 400 as success and drops the job.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/offline-queue.ts:22`
  - fails when: A user logs twelve sets in a basement gym. Connectivity returns and flush() replays them. A LoggedSet field tightened in packages/types since those sets were queued makes route() reject one body with 400 at api.ts:15; line 22 counts it as sent, deletes the job, and the set is gone with no error shown and no dead letter. The mirror case is just as open: a change to logSet that appended instead of replacing by setNumber would double every replayed set's volume into the weekly adaptation, and the only statement that this must not happen is a comment.
  - fix: Test flush() against a stubbed fetch: same job replayed twice asserts one effect; a 400 keeps or dead-letters the job rather than deleting it; an offline navigator leaves the queue untouched. Pair it with a packages/api test calling logSet twice with the same setNumber and asserting one logged set and one estimated_max row.
  - ruling: The replay path has no test in any tier: apps/web has no test script and the e2e never goes offline. Its stated invariant is a comment (offline-queue.ts:4) and its flush treats 400 as success and deletes the job (:22), against a server-side merge that is a read-modify-write with no transaction (session.ts:146, :149) and an `unchanged` guard at :158-159 that is correct today and asserted nowhere.
- **P2** `TEST-N1` The suite's green status was manufactured four days ago: --passWithNoTests was added to silence a failing root `pnpm test` - `pnpm test` did not pass on 23 Nov 2026 - it failed because a package had no test files - and the commit that 'fixed' it added `--passWithNoTests` to two packages rather than adding a test. One of those two (packages/integrations) already had a test file, so the flag there does nothing except guarantee that deleting the only test of the six-provider wearable layer leaves the suite green.
  - where: `packages/api/package.json:12`
  - fails when: A developer refactors resolveSamples and deletes or renames packages/integrations/src/resolve.test.ts - the only test covering multi-provider sample resolution and dedup. `pnpm test` prints "No test files found, exiting with code 0" and the run is green, so the package holding the OAuth exchange, the expiry check and six provider adapters now has zero tests and nothing says so. The same flag hides the reverse mistake: a test written at packages/api/test/policy.test.ts rather than under src/ matches neither vitest config's include and the task still exits 0 - which is where the AI cap's test would naturally be put.
  - fix: Drop `--passWithNoTests` from both packages/api and packages/integrations so an empty suite is red, and seed packages/api with the tests TEST-3/TEST-4/TEST-5 call for (the unmerged branch already ships packages/api/vitest.config.ts and a 70-line policy.test.ts). If a package genuinely has no tests yet, leave it with no `test` script rather than a passing one - `pnpm -r test` and `turbo run test` both skip a missing script, so the root suite stays green without a false green per package.
  - ruling: I checked the history. `git show f92bfe6 -- packages/api/package.json packages/integrations/package.json` changes `"test": "vitest run"` to `"test": "vitest run --passWithNoTests"` in both packages in one commit of 23 Nov 2026, and `git cat-file -e f92bfe6^:packages/integrations/src/resolve.test.ts` succeeds, so integrations already had a test file and was not one of the packages that was failing. The flag there buys nothing except making a deletion invisible.
- **P2** `TEST-N2` `pnpm test` can report a cached pass it never ran: turbo hashes nothing from packages/db, which the case-study suite consumes - The root suite is `turbo run test` with `"test": { "dependsOn": ["^build"] }` and no `inputs`, so @kettleworth/core#test is keyed on core's own files plus its declared dependencies (@kettleworth/config, @kettleworth/types). packages/core/src/programme/case-study.test.ts reads two files out of packages/db, which core does not depend on, so a change to either leaves the hash unchanged and turbo replays a cached PASS without running the six case studies that exercise the engine against the real 855-exercise library.
  - where: `turbo.json:10`
  - fails when: Someone edits packages/db/seed/map-exercise.ts - the mapper that decides a movement's equipment, pattern and contraindicated regions - and loosens contraindications so a severe-shoulder user can be prescribed an overhead press. `pnpm test` is the only gate that runs at all, because CI has never run (OPS-1), and it replays a cached PASS for @kettleworth/core: the assertion written to catch exactly this, case-study.test.ts's exclusionReason check against the real library, is never executed. The developer sees green for the change the suite exists to catch.
  - fix: Declare the dependency the test actually has: add `"inputs": ["$TURBO_DEFAULT$", "../db/seed/**"]` to the `test` task (or give @kettleworth/core a workspace devDependency on @kettleworth/db so `dependsOn: ["^build"]` picks it up), and move the seed fixture and mapper behind an exported entry point rather than a `../../../` reach across package boundaries.
  - ruling: I ran the dry run myself. turbo.json:10 declares `"test": { "dependsOn": ["^build"] }` with no inputs and cache enabled, and `turbo run test --dry=json` reports @kettleworth/core#test with 48 inputs, none containing db or a parent path, and dependencies of only @kettleworth/config#build and @kettleworth/types#build. Yet case-study.test.ts:18 reads ../../../db/seed/data/free-exercise-db.json and :20 imports from ../../../db/seed/map-exercise, and packages/core/package.json lists only @kettleworth/types and zod.
- **P2** `TEST-N4` The only test of the critical journey is skipped, not failed, whenever an assertion about landing-page copy breaks - core-flow.spec.ts configures serial mode at file scope with three top-level tests. In Playwright serial mode a failure in one test skips the rest of the group, so the sign-up -> intake -> programme -> set-logging journey - the only end-to-end coverage the product has - is conditional on two assertions about landing and library copy that a UI change is expected to break.
  - where: `apps/web/e2e/core-flow.spec.ts:11`
  - fails when: This run's own brief allows the hero to be recomposed through the sensational-ui skill. The H1 is reworded and the hero table becomes a different element. Test 1 fails; Playwright marks tests 2 and 3 skipped. The report reads one failed, two skipped, and the product's only end-to-end coverage of sign-up, intake, programme generation, set logging, the rest timer and the sound setting silently did not run. The red is attributed to a copy change, someone updates the string, and nobody notices the journey was never exercised. A flake in test 1 does the same.
  - fix: Serial mode is there for the shared account, which only test 3 uses. Put test 3 in its own `test.describe.serial` block (or drop serial entirely, since the two public-page tests share no state), so a landing-copy failure cannot skip the journey. Separately, assert structure and role rather than hero copy in tests 1 and 2 - `getByRole("link", { name: "Start free" })` already does; `toContainText("Your first block")` does not.
  - ruling: core-flow.spec.ts:11 is `test.describe.configure({ mode: "serial" })` at file scope with the three tests declared at file scope, so they form one serial group in declaration order: landing copy (:42), library copy (:51), then the journey (:57). In Playwright serial mode a failure in one test skips the rest of the group, and the first two assert copy and markup - a level-1 heading containing "Your first block", a visible table, an href - rather than behaviour.
- **P2** `TEST-N6` The only rate limit in the codebase is untested, and its storage defaults to per-instance memory - `rateLimit: { enabled: true, window: 60, max: 30 }` is the single limit anywhere in the product, and 09-tests requires every cap to have a test at the limit and past it. There is none - packages/api has no test files - and because `storage` is left unset better-auth defaults it to `"memory"`, so the limit is per lambda instance on Vercel. A test at the limit is also the only thing that would have surfaced that default.
  - where: `packages/api/src/auth.ts:24`
  - fails when: A typo turning max: 30 into max: 3000, a future change to better-auth's defaults, or the per-instance memory behaviour itself are all equally invisible, because nothing in the suite ever sends a 31st request. The same gap means the per-account AI cap this run must add has no precedent to copy and no test tier it can live in - packages/api would run such a test only if it were placed under src/, and would exit 0 if it were not. The per-instance storage defect itself is ruled at SCALE-7; what is rated here is the untested cap.
  - fix: With a test task in packages/api (TEST-3), add a test that drives `auth.api` 30 times and asserts the 31st is refused, and a second that asserts the configured storage is not `"memory"` once a shared store is wired. The per-instance limiter itself is dimension 2/3's finding - escalate it there rather than fixing it here - but the test belongs in this dimension and is what makes the fix verifiable.
  - ruling: auth.ts:24 is the only cap in the product and nothing tests it: packages/api has no test file and its script passes with --passWithNoTests, so the config is a literal no test reads. I also confirmed the storage default in the installed better-auth 1.7.4 (create-context.mjs:175 falls back to "memory" with no secondaryStorage), which is what a test at the limit would have surfaced.
- **P2** `TEST-N7` The single-use OAuth state is the only guard on an unauthenticated callback that attaches a wearable account, and no test asserts it refuses anything - `/api/integrations/[provider]/callback` takes no session (it cannot - the provider redirects there) and hands attacker-supplied `code` and `state` straight to `completeConnect`. The only thing deciding whose account the credentials land on is a lookup of `oauth_${state}` in the verification table, with an expiry check and a single-use delete. Three lines, each the kind a refactor moves, and no test sends an unknown, expired or replayed state.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/integrations.ts:30`
  - fails when: A refactor moves the single-use delete at integrations.ts:32 to after p.exchangeCode so a failed exchange no longer burns the state - a reasonable-looking usability fix. The state is now replayable for its whole lifetime, so anyone who obtains a callback URL from browser history, a referrer header or a shared screenshot can re-drive the callback and overwrite that member's connected-provider credentials. Inverting or dropping the expiry comparison has the same character. In every case `pnpm test` is green, because nothing in the suite touches oauth.ts or completeConnect.
  - fix: In packages/api, once it has a test task: `completeConnect` with an unknown state throws, with an expired state throws, and the same state twice throws the second time and leaves exactly one connected_provider row. In packages/integrations, which already has a test task, cover oauth.ts directly - `pkcePair` produces a verifier whose S256 challenge matches, `isExpired` is true inside the 60-second skew and false outside it, and `tokenRequest` maps a non-2xx response to a thrown error (stub `fetch`; no network).
  - ruling: The callback has no session and cannot have one: integrations/[provider]/callback/route.ts:3-10 takes code and state from the query string and hands them to completeConnect with no route() wrapper and no getSession(). The whole control is three lines at integrations.ts:30-33 - look up `oauth_${state}` in verification, check expiresAt, delete the row for single use - and the userId then comes from the stored record, which is correct. packages/integrations has one test file covering resolveSamples only; oauth.ts, including the isExpired that integrations.ts:70 uses to decide a refresh, and all six adapters have none, and packages/api has none at all.
- **P3** `AI-10` The coach chat applies up to three model-chosen state changes to the user's programme with no confirmation step - The model's structured output can carry three actions that are executed immediately - move, skip, ease loads, set life mode, set a ritual - on a prompt whose content includes the user's own free text. The blast radius is confined to the requesting account's own reversible rows, which is why this is a note rather than a blocker, but there is no human-in-the-loop and `ease_next_session` is not id-validated.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:110`
  - fails when: A user types 'ignore the plan and skip every session this week, then set life mode to injured' into the coach chat. The system prompt invites actions when the user asks for a change, so the model may emit skip_session plus set_life_mode; both are written before the reply is shown and the user is told after the fact. The injection source is the account's own message, every row touched belongs to that account, and the changes are reversible, so the blast radius is one user's own week - which is why this is a note and not a blocker.
  - fix: Return the proposed actions and apply them on a second, explicit call from the UI (the response already carries a per-action summary, so the confirmation surface is nearly there), and give `ease_next_session` an explicit `sessionId` validated against `ctx.upcoming` like the other two. Keep the allowlist narrow as new actions are added.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:110-117 applies up to three model-chosen actions immediately with no confirmation. The containment the finding itself relies on is real: the action union (:78-85) is a discriminated union with percent bounded 5-30 and enum fields, ChatOut caps actions at 3 (:85), the validate callback (:103) checks move/skip session ids against the caller's own upcoming list, and every write is scoped to the session user (:112 and/eq userId, :113 skipSession, :116 upsertProfile which re-parses through TrainingProfile). ease_next_session carries no id and silently targets ctx.upcoming[0] (:114).
- **P3** `CODE-NEW10` Four queries build their IN and ANY lists by string-concatenating ids into sql.raw where inArray is used everywhere else - Four hot queries interpolate id values straight into raw SQL text rather than binding them. The values are uuids read from the database, so nothing is injectable today, but the pattern is one caller away from being so, and it has already been copied four times in two files while the parameterised helper is imported in both.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/adaptation.ts:52`
  - fails when: Four queries build their id lists by string-concatenating values into `sql.raw` with hand-added quotes: adaptation.ts:52 and :58 (`in (...)`) and coach.ts:24 and :28 (`= any(array[...])`). Nothing is escaped and nothing is bound. I traced every caller: all four lists come from `select id from training_session`, i.e. uuid columns, so no quote can appear and there is no injection today. The cost is the next copy - the codebase already has handlers that take id arrays straight from a request body - and that every call ships a distinct statement text, so Postgres plans each one afresh on paths that run per user in the letter cron and per message in the coach chat.
  - fix: Replace all four with `inArray(...)`. The empty-list guards already present (`ids.length ? ... : []`) can stay or be dropped, since Drizzle handles an empty inArray.
  - ruling: Read all four sites and the values reaching them. `inArray` is imported and used for the identical shape in the same package (session.ts:40, session.ts:57, photos.ts:129-134), so this is four copies of a pattern with no reachable abuse: P3 hardening by the rules, not a vulnerability.
- **P3** `CODE-NEW11` deleteAllPhotos deletes no photos in the production storage mode, and the GDPR delete only works because of a cascade nobody states - The function named deleteAllPhotos removes a filesystem directory and nothing else. In the production configuration photo bytes live in Postgres, not on disk, so the call deletes nothing at all; the hard delete still succeeds, but only because the user row cascades, which no comment or test records. The name promises more than the code does on the one path where that matters.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:43`
  - fails when: deleteAllPhotos (photos.ts:43-45) only removes a filesystem directory. In the production storage mode - `inDb()` is true when PHOTO_STORAGE=db or VERCEL is set without STORAGE_DIR (photos.ts:13) - nothing was ever written to disk, the bytes live in progress_photo.data, and the rm therefore deletes nothing. The GDPR hard delete still removes the rows, but only because progress_photo.userId cascades from user.id, which no comment and no test records; its one caller swallows errors (`await deleteAllPhotos(userId).catch(() => {})`, account.ts:32). The defect becomes real on the next change: a 'delete all my progress photos' control, a retention job, or any caller that wants the images gone without deleting the account will call the function with that exact name and get a silent no-op in production.
  - fix: Make it do what it is called: `await db().delete(progressPhoto).where(eq(progressPhoto.userId, userId))` after the rm, and drop the `.catch(() => {})` at account.ts:32 so a failure on the GDPR path is loud. Add a line to the hard-delete comment naming the cascade as the mechanism of record.
  - ruling: I verified the cascade in packages/db/src/schema/profile.ts:40 before accepting that today's hard delete is complete, and that deletePhoto (photos.ts:40-41) handles both storage modes correctly - so the author knew about both. No data survives today, hence P3 rather than a GDPR finding.
- **P3** `CODE-NEW5` findMatches loads every non-private community profile joined to its training profile with no limit, on a page render - The matching query selects all community profiles that are not private, inner-joined to profile.data, with no LIMIT and no filter beyond visibility, and ranks them in application memory. It is bounded only by how many accounts have made a community profile.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:94`
  - fails when: findMatches (community.ts:94) selects every community_profile that is not private, inner-joined to the full profile.data JSONB, with no LIMIT and no selective predicate, and ranks the whole array in Node before the limit of 8 is applied. With discovery on and 1M registered users, even a small opt-in rate pulls tens of thousands of rows plus their training-profile documents into one invocation on every /app/community render. listGroups (community.ts:148) has the same unbounded shape.
  - fix: Push the ranking into SQL or at least pre-filter in the query - same country or city, same level band, trainTogether - with an index on (visibility, country, city) and a hard LIMIT (a few hundred) before rankMatches ever sees a row. Cap listGroups the same way with a LIMIT and keyset pagination.
  - ruling: Read the query and confirmed there is no limit and that the same file caps growthBoard at 200. Rated P3 because the path dies at a flag: discoveryEnabled() reads COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY, .env.example ships it `off`, and community.ts:88 returns early - so there is no demonstrated path today. It is a hardening-and-before-you-flip-it item, and the flag must not be turned on until the candidate set is bounded and indexed.
- **P3** `LEAN-9` No client-side request module: the JSON POST shape is spelled out 32 times across 17 files, so the 401 policy exists in only one of them - `{ method, headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify(...) }` is written out 32 times in 17 files, with no `lib/client.ts` to own it. Response handling is therefore invented per site: some check `!r.ok` and toast, some `.catch(() => null)`, some ignore the result. Only `postResilient` (apps/web/lib/offline-queue.ts:37) knows what a 401 means.
  - where: `apps/web/components/settings/settings-view.tsx:62`
  - fails when: `headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" }` is written out 32 times across 17 files with no client module to own it, so response handling is invented per site: some check `!r.ok` and toast, some `.catch(() => null)`, some ignore the result. Only postResilient knows what a 401 means. When a session cookie expires in a standing PWA tab, settings-view.tsx:62 shows 'Couldn't save' for ever with no sign-in prompt, and 29 of the 32 sites behave the same way.
  - fix: Add `apps/web/lib/client.ts` with one `api(path, {method, body})` that sets the header, stringifies, parses `{ error }` on failure, and on 401 redirects to /sign-in; replace the 32 call sites with it. Roughly 20 lines added against ~32 lines of repeated option objects, and the 401 behaviour becomes one decision instead of 32.
  - ruling: I counted the occurrences myself (32 across apps/web) and read three of the divergent policies. Every handler already returns the same `{ error }` shape from one place, so the contract is centralisable. Readability and a consistent 401 policy, no demonstrated data defect: P3.
- **P3** `LEAN-NEW7` The app nav and the marketing header are the same component written twice: eleven duplicated blocks, already drifted, with the shared hook sitting right beside them - apps/web/components/app/nav.tsx and apps/web/components/marketing/site-header.tsx repeat eleven blocks of six or more identical code lines - the escape-and-scroll-lock effect, the floating pill wrapper, the brand pill and the sheet scaffolding - and the copies have already diverged. The project already shares the hard part of this component through apps/web/lib/use-hide-on-scroll.ts, so the seam exists and only the duplicated half was left behind.
  - where: `apps/web/components/marketing/site-header.tsx:36`
  - fails when: apps/web/components/app/nav.tsx and apps/web/components/marketing/site-header.tsx are the same component written twice - the Escape-and-scroll-lock effect, the floating pill wrapper, the brand pill and the sheet scaffolding - and the copies have already drifted by a flex gap in the wrapper className, while the hard part (useHideOnScroll) is already extracted into apps/web/lib. The next change to either header lands in whichever file the author opened, and the landing page and the app then disagree about the same chrome.
  - fix: Extract the two mechanical parts next to the hook that is already extracted: `useScrollLockAndEscape(open, setOpen)` in apps/web/lib (beside use-hide-on-scroll.ts), and one `<FloatingHeaderShell hidden>{...}</FloatingHeaderShell>` wrapper component for the translate-on-hide container and the brand pill, with the href, label and right-hand slot as props. Roughly 40 duplicated lines become one hook and one shell. Both files are UI files behind the sensational-ui hook, so per the brief this is reported, not fixed in this run, even though extracting a hook changes no markup, no tokens and no motion.
  - ruling: Maintenance cost with no runtime defect, which is the P3 readability case. Both files are UI and are gated by the sensational-ui hook, so per the brief this is reported, not fixed in this run - even though extracting a hook changes no markup, tokens or motion.
- **P3** `LEAN-NEW8` A dead re-export in the api barrel gives one core function two import paths and ships a helper nobody imports - `export { mondayOf as coachMondayOf, isoWeek };` is the last line of coach.ts. Nothing imports coachMondayOf anywhere in the repository, and isoWeek is re-exported out of @kettleworth/api although it belongs to @kettleworth/core, so the same function now has two public import paths and the api package appears to own a date helper it does not.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:123`
  - fails when: coach.ts:123 is `export { mondayOf as coachMondayOf, isoWeek };`, reached by `export * from "./services/coach"` in the api barrel. Nothing in the source imports coachMondayOf, and isoWeek belongs to @kettleworth/core - which coach.ts itself imports it from on line 4 - so one function now has two public import paths and the api package appears to own a date helper it does not. The cost is a wrong signal: the next author looking for where the week-start rule lives finds an exported coachMondayOf in the api barrel and imports it from there.
  - fix: Delete coach.ts:123. When the shared date module LEAN-2 asks for lands, mondayOf moves there and both coach.ts and nutrition.ts import it; nothing should re-export a core function through the api package.
  - ruling: A grep over source for coachMondayOf returns only this line (it appears in .next build output, which is generated from it). Documentation and dead-export debt with no runtime consequence: P3.
- **P3** `OPS-11` No pinned toolchain: three different Node majors across the places this is built, and nothing in the repository fixes the production one - `engines.node` is `>=22` with no upper bound, and there is no `.nvmrc`, `.tool-versions` or `.node-version` in the repository. CI pins Node 22 in the workflow, this machine runs 24.11.1, and the version Vercel builds with is a project setting in a dashboard that is not in the repo. A green build therefore does not demonstrate that the production build works on the runtime that produces it.
  - where: `package.json:6`
  - fails when: A contributor working on Node 24 (this machine) introduces code or a dependency build that behaves differently on Node 22; CI pins 22 but has never run (OPS-1), and the major the production build uses is a Vercel dashboard setting that appears nowhere in the repository. The first place the three runtimes are reconciled is the production build itself, so the first evidence of a mismatch is a failed deploy or a runtime error inside a route. Nothing in the repository can answer which major production builds on.
  - fix: Add `.nvmrc` with the exact major, narrow `engines.node` to that major range, and set the same version in the Vercel project settings so all three build locations agree.
  - ruling: The facts hold: package.json:5-7 is `engines: { node: ">=22" }` with no upper bound, no .nvmrc/.tool-versions/.node-version exists, ci.yml pins node-version 22, this machine runs v24.11.1, and apps/web/vercel.json carries only framework, crons and regions. I set P3 rather than P2 because nothing demonstrates a path from the unpinned major to a failure - no dependency is shown to behave differently, and the production major is a dashboard setting nobody has read. It is hardening with no demonstrated path.
- **P3** `OPS-12` Two branches, including the one holding the AI spend policy, exist only on this laptop - `origin` holds only `main` and `kw-weight`, both at the same commit. The branches `ai-policy-and-cron-batching` - which the brief identifies as the only place `packages/api/src/ai/policy.ts` exists, the fix for the run's largest cost exposure - and `revamp/app-surfaces` have never been pushed. The work that closes the AI-cost gate lives on one unbacked machine, and no one else can review, build or deploy it.
  - where: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:2`
  - fails when: The laptop is lost, reimaged, or this worktree is cleaned up. packages/api/src/ai/policy.ts and the batched cron go with it, and the fix for ARCHITECTURE.md gate 3's cost finding has to be written again from the record. Until then nobody else can review, build or take over that work, because there is nothing on the remote to fetch.
  - fix: Push both branches to origin.
  - ruling: `git ls-remote --heads origin` returns only refs/heads/main and refs/heads/kw-weight, both at 003fe0bb; `git branch -a` shows local-only ai-policy-and-cron-batching and revamp/app-surfaces with no remote tracking refs. The work that closes the AI-cost gate exists on one machine. There is no consequence for the running system, which is why this is a note rather than a condition.
- **P3** `OPS-13` No dangerous-operation list, and `db:push` sits beside `db:migrate` in the root scripts while the runbook has the operator holding a production URL in that shell - Nothing in the repository lists which commands can destroy data, who may run them, or which of them are in the deploy path. `pnpm db:push` - drizzle-kit's schema-diff apply, which emits DROP statements when the live database differs from the TypeScript schema - is one line below `db:migrate` in the root scripts, and DEPLOY.md step 5 instructs the operator to run a migrate command with the production `DATABASE_URL` in that same shell.
  - where: `package.json:17`
  - fails when: The operator is in the shell from DEPLOY.md step 5 with the Neon URL exported and shell history full of db: commands, at the normal moment when the TypeScript schema is ahead of the deployed migrations because a migration is being written. They reach for history and run pnpm db:push against production. strict: true prompts, so the only thing between a health-data database with no backup the project owns (OPS-7) and a destructive diff is a yes/no in a terminal, with nothing written down to say that command must never be pointed at production.
  - fix: A short "Dangerous operations" section in DEPLOY.md naming `db:push`, `db:seed` and `purge-test-accounts` with who may run each and against which database, and remove `db:push` from the root scripts so it is only reachable inside packages/db.
  - ruling: package.json:16-19 puts db:push one line below db:migrate with no annotation, DEPLOY.md:16-17 has the operator run a migrate command with the production DATABASE_URL in their own shell, and a grep for dangerous|destructive|do not run over docs/, README.md and package.json finds nothing. The mitigation the finding itself names is real: drizzle.config.ts:9 sets strict: true, so push prompts before applying, and against an undrifted database the diff is empty - which is why this stays P3.
- **P3** `OPS-NEW10` The turbo build task does not declare the service-worker artefacts as outputs, so a cache-hit build can produce a tree with no service worker - `turbo.json`'s build task declares `outputs` as `.next/**`, `!.next/cache/**` and `dist/**`. The production build also writes `apps/web/public/sw.js` and `apps/web/public/swe-worker-*.js` (both gitignored, so they exist only as build output), and neither is declared. A cache hit restores `.next` and does not restore them, so `pnpm build` can report success and leave the PWA - the brief's central offline mechanism - without its service worker. `globalEnv` is also narrower than the build's real inputs: it lists five variables while the app reads 50-odd, among them the other two `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` keys that are inlined into the client bundle.
  - where: `turbo.json:6`
  - fails when: Someone self-hosts per ARCHITECTURE.md's weekend test, or a CI job caches .turbo: pnpm build hits the cache, restores .next, and never regenerates apps/web/public/sw.js, which is not in the checkout because it is gitignored. next start then serves 404 for /sw.js, registration fails, and the background-sync kw-flush listener is never installed - so queued sets flush only while the app is open, which is the opposite of what the offline mechanism is for. The build was green. The globalEnv half bites later: once PostHog is wired behind NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY, setting that key will not invalidate the build hash, so a cached bundle ships without it and observability looks configured while collecting nothing.
  - fix: Add `public/sw.js` and `public/swe-worker-*.js` to the build task's `outputs`, and list the variables the build actually depends on - at minimum every `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` key - in `globalEnv` or in a per-task `env`. Vercel builds apps/web directly with `next build` and does not go through turbo, which is why this has not bitten production yet; it is the self-host and CI-cache paths that are exposed, and ARCHITECTURE.md's exit plan depends on one of them.
  - ruling: turbo.json:6 declares build outputs as .next/**, !.next/cache/** and dist/** only, while next.config.ts:4 points Serwist's swDest at public/sw.js, and .gitignore:13-14 ignores apps/web/public/sw.js and public/swe-worker-*.js - so those artefacts exist only as build output and are not in the declared output set. turbo.json:4's globalEnv lists five variables while .env.example lists more than forty, among them the two NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_* keys that would be inlined into the client bundle.
- **P3** `OPS-NEW8` The runbook's function-timeout model is wrong in both directions, and the one route that sets maxDuration sets it below its own Anthropic client timeout - DEPLOY.md tells the operator that functions run "up to 10 s by default" and that AI calls use a 60 s timeout "set in the route config where needed". Both halves are wrong. Vercel's Hobby default and maximum are both 300 s with fluid compute, which is on by default, so the stated default is off by a factor of thirty and the one route that does set `maxDuration = 60` is *lowering* its ceiling, not raising it - and 60 s is less than the Anthropic client's own single-attempt timeout of 120 s with two retries, so that route is guaranteed to be killed before its retry budget is spent.
  - where: `docs/DEPLOY.md:26`
  - fails when: A user photographs a meal on hotel wifi. POST /api/nutrition/estimate calls Claude; the first attempt is slow, as vision calls are. The platform kills the function at its 60 s ceiling before the SDK's own 120 s timeout expires, so the retry logic that exists is never reached and the deterministic fallback the module was designed around never runs - the user gets a platform error instead. I rate this P3 because what I can settle from the repository is documentation accuracy plus one misconfigured ceiling; the claim that the platform default is 300 s rather than 10 s depends on the Vercel project's fluid-compute setting, which is not in the repository, and the functional consequence for the unbounded AI routes is ruled at P2 as SCALE-NEW8.
  - fix: Correct DEPLOY.md:26 to the platform's actual Hobby numbers, and make the duration explicit per route rather than implicit: raise nutrition/estimate above the client's worst case or lower the client's timeout and retries to fit inside the function, and give the cron and the photo upload an `export const maxDuration` chosen on purpose. `vercel inspect <url> --json` reports each function's real `timeout`, and that number belongs in the runbook rather than a remembered one.
  - ruling: The in-repository half checks out and is the part I rule on: `grep maxDuration` over apps/web/app returns exactly one hit, nutrition/estimate/route.ts:4 `export const maxDuration = 60`, against ai/client.ts:14 `new Anthropic({ timeout: 120_000, maxRetries: 2 })` - so the only route in the app with an explicit duration is bounded below its own client's single-attempt timeout and can never spend its retry budget, while the cron that genuinely needs a bound (SCALE-1) has none. DEPLOY.md:26's "up to 10 s by default" is inconsistent with that same sentence's claim that 60 s is set "where needed", since it is set on one of five AI-reaching routes.
- **P3** `OPS-NEW9` `pnpm lint` lints one package out of eight: no workspace package has a lint script, so adding the missing CI step would still cover nothing but apps/web - The root `lint` script is `turbo run lint`, and `apps/web` is the only workspace with a `lint` script at all. Turbo silently skips a package with no matching task, so `pnpm lint` has never looked at packages/core, packages/api, packages/db, packages/integrations, packages/types, packages/ui or packages/config - which is where the programme engine, the services, every LLM call site and all the encryption live. OPS-10's fix of "add `pnpm lint` as a step" therefore buys less than it appears to.
  - where: `package.json:11`
  - fails when: OPS-10's fix lands and pnpm lint is added to CI, so the workflow reports a lint gate. A floating promise, an unused await or a swallowed rejection is then introduced in packages/api/src/services - the code that writes health rows and calls Claude - and the gate passes, because turbo ran the task in apps/web and silently skipped the other seven packages. The team now believes a control exists over the code it matters most for.
  - fix: Add a shared eslint configuration at the repository root and a `lint` script to each workspace package that points at it, then add `pnpm lint` to the workflow as OPS-10 asks. Until the packages have the script, the CI step should be `eslint .` from the root over the whole workspace rather than `turbo run lint`, so nothing is silently skipped.
  - ruling: Verified per package: package.json:11 is `turbo run lint`, and apps/web/package.json:9 is the only lint script in the workspace - packages/api, config, core, db, integrations, types and ui have none, and turbo skips a package with no matching task. By contrast typecheck is defined in seven of eight packages, which is why ci.yml:25's packages typecheck does cover them. So the gap is specific to lint and no eslint config exists at the repository root.
- **P3** `PERF-13` The database pool is a hard-coded 10 connections per instance with no idle timeout, and nothing in the code requires the pooled Neon endpoint the deployment depends on - createDb opens up to 10 connections per serverless instance with no idle_timeout, max_lifetime or connect_timeout, and the whole deployment's safety rests on DATABASE_URL happening to be Neon's pooled endpoint - a convention that lives only in a deploy document.
  - where: `packages/db/src/index.ts:13`
  - fails when: packages/db/src/index.ts:13 is `postgres(url, { max: 10, prepare: false })` - a hard-coded ten connections per serverless instance, not configurable by environment, with no idle_timeout, max_lifetime or connect_timeout passed - and the whole deployment's safety rests on DATABASE_URL happening to be Neon's pooled endpoint, a requirement that appears only in docs/DEPLOY.md and is asserted nowhere in code or CI. If that one environment variable is ever set to the direct endpoint, the compute's own max_connections is exhausted after a dozen warm instances and from then on every request on every new instance fails at connect, all at once rather than gradually, with nothing at startup to catch it.
  - fix: Add `idle_timeout: 20, max_lifetime: 60 * 30, connect_timeout: 10` and lower `max` to match what one instance can actually use concurrently; fail fast at startup if the host does not look like a pooled endpoint (or read the pool size from an env var with a safe default).
  - ruling: Read the pool construction and confirmed `prepare: false` is correct for a transaction-mode pooler and that no timeouts or env-driven sizing are passed. Rated P3, not the P2 claimed: behind the documented pooled endpoint the connection count is survivable at the design point, so this is defence in depth with no demonstrated path - a startup assertion on the endpoint and explicit timeouts, not a live defect.
- **P3** `PERF-15` Programme generation performs about 110 serial round trips inside one transaction, each insert waiting for the previous one's RETURNING - generateAndSaveProgramme writes a week, then each of that week's sessions one at a time with `.returning()` so it can use the id for the exercise rows, holding one pool connection in an open transaction for the whole run.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/programme.ts:52`
  - fails when: generateAndSaveProgramme's transaction (programme.ts:49-57) inserts each mesocycle, then each week, then each session one at a time with `.returning()` so the id can be used for that session's exercise rows - so a 12-week, 4-day plan is on the order of a hundred serial round trips, every one waiting on the previous RETURNING, with one of the instance's ten pool connections held open throughout. The user waits for it at the end of onboarding and again on every 'build next block', and a handful of concurrent generations on one warm instance exhausts that instance's pool so every other request on it queues behind them.
  - fix: Generate the ids client-side (uuid in JS, as progress_photo already does at photos.ts:20) so weeks, sessions and instances can each be inserted in one multi-row statement - three statements instead of ~110, with no change to the rows written.
  - ruling: Read the nested insert loops and confirmed every level takes .returning() before the next. Rated P3: the cost is bounded by the plan size, the path is user-initiated and rare, and the fix (client-side uuids, as progress_photo already does at photos.ts:20, then three multi-row inserts) changes no rows.
- **P3** `PERF-NEW5` The whole exercise library is re-read unprojected into every instance every five minutes, on whichever user request happens to trigger it - libraryForEngine caches the library in a module-level variable with a five-minute TTL, so every serverless instance re-reads all 855 exercise rows plus the video table every five minutes for the life of the deployment, and the read is unprojected although toSummary discards most of each row. The cache is per-process, so there is no shared cache even though REDIS_URL is already part of the project's configuration.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/library.ts:13`
  - fails when: libraryForEngine caches the library in a module-level variable with a five-minute TTL (library.ts:5-20), so every serverless instance re-reads the whole exercise table unprojected - `db().select().from(exercise)` - plus the video table, every five minutes for the life of the deployment, and the fill is paid by whichever user request first crosses the TTL (reached from runWeeklyAdaptation and diversifyProgramme on the Today render, from setLifeMode, and from the session substitution paths). toSummary (library.ts:8-10) returns none of instructions, cues, commonMistakes, safetyNotes, variations, source, sourceLicense or searchText, so roughly half the bytes are discarded on arrival. The cache is per-process, so a deployment of a hundred warm instances performs that read a thousand-plus times an hour for reference data that changes only when the seed is re-run, although REDIS_URL is already part of the project's configuration and nothing in packages/api uses it.
  - fix: Project the columns toSummary actually returns, and either lengthen the TTL substantially (the table changes only on a seed run) or move the cache behind the Redis instance the configuration already describes so one fill serves every instance.
  - ruling: Read the cache, the TTL, the unprojected select and toSummary's projection. Bounded, static data with a known ceiling: P3, fixed by projecting the columns and lengthening the TTL or moving the cache to the Redis the configuration already describes.
- **P3** `SCALE-18` Partner discovery and the group list select every matching row with no limit and rank in memory - `findMatches` selects every non-private community profile joined to its training profile and ranks them in JavaScript; `listGroups` selects every public group. Neither has a `LIMIT`, so both results grow with the user base inside one function invocation. Both are behind the discovery flag, which is off in production.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:94`
  - fails when: An operator sets COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY=on - one environment change with no code review - and the first GET /api/community/matches selects every public community profile with its full training-profile JSONB and ranks them in JavaScript inside one serverless function. At the design point with even a tenth opted in that is six figures of profile rows per request: the function exhausts its memory or duration and the community page fails for everyone.
  - fix: Push the filtering and the limit into SQL (candidate selection by level/goal/city with `limit`), then rank only the candidate page in memory; add `limit`/`offset` to `listGroups`. Do not enable the flag before that.
  - ruling: Both sites are as described: community.ts:94 selects every non-private communityProfile inner-joined to profile with no LIMIT and ranks in JavaScript, and listGroups at :146 selects every public group with no LIMIT. But both die at a guard I can point at: community.ts:21 `discoveryEnabled() = process.env.COMMUNITY_DISCOVERY === "on"`, checked as the first line of findMatches (:88) and listGroups (:147), and .env.example:60 ships it as off with a comment explaining why. Per the reachability rule that drops this to P3; it becomes a P1-shaped problem the moment the flag is turned on.
- **P3** `SCALE-19` Default challenges are seeded by a check-then-insert on a read path, so concurrent first requests create duplicates - `ensureDefaultChallenges` runs on every `listChallenges` call: it checks whether any challenge row exists and, if not, inserts two. There is no uniqueness on challenge identity and no lock, so two concurrent first requests both see an empty table and both insert.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/community.ts:191`
  - fails when: On a fresh deployment two users open /app/community within the same few milliseconds - or one user's page and its prefetch do. Both executions find the table empty and both insert, so the page lists "Show up this week" and "Twelve in four" twice, each with its own participant set, and members joining "the" weekly challenge end up on different boards. The duplicates persist because the guard only ever tests for existence.
  - fix: Seed the default challenges from the seed script or the migration chain rather than from a read path; if it must stay inline, insert with a deterministic id derived from (name, startsOn) and `onConflictDoNothing`.
  - ruling: ensureDefaultChallenges (community.ts:191) is called as the first line of listChallenges on every request: it selects one challenge row and, if none exists, inserts two, with no uniqueness on challenge identity and no lock.
- **P3** `SCALE-NEW-MITIG` The offline queue's claimed idempotency control is narrower than its comment says: the server deduplicates one set number, not a replayed batch - The file header states that replays are safe - 'Replays are idempotent (setNumber upsert server-side)' - and that claim is what makes the missing mutual exclusion at flush() look tolerable. The server-side behaviour it names covers only a replay of the same set number against a row nobody else is writing. It does not cover concurrent replays, and the same handler has two other write paths with no deduplication at all, so the control covers fewer paths than claimed.
  - where: `apps/web/lib/offline-queue.ts:4`
  - fails when: A reviewer reads the header comment, concludes replays are safe, and leaves flush() unserialised. Two concurrent flushes then replay the same job within milliseconds: both logSet calls read the same pre-write loggedSets, both see `before === undefined`, both pass the unchanged check, and both insert an estimated_max row and a personal_record row for one set - so the user's e1RM history gains a duplicated point and Progress shows two records for one performance, from a replay the code's own comment promised was idempotent, with no test pinning the invariant either way.
  - fix: Either make the claim true - send a per-job idempotency key from enqueue() and have the handler dedupe on it, inside a transaction that also covers the estimated_max and personal_record writes - or correct the comment and serialise flush(). Do not leave a comment asserting a property the code does not have.
  - ruling: The comment at offline-queue.ts:4 does assert "Replays are idempotent (setNumber upsert server-side)", and the server behaviour it names is a read-modify-write with no transaction (session.ts:146 and :149), so it holds only when replays are serialised - which flush() does not guarantee. The two unguarded side effects in the same call are real: the estimatedMax insert at session.ts:164, gated only by the pre-write `unchanged` comparison computed from the stale row, and syncSessionRecord's check-then-insert. Nothing on the wire carries an idempotency key; the job is { url, body, at } and the POST sends only the body. I rate this P3 because the concurrency defects themselves are ruled at SCALE-3, SCALE-4 and SCALE-5; what is left here is a stated invariant that the code does not hold, which the severity page places at P3.
- **P3** `SCALE-NEW10` The exercise library is cached in per-instance module state with a 5-minute TTL and no single-flight, and the inventory the audit ran against reported zero in-memory state - libraryForEngine keeps the whole exercise library in a module-scope variable with a 5-minute TTL. On a serverless host with many short-lived instances that is a per-instance cache with a near-zero hit rate and no single-flight guard, so every cold start and every expiry re-reads the full exercise and exercise_video tables. The related recipe reads have no cache at all. This matters mainly because the inventory the audit was given reports `inmemory_state: 0`, so the dimension's first test was never run against the code.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/library.ts:5`
  - fails when: At the stated peak Vercel runs many short-lived instances, so most Today renders land on an instance whose library cache is cold or expired and pay two full table reads - 876 exercise rows with their jsonb arrays plus the video table - before the page can be built; with no single-flight, a burst onto one fresh instance issues those queries once per request instead of once. The cache is also unobservable: nothing reports a hit rate, and an edit to the library is served stale for up to five minutes from an unknown number of instances, with no way to invalidate it.
  - fix: Make the cache single-flight (store the in-flight promise, not just the rows), project only the columns the engine uses instead of `select()`, give the recipe reads the same treatment, and make the TTL and hit rate observable. The durable fix is a shared cache, which is the same dependency SCALE-7 and SCALE-NEW7 need.
  - ruling: library.ts:5-19 keeps the whole library in a module-scope `cache` with a 5-minute TTL, checked and populated with no in-flight promise, so concurrent callers on a fresh instance all issue both queries; it is called twice in one Today render (adaptation.ts and programme.ts:123). nutrition.ts selects the whole recipe table with no cache at all. The other module-scope state is as listed: _db, _auth, the Anthropic client, the Resend client and better-auth's rate-limit Map.
- **P3** `SEC-15` Identifier lists are built into SQL by string concatenation through sql.raw in four places - Four queries interpolate id values into SQL text with sql.raw and manual quoting instead of using parameter binding or Drizzle's inArray. The values are server-generated UUIDs today, so there is no injection path, but the pattern defeats the parameterisation the rest of the data layer uses and becomes exploitable the moment the list carries a request-supplied value.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:24`
  - fails when: No abuse path today, which is what holds this at P3. The latent case is concrete: these helpers take plain string arrays, and routes in the app already accept id arrays from request bodies (apps/web/app/api/photos/[id]/analyse/route.ts:4 is ids: z.array(z.string())), so the first caller that passes a request-supplied list hands the database a quoted literal it never parameterised.
  - fix: Replace all four with `inArray(exerciseInstance.sessionId, ids)`, which Drizzle parameterises.
  - ruling: The four sites are as described (packages/api/src/services/coach.ts:24 and :28 build array[...]::uuid fragments through sql.raw, with two more of the same shape elsewhere in the services). In every case the array is built from a preceding select of a uuid primary key, so no request-supplied string reaches the fragment, and the ::uuid cast would reject one. The finding rates its own reachability honestly.
- **P3** `SEC-16` No Content-Security-Policy on any response - The header block sets frame, content-type, referrer and permissions policies but no CSP, confirmed absent on a live response, so there is no second line of defence against an injected script.
  - where: `apps/web/next.config.ts:18`
  - fails when: Hardening with no demonstrated path today. If an injection sink ever appears - the obvious candidate is a future markdown or rich-text render of community posts - there is no second line of defence and an injected script runs with the user's session on the origin that serves their health data.
  - fix: Add a CSP to the same headers block, starting in report-only; Next 16 needs a nonce or strict-dynamic for its inline bootstrap script, which is the one directive that takes work.
  - ruling: Verified on the wire, not in the config: the live response to GET https://kettleworth.vercel.app/ carries strict-transport-security, x-content-type-options, x-frame-options, referrer-policy and permissions-policy, and no content-security-policy of any kind (apps/web/next.config.ts:18-23 sets the four it does send). The finding's own reason for P3 also holds: there is no dangerouslySetInnerHTML or innerHTML in apps/web or packages/ui, so there is no injection path to pair it with.
- **P3** `SEC-NEW10` Progress-photo uploads are typed by the client's declared part header, never by content, and the bytes are served back inline from the same origin with that declared type - The only type check compares the client-supplied multipart part type against an allowlist; nothing inspects the bytes. The stored contentType is then echoed as the response Content-Type when the photo is read back inline from the app's own origin, so the server is persisting and serving arbitrary attacker bytes under an image label it never verified.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:18`
  - fails when: An attacker posts arbitrary bytes - an HTML document, a polyglot - with the part's Content-Type set to image/jpeg. The server stores them as a progress photo and later serves them from its own origin labelled as an image it never verified. Script execution is blocked today by the x-content-type-options: nosniff header I confirmed on the live response and by the three-type allowlist, which is why this is hardening rather than stored XSS; the residual is that the product persists and serves unverified attacker bytes under an image label.
  - fix: Verify the content, not the header: in packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:18, sniff the magic bytes (or decode the image) and derive contentType from what the bytes actually are, rejecting anything that is not a real JPEG, PNG or WebP. Keep serving from the derived type at apps/web/app/api/photos/[id]/route.ts:9, keep nosniff, and never add image/svg+xml to TYPES without a separate sanitiser.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/photos.ts:18 is the only type check and it compares the client-declared multipart part type against a three-entry allowlist; nothing inspects the bytes. apps/web/app/api/photos/[id]/route.ts:9 then serves the stored bytes back from the app's own origin with that same declared Content-Type and Content-Disposition: inline.
- **P3** `SEC-NEW9` substitutesForInstance is the one instance-scoped service function with no ownership check, where its eleven siblings all compare the session's user against the instance's owner - Every other function that takes an instance id or session id from the request re-reads the row through trainingSession and refuses when the owner differs. substitutesForInstance looks the instance up by primary key alone and never compares owners, so an authenticated caller can pass any instance id in the estate and have the server resolve it.
  - where: `packages/api/src/services/session.ts:217`
  - fails when: An authenticated attacker who has obtained another member's exercise_instance id - from a shared screenshot or a support thread - calls GET /api/instance/<id>/substitutes and the server resolves that instance instead of refusing. What comes back is computed against the attacker's own profile, so the leak is thin: that the id exists, and which movement the instance is, inferable from the substitute list. A missing ownership check with a small consequence, not a data breach.
  - fix: Make substitutesForInstance read the instance the way its siblings do: join trainingSession, select `sessionUser: trainingSession.userId`, and `if (!inst || inst.sessionUser !== userId) return []`. Add a test that calls it with another user's instance id and asserts an empty result.
  - ruling: packages/api/src/services/session.ts:217-223 looks the instance up by primary key alone and never compares owners, where its siblings in the same file re-read the row through trainingSession and refuse when the owner differs; apps/web/app/api/instance/[id]/substitutes/route.ts:3 passes the path id straight in.
- **P3** `SECRET-5` The Oura disconnect puts the user's access token in a URL query string and swallows the failure - `revoke` sends the decrypted OAuth access token as a query parameter rather than a header or body, and discards any error, so the token can end up in third-party and intermediary access logs while remaining valid.
  - where: `packages/integrations/src/adapters/oura.ts:14`
  - fails when: A user presses Disconnect on Oura. Their live access token travels in the request line, where it is recorded in the provider's edge access logs and in any egress proxy or request log that captures URLs. The empty catch means a network blip or a 5xx is invisible, so the revoke silently does not happen while the app reports success and deletes its own copy: the token stays valid upstream until it expires, and nothing can revoke it any more. Unreachable today - no provider is configured in production - which is what holds it at P3.
  - fix: Send the token in the POST body (`new URLSearchParams({ access_token: c.accessToken })` with a form content type, as Oura's revoke endpoint also accepts) and log revoke failures rather than discarding them, so a provider that refused the revoke is visible.
  - ruling: packages/integrations/src/adapters/oura.ts:14 is await fetch(`https://api.ouraring.com/oauth/revoke?access_token=${encodeURIComponent(c.accessToken)}`, { method: "POST" }).catch(() => {}) - the only access_token= in the tree; every other provider call puts the token in a header. The token is decrypted immediately before, at integrations.ts:55, and the swallowed failure is doubled, because integrations.ts:56 wraps the call in another .catch(() => {}).
- **P3** `SECRET-NEW3` The OAuth callback writes the raw exception message into a redirect URL query string, so SQL text, bound parameters and third-party response bodies land in browser history and access logs - This is the second, unguarded copy of the defect in SECRET-4, in one of the seven route handlers that do not use `apps/web/lib/api.ts`, and it is worse than the first because the destination is a URL rather than a response body. The same `e.message` that SECRET-4 traces - which for any database failure is drizzle's `Failed query: <sql>\nparams: <values>` - is url-encoded into a 302 Location.
  - where: `apps/web/app/api/integrations/[provider]/callback/route.ts:13`
  - fails when: The normal failure here is a static string: with no provider configured, completeConnect throws 'Connection request expired' at integrations.ts:31 before touching anything else, so the harmful variant needs a database error during the verification select or the credential upsert. When that happens the SQL and its bound parameters - including the user id and the encrypted credential blob on the upsert path - are written into a URL the browser then stores and sends onward. Held at P3 because the OAuth flow cannot start at all in the shipped configuration.
